
===== [p91-92] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.2 Thermal Diffusion in Laser Heat-Mode Lithography (11块) =====
--[p92]-- In the perpendicular direction, the thermal diffusion is perpendicular to the heat-mode resist thin film, and called out-of-plane thermal diffusion channel. The capability of thermal diffusion can be marked as $\mathrm { \Delta D _ { \perp } }$ . The out-of-plane thermal diffusion channel can be divided into two parts: one is upward and the other is downward.
--[p91]-- The DOF is estimated by $\mathrm { D O F } = k \lambda / N A ^ { 2 }$ , where k is processing factor, k is laser wavelength, and NA is numerical aperture of converging lens. The DOF is about 600 nm for the laser heat-mode lithography system with k of 405 nm and NA of 0.85.
--[p92]-- Besides the phase-change threshold effect, the feature size is also determined by other factors, such as heat-mode resist material itself, thin-film thickness, and writing speed. In fact, the essence of these factors is thermal diffusion. Thermal diffusion can take place as the temperature of the heated region is higher than that of its surroundings. Thermal diffusion generates primarily along horizontal and perpendicular directions, as shown in Fig. 4.1b.
--[p92]-- The capability of thermal diffusion can be marked as $\mathrm { D } _ { \parallel }$ The in-plane thermal diffusion channel will cause the temperature profile to become flat compared with the spot intensity profile and enlarge the heated region size, and generate a large thermal diffusion influence region, whose size can be roughly estimated as $\sqrt { \mathbf { D } _ { \parallel } t } .$ , where t is thermal diffusion time. As shown in Fig.
--[p92]-- Besides the in-plane thermal diffusion channel, the thermal diffusion time is also one of the factors influencing the phase-change region size, because a long thermal diffusion time will increase the thermal diffusion distance.
Therefore, to obtain a small feature size of pattern structures through laser heat-mode lithography, one needs to give effective methods for manipulating in-plane thermal diffusion channels and thermal diffusion time.
--[p91]-- 4.2 Thermal Diffusion in Laser Heat-Mode Lithography
In laser heat-mode lithography, a collimated laser beam is focused on the heat-mode resist thin film material. Generally speaking, the intensity of laser spot presents a typical Gaussian profile, as shown in Fig. 4.1a, where the heat-mode resist thin film absorbs the laser energy and is heated. In the range of depth of focus (DOF) of the laser spot, the laser spot volume can be considered as cylindric volume.
--[p92]-- The phase-change region can be further etched into pattern structures in acid or alkaline solution.
In laser heat-mode lithography, the critical advantage is that the feature size of obtained pattern structures is smaller than the laser spot, and can be reduced down to sub-micrometer or even nanometer scale. However, in practical operation, there are several factors influencing the feature size.
--[p91]-- The most heat-mode resists have the absorption coefficient of about $1 0 ^ { 7 } / \mathrm { m }$ and the penetration depth (L) of laser spot into the heat-mode resist is about 100 nm by $L = 1 / \alpha ,$ where a is absorption coefficient. Thus, an approximately cylindrical heated region is formed within the heat-mode resist thin film, and the cylindrical heated volume in the heat-mode resist is valid for the resist thicknesses of about 100 nm.
--[p92]-- Whether in the heated region or in phase-change region, the out-of-plane thermal diffusion channel will not change the temperature profile; thus, this is helpful for obtaining sub-micrometer or even nanoscale pattern structures.
In the horizontal direction, the thermal diffusion is perpendicular to the side face of the heated volume and outward into the unheated region, called the in-plane thermal diffusion channel.
--[p92]-- The temperature profile of the heated region is similar to the laser spot intensity. For heat-mode resist thin film, there is an obvious thermally induced phase-change threshold effect. The phase change takes place only when the temperature is above the threshold temperature. Thus, a below-laser-spot phase-change region is generated at the center of the laser spot, owing to the Gaussian profile of spot intensity.
--[p92]-- 4.1c, in parallel, the size of phase-change region in the center of spot also becomes large, accordingly. This will produce a thermal diffusion-induced phase-change region. That is, the thermal diffusion-induced phase-change region size is also enlarged. The increase of phase-change region size means that the feature size of pattern structures will become large. Thus, the in-plane thermal diffusion channel is disadvantageous for obtaining sub-micrometer or nanometer pattern structures.

--[p201]-- The increment of $\mathrm { S F } _ { 6 }$ flow rate can enhance the etching of Si, however, further increasing flow rate shortens the gas residence time on the surface of Si and the reaction probability of free fluorine with Si weakens. Therefore, the etching rate of Si decreases when ${ \mathrm { S F } } _ { 6 }$ flow rate is over 40 sccm.
--[p202]-- 150 W, while the etching rate of GSBT enhances steadily. The maximum etching selectivity exceeds 200:1 when the etching power is fixed at 50 W. On one hand, the reaction of fluorine atoms with Si becomes stronger with etching power increasing. However, the fluorine atoms reacted with Si is limited by the ${ \mathrm { S F } } _ { 6 }$ flow rate, thus, the etching rate of Si tends to saturate at high power.
--[p202]-- On the other hand, the etching of GSBT is a physical bombardment process, which can be improved by increasing etching power owing to the higher mean kinetic energy of the plasma.
Figure 9.6c reveals that, under the gas flow rate of 40 sccm and etching power of 50 W, the etching rates of GSBT and Si both increase first and then decrease with the increase of etching pressure. The etching selectivity of Si to GSBT is as high as 524:1 at 14 Pa.
--[p201]-- However, $\mathrm { S F } _ { 6 }$ does not react with GSBT, the GSBT etching is dominated by physical collision, which has far lower efficiency than the Si etching, and the etching rate of GSBT is quite low.
Figure 9.6b indicates that, under the gas flow of 40 sccm and pressure of 10 Pa, the etching rate of Si increases sharply with increasing the etching power from 20 to 50 W, but becomes gradually saturated with further increasing etching power to

--[p200]-- 9.2.1.3 Transferring the Patterns on $\mathbf { G e } _ { 2 } \mathbf { S b } _ { 1 . 5 } \mathbf { B i } _ { 0 . 5 } \mathbf { T e } _ { 5 }$ to Si Substrates

--[p202]-- The high etching selectivity is attributed to the competition of two effects from etching pressure. One is that the high etching pressure shortens the gas mean free path and lowers the energy of fluorine radicals or particles participating in the physical bombardment. The other is that the increasing pressure enhances the ion bombardment, favoring the etching.

===== [p93-95] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.3.1 Thermal Diffusion Coefficient (13块) =====
--[p95]-- In the laser-heating process, laser power can affect the temperature distribution of heat-mode lithography material, the temperature profiles under different laser power are simulated. Here, the AZ5214E heat-mode resist is taken as an example. Figure 4.2c is the simulated result. It is found that the peak temperature increases with increasing laser power. When the laser power is lower than 5.5 mW, the peak temperature cannot reach the threshold temperature (110 °C) of AZ5214E heat-mode resist.
--[p95]-- This can be attributed to the low thermal diffusion coefficient that leads to small thermal diffusion even if the laser power increases. Thus, the laser power has no influence on the normalized temperature profile.
Because of the low thermal diffusion coefficient, one successfully fabricated convex-type patterns with nanoscale feature size on $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ thin film as shown in Fig. 4.3 [5].
--[p93 [表]]-- The AZ5214E heat-mode resist has the lowest thermal diffusion coefficient, thus the heat-spot size is the smallest among the heat-mode resists.

--[p168]-- The upper part of the top ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ layer is influenced by the air space, and has worse heat dissipation compared with the lower part. --[p118]-- 5.2.2 Ge–Sb–Te Resists

The Ge–Sb–Te generally experiences a thermally induced structural change from amorphous state to crystalline state after the laser pulse heating [13].
Actually, the Ge–Sb–Te is also a kind of laser heat-mode resist.

--[p191]-- When the laser energy increases beyond the crystallization threshold of Te, the crystallization of Te occurs, as shown in Fig. 8.13b, where the film appears light-dark due to many black particles emerging and dispersing in the matrix. --[p190]-- The as-deposited $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ thin film is amorphous, and the crystalline Te occurs when samples are irradiated by laser energy. When the laser energy is higher than 4200 $\mathrm { m J } / \mathrm { c m } ^ { 2 }$ the diffraction peaks of Te crystals become weaker.
--[p190]-- 8.4.1 Structural Evolution
When the laser beam spot with a Gaussian intensity profile irradiates onto the $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ thin films, the $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ thin films absorb the laser energy and are heated, and the temperature rise occurs. The structural evolution of TeO happens when the temperature rise exceeds to certain threshold. Figure 8.12a shows XRD patterns of $\mathrm { T e O } _ { \mathit { s } }$ thin film by different laser energy irradiations.
--[p192]-- This ablation happens when the laser energy is beyond the ablation threshold, as shown in Fig. 8.13d, where dark-gray particles disperse in the white background. The SAED pattern shows diffuse halo with some small bright dots dispersed in the dark background, indicating that this area consists mainly of small and randomly oriented crystallites. When the laser energy is much larger than ablation threshold, the randomly oriented crystallites reduce or even disappear.
Figure 8.13e presents the corresponding TEM analysis, where SAED pattern is a single diffuse halo.
--[p191]-- Due to the immiscibility of Te and $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ , the Te is segregated from $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ , and driven to the free upper surface because of the thermal gradient effect during laser irradiation, and finally, a Te-rich layer is formed on the surface after the laser pulse is off. The crystallization of Te-rich layer results in a large optical reflectivity. Figure 8.13c presents the TEM picture. Compared with Fig.

Figure 8.13a is the TEM pictures of the as-deposited state. In as-deposited state, the area appears to be homogeneous and fine, and consists of many granular structures.
--[p191]-- 8.13b, the black particles get much large and agglomerates into bulks. The SAED pattern indicates polycrystalline state with relatively large crystallites of different orientations.
The ablation zone and the substrate appearing as the opposite grayscale of dark and bright grayscales are actually the same zone but with different extents of ablation, thereby forming the dark ring surrounding a light-gray center.
--[p191]-- The corresponding SAED pattern shows only a weak diffuse halo and reveals the amorphous state, which agrees well with the XRD result mentioned above that the as-deposited $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ film is amorphous granular structure. When the laser energy is very low, the tiny Te grains agglomerate to the air–film interface, which leads to the growth of Te grain size. The extent of agglomeration depends much on the temperature gradient during laser irradiation, and mainly agglomerates.
--[p191]-- The crystallization is from two aspects: (1) the direct transformation from amorphous Te to crystalline Te at lower laser energy, (2) the recrystallization of the molten Te after quenching to the room temperature. As the laser energy increases, the amorphous $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ is also crystallized and transforms into $\mathrm { \beta } \mathrm { - } \mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ when the temperature is about $3 6 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ .
--[p190]-- XPS analysis of $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ thin films is performed to investigate the chemical bonding changes due to the laser irradiation. Figure 8.12b shows the XPS of Te 3d states at different laser energy irradiation. For the as-deposited sample, the Te $3 \mathrm { d } _ { 5 / 2 }$ for Te–Te bonding and Te–O bonding is located at 571.08 eV and $5 7 4 .

===== [p4-7] Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > © Springer Nature Singapore Pte Ltd. 2019 (18块) =====
--[p4]-- All rights are reserved by the Publisher, whether the whole or part of the material is concerned, specifically the rights of translation, reprinting, reuse of illustrations, recitation, broadcasting, reproduction on microfilms or in any other physical way, and transmission or information storage and retrieval, electronic adaptation, computer software, or by similar or dissimilar methodology now known or hereafter developed.
--[p5]-- In order to obtain small feature size, one needs to reduce the laser wavelength of lithography system, which is required to develop new organic photoresists corresponding to every new laser wavelength, accordingly, due to each photoresist being only sensitive to specific wavelengths. This often needs a very long research/development period and a high investment.
Laser heat-mode lithography uses a laser spot to irradiate onto the resist thin films.
--[p6]-- That is, the resist thin films are not sensitive to laser wavelength, thus different from light-mode exposure, the writing process does not need any darkroom or yellow light environment. The advantages can be summarized as follows:
1. The laser heat-mode lithography usually uses a semiconductor diode as the light source, the laser wavelength is in the range of visible light, which does not need any vacuum environment. Thus, the lithography cost is very low.
2.

Shanghai, China
Jingsong Wei
--[p5]-- Direct laser writing lithography based on light-mode exposure is a good method to fabricate arbitrary structures for applications in microelectronics, integrated optics, and diffractive optics, etc. Compared with other lithography methods, its operation is facile, and conducted in air. Moreover, its cost is relatively low.

--[p6]-- In laser heat-mode lithography, the resist thin films are essentially opto-thermal response mode to the laser spot with broad light spectrum features, thus, the laser wavelength can be in the broad range of light spectrum. The laser heat-mode lithography greatly simplifies production procedures because they need neither a particular light source nor a particular environment, there are no pre-baking and post-baking steps required for inorganic resists.
--[p5]-- Unfortunately, the feature size is dominated by the laser spot size, and it is difficult to surpass the diffraction limit determined by 1.22k/NA, where k is the laser wavelength and NA is the numerical aperture of the converging lens.
--[p5]-- The light energy is absorbed and transferred into thermal energy. The thermally induced structural transformation takes place when the resist thin film is heated to certain phase-change threshold temperature such as crystallization point. The phase-change region is further etched in acid/alkali solution due to the etching selectivity between laser irradiated and non-irradiated regions.
--[p6]-- The pattern feature size can be either larger or far smaller than laser spot by tuning the writing strategy. That is, the laser heat-mode lithography can break through the diffraction limit and realize super-resolution nanolithography and trans-scale lithography. One can also arbitrarily tune the lithographic feature size from nanoscale to tens of micrometers without changing the laser spot size.
4. The laser heat-mode resists thin films are usually chalcogenide phase-change materials.

However, progresses toward higher resolution and individual customer demands have proven to be increasingly difficult as feature size decreases.
Various patterning and lithographic methods have been proposed.

--[p6]-- The etching process is a broken process of bonds among atoms. Thus, the edge of lithographic patterns is more smooth and clear than the small molecular photoresist materials. That is, the line edge roughness can be controlled at a very low value.
This book provides a systematical description and analysis of laser heat-mode lithography, including principle, lithography system, and manipulation on feature size, resist thin films, and some typical experimental results and applications.
--[p5]-- Probe-based lithography is able to fabricate nanoscale arbitrary patterns in atmospheric environment, but the lithography remains of low speed and fits only for small area. Template-based lithography (such as nanoimprint, projection lithography, and self-assembly) is good method for mass production, while it is not good choice to meet individual demands due to some difficulties in fabricating large-area stampers with nanoscale feature size.
--[p5]-- Among these methods, vacuum-based high-energy beam (such as X-ray and ion/electron beam) lithography has been extensively utilized to fabricate small area micro/nanostructures because of their high resolution, however, the high-vacuum environment is required in the process of micro/nanofabrication, and this technique also suffers from low speed, high cost, and low throughput.
--[p6]-- The laser beam is focused on a far-field range, and the distance between sample and writing head is far larger than light wavelength, thus the writing operation is conducted in far-field and atmospheric environments, the operation is easy and writing speed is very fast, accordingly.
3. The lithographic feature size is determined by some main factors including laser spot, thermal phase-change threshold, thermal diffusion, and nonlinear responses.

===== [p71-72] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.3.4.3 Servo-Tracking Control Unit (6块) =====
--[p71]-- Writing strategy block can provide modulation signal for LD driver, which is based on the (r, h) movement speed. Position interface block can receive the signal from (r, h) movement stage. In addition, it needs to be explained that all the operations, such as S-curve searching, focus-seeking, writing test, laser power tuning, and response frequency test, can be conducted through sequential program in 8051 Micro-controller.
The servo-tracking flow is as follows:
--[p72]-- During the laser heat-mode exposure, the temperature of the laser diode may change due to prolonged cyclic operation, which will have an ill-influence on the pattern structures. Therefore, the output power of blue laser diode needs to be accurately controlled. An alternative way to use front monitor diode circuit to monitor the output power of the laser diode and transform the output power to a voltage signal.
--[p72]-- One sends the voltage signal to the closed-loop circuit and compares with the given voltage signal, and obtains a difference value, which is used as a feedback signal to compensate the variance of the laser power and increases the output power of blue laser diode reliability.
--[p71]-- 3.3.4.3 Servo-Tracking Control Unit
In the servo-tracking module, the control unit is also critical to improve the focusing precision and speed. Figure 3.12 is a block diagram and signal flowchart [3]. The whole unit flow is controlled by 8051 Micro-controller. FPGA block is divided into servo-controlling, writing strategy, and position interface. Servo-controlling block allows to control the sequential logic of AKM analog signal processor.
--[p72]-- (1) Using S-curve search function to find the proper working distance for the laser heat-mode lithography system.
(2) Going back to the main program and determining the focusing function while the (r, h) stage keeps moving.
(3) Analyzing FES through AKM processor and sending to DSP through servo-controlling block.
(4) Calculating the compensated digital signal through DSP and sending to the actuator, which makes whole servo-tracking module become a closed-loop system.
--[p72]-- (5) Keeping the servo-tracking module in focusing status until receiving the trigger signal from $( r , \theta )$ movement stage.
The trigger signal is sent to FPGA writing strategy block when the $( r , \ \theta )$ movement stage reaches the predetermined position, then the writing strategy block sends the modulated electric signal to LD driver and the laser heat-mode exposure works.

===== [p177-178] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.2.1 Micro/Nanopatterning Through Marangoni Effect [1] (16块) =====
--[p177]-- 8.2.1 Micro/Nanopatterning Through Marangoni Effect [1]
Chalcogenide phase-change thin films are good laser patterning materials due to Marangoni and volume expansion effects during the laser pulse irradiation. Marangoni effect is the phenomenon of fluid flow caused by surface tension gradients, and consists of the thermocapillary effect and chemicapillary effect.
--[p177]-- In addition, the linear thermal expansion coefficient of liquid is about ten times that of the solid, thus the closed system of the molten pool will expand due to the large linear thermal expansion coefficient of melt compared with that of solid. This may be another mechanism for micro/nanostructure formation in the chalcogenide phase-change thin films.
--[p177]-- Subsequently, the heat starts to transfer to the inner part of the film, and the material melts to form a molten pool. Generally, the surface tension decreases with the temperature of the molten material. Thus, the central molten material has a surface tension higher than that on the outer part. In this case, the molten material at the melt/air interface quickly moves from the center to the periphery.
--[p177]-- The thermocapillary effect results from the thermal potential of temperature gradient caused by laser beam with Gaussian intensity distribution, and the chemicapillary effect is generated by the chemical potential caused by compositional gradient from the temperature-activated vaporization and diffusion of solute specials.
--[p177]-- The thermocapillary effect usually pushes the molten materials toward the cold peripheral region with higher surface tension, whereas the chemicapillary effect often pushes the liquid material toward the regions with higher surface energy and lower concentration. The thermocapillary and chemicapillary effects often coexist during the whole process, and the competition between them determines the molten flow and leads to the different patterns in different laser energy conditions.
--[p177]-- The strong convection flow generates large pressure on the film surface, which is the main reason of microbump formation. The direction of the convection flow is shown in Fig. 8.1a, where the molten pool is formed in the center of laser spot. The molten pool is actually a closed system due to the C thin film. The convection flow is almost symmetric, which is in correspondence to the annular distribution of laser energy.
--[p177]-- Here AgInSbTe (AIST) thin film is taken as an example, AIST is one of the typical chalcogenide materials. The AIST thin film is deposited onto the glass substrate, and a carbon (C) thin film with a thickness of 5 nm is deposited onto the AIST layer to avoid the AIST being oxidized during laser irradiation. According to the Marangoni effect, when a laser beam irradiates the film, a small volume of AIST is rapidly heated to above the melting point.
--[p177]-- According to the Marangoni effect, when the laser pulse spot irradiates onto the chalcogenide phase-change thin films, the surface of chalcogenide phase-change thin films is heated to melting temperature, and the central temperature of laser spot is high and decreases along the radial direction, that is, there is radial temperature gradient in the laser spot. At central part of the laser spot, the chalcogenide phase-change material becomes molten and flows, and the surface tension occurs.
--[p178]-- 8.1c are magnified in the inset, where the thin-film volume expansion occurs under laser irradiation, and the carbon layer does not melt during the laser pulse irradiation, that is, the AIST is protected from vaporization and almost completely enclosed by the carbon layer. A hole at the apex of the structure (\*75 nm in diameter) in the inset is formed due to too high laser energy. To observe the interior structure, FIB is used to mill the structures.
--[p178]-- After removing the laser beam, the solidification of the molten material begins at the periphery because of the strong temperature gradient along radial direction. The solidification rapidly proceeds to the center of the molten pool. The molten material also moves closer to the center under the effect of the surface tension at the solid/ liquid interface. This movement is toward the liquid, and forms the column structure in the center of the molten pool.
--[p178]-- When the laser energy causes the temperature of the material to exceed the boiling point, the vaporized gas fills the upper part of the micro/nanostructures, and solidifies as a heavy block at the top of the micro/nanostructures.
--[p177]-- The surface tension of the molten flow depends not only on the radial temperature gradient, but also on the radial concentration gradient. The radial temperature gradient corresponds to the thermocapillary effect due to the different thermal potential, and the radial concentration gradient corresponds to the chemicapillary effect due to the different chemical potentials in the molten pool.
--[p177]-- This creates strong radial temperature gradients at the liquid surface. These gradients produce radial surface tension gradients, which in turn generates radial convective liquid flows. The C film has a higher melting point than that of AIST. At the beginning of the process, the surface AIST material is heated to melt. The highest temperature is produced at the melt/air interface.
--[p178]-- Moreover, the atoms of the molten material have no time to rearrange because of the rapid solidification, which causes the structural change, and shrinkage cavities and pores to form inside the micro/ nanostructures (Fig. 8.1b). Figure 8.1c is the micro/nanostructures formed onto the AIST thin films, where the diameter of the structures is 200–450 nm. Three structures encircled in Fig.
--[p177]-- Generally speaking, the two effects coexist during the laser pulse irradiation process, and the competition between them determines the material flow and leads to the different pattern structures.
--[p178]-- A Pt layer with nanoscale thickness is deposited before the milling. Figure 8.1d presents the interior characteristics of the structures. One can see the “AIST layer/C layer/Pt layer”. The interior of the structure is hollow, and the material solidifies. Some shrinkage cavities and pores generate inside the structure. That is, the micro/nanostructure is hollow besides several solid leavings, which may from vaporization effect of molten materials in the closed system.

===== [p18-19] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (4) Reverse Nanoimprint (3块) =====
--[p18]-- 1.6b ) if the polymer is UV curable, such as in the case of negative-tone light-mode resists or transparent molds. The UV curing can also be performed from the substrate side if the substrate is transparent and the mold is opaque. A pressure is needed to achieve intimate contact between the polymer and substrate. After demolding, the polymer structures are transferred onto the substrate (Fig. 1.6c), which is equivalent to that obtained by conventional imprinting.
--[p18]-- (4) Reverse Nanoimprint
The reverse nanoimprint process is schematically shown in Fig. 1.6 [3], whereby the polymer is spin-coated onto a structured stamp to fill up all the stamp cavities (Fig. 1.6a). Soft baking is carried out to evaporate the solvent in the polymer. The molded polymer structures can be transferred to a flat substrate either through thermal press (Fig. 1.6b ) if the polymer is polystyrene (PS) or polymethylmethacrylate (PMMA), or through UV curing (Fig.
--[p19]-- Nanoimprint lithography can be used to fabricate high-resolution nanostructures with low cost, high efficiency, and in large volumes. However, nanoimprint stamp can be polluted readily owing to the direct contact between the resist layer and the mold. Additionally, the high-resolution stamp is generally fabricated by electron-beam lithography. Thus, it is difficult to fabricate a stamp with a large area.
Large size stamps are also very expensive if the feature size of the structures is at the nanoscale.

===== [p49-49] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.3.4 Broadband Lithography (6块) =====
--[p49]-- Compared with spin-coated organic light-mode resist thin films, the spin-coated inorganic heat-mode resist thin films provide the advantages as follows: good contrast, sensitivity as well as lower surface roughness and line edge roughness. Compared with vacuum-based physical vapor deposition thin films, the coatable sol–gel inorganic resist thin films are low cost, and can be coated on a large-sized substrate. Without high-vacuum systems are required.
--[p49]-- Additionally, compared with sputtering film-formation methods, no crystalline phases and textures of microscopic crystals occur in the spin-coated inorganic heat-mode resist thin films. Using the $\mathrm { T i O } _ { 2 }$ sol–gel inorganic resist thin films, broadband optical lithography is obtained, as shown in Fig. 2.16. Figure 2.16a is the clear and yellowish sol–gel inorganic resist solution. The inorganic resist sol–gel solution is spin-coated on the substrate.
--[p49]-- The pattern structures are written through different wavelength laser beam, such as line structures obtained at 413 and 351 nm laser wavelengths (Fig. 2.16b, d), the dot structures obtained at 257 nm laser wavelength (Fig. 2.16c).
--[p49]-- The laser wavelength shorter than 825 nm can excite band to band transitions [19], that is, the heat-mode resist can absorb photonic energy when the laser wavelength is below 825 nm. Thus, for most Te-based phase-change materials, the heat-mode exposure may happen only if the laser wavelength is shorter than 825 nm, as shown in Fig.
--[p49]-- 2.3.4 Broadband Lithography
The laser heat-mode lithography is essentially a photothermal effect. The laser wavelength is ranged from near-infrared to extreme ultraviolet light due to wide light absorption spectra of the heat-mode resists. For example, if Te-based phase-change materials are regarded as heat-mode resists, the bandgap is well below 1.5 eV, which corresponds to 825 nm light wavelength.
--[p49]-- 2.15, where one uses the 635 nm wavelength laser as heat-mode exposure light source, the pattern structures are successfully obtained on the Te-based chalcogenide thin films.
The inorganic heat-mode resist thin films are generally prepared through vacuum-based physical vapor deposition method. Actually, they can be also deposited on the substrates through spin-coating method, where the coatable sol– gel inorganic heat-mode resists are used.

--[p154]-- It is found that the binding energy of O 1s region is located at 530.83 and 531.58 eV in the as-deposited ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ film. --[p154]-- However, the binding energy of Si 2p region in $\mathrm { S i O _ { 4 } }$ tetrahedral network is 103–103.5 eV [9, 10]. Moreover, the Si $2 \mathrm { p }$ binding energy decreases with the reduction of oxygen content. Therefore, in as-deposited ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ film, the $\mathrm { S i O _ { 4 } }$ tetrahedral network does not exist.
--[p152]-- 7.4 shows the XRD patterns of ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin films at as-deposited and laser-irradiated states, respectively, where the laser power density is fixed at $1 . 4 \times 1 0 ^ { 6 } \mathrm { W / c m } ^ { 2 }$ and duration time is 500 ns. One can see that the ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin films present an amorphous state before and after laser irradiations.
--[p154]-- It is considered that Si exists as ${ \mathrm { S i S } } _ { n } { \mathrm { O } } _ { 4 - n }$ tetrahedrons [11]. Partial S element is used to substitute O element in $\mathrm { S i O _ { 4 } }$ tetrahedral network. Thus, oxygen content decreases in ${ \mathrm { S i S } } _ { n } { \mathrm { O } } _ { 4 - n }$ tetrahedral network and the Si $2 \mathrm { p }$ binding energy is lower than 103–103.5 eV.
--[p154]-- 3 4 ~ \mathrm { e V }$ and 101.61 eV, respectively. It is suggested that more S element is used to replace oxygen in ${ \mathrm { S i S } } _ { n } { \mathrm { O } } _ { 4 - n }$ tetrahedrons. After etching for 5 s in hydrofluoric acid, the Si signals of the laser-irradiated sample become weaker than that of as-deposited region.
--[p154]-- Moreover, Zn–O bonds are also damaged by hydrofluoric acid. The residual oxygen element may exist as highly chemically disordered ${ \mathrm { Z n S 3 . O } }$ crystallites [11]. The solubility of ZnS:O crystalline is lower due to the formation of $Z \mathrm { n } { - } S$ bonds.
--[p154]-- In addition, the addition of S into ${ \mathrm { S i S } } _ { n } { \mathrm { O } } _ { 4 - n }$ tetrahedral network results in the formation of Si–S bonds in which the binding energy is located at 101.96 eV [12]. When the ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film is irradiated at $1 . 4 \times 1 0 ^ { 6 } \mathrm { \ W / c m ^ { 2 } } .$ , the Si $2 \mathrm { p }$ peak is shifted to low energy direction located at $1 0 2 .

===== [p106-107] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.5.1 High-Speed Writing (9块) =====
--[p106]-- 4.5.1 High-Speed Writing
High-speed writing, which is realized by high-speed sample movement, can not only improve throughput, but also reduce the size of heat-spot. For instance, columnar patterns with the minimum diameter of 120 nm were achieved in ZnS– $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 } – Z \mathrm { n }$ thin film and the writing speed was 4.5 m/s [14]. The line-grating patterns with 46 nm linewidth were obtained on AIST thin films and the patterning speed was as high as 8 m/s [3].
--[p107]-- The minimum full width at half maximum (FWHM), called as normal width, is about $4 6 \pm 5$ nm, which is far smaller than the writing spot (1.22 $\lambda / N A = 5 5 0 ~ \mathrm { n m } )$ . The actual spot size is about 600 nm for the laser writing system. Therefore, the achieved minimum size is about 1/12 of the laser spot at the writing speed of 8 m/s.
One can see from Fig.
--[p106]-- The peak temperature increases with the increment of laser power, and becomes higher than the threshold temperature. The micro/ nanopatterns can be obtained on the AIST thin film when the laser power exceeds 1.0 mW. Figure 4.14d presents the normalized temperature profiles. It is found that the normalized temperature profiles are independent of the laser power, and the heat-spot size is 428 nm.
--[p106]-- This is because the heating time decreases when the writing speed increases. Figure 4.14b shows the normalized temperature profiles, the inset is the relationship between writing speed and heat-spot size. It is found that the heat-spot size can be reduced from 455 to 395 nm when the writing speed increases from 1 to 35 m/s, where the speed of 35 m/s may be obtained through using advanced Bly-ray disk recording players. The reduction amplitude can reach 13.2%.
--[p106]-- The reduction of heat-spot size is due to the decrease in heating time, which leads to short thermal diffusion distance. In addition, high-speed writing can take away the heat by promoting air flow and improve the out-of-plane thermal diffusion channel. Moreover, the higher the writing speed, the more the heat taken away by air flow. Thus, high-speed writing is an efficient pathway to obtain small heat-spot size.
--[p107]-- One experimentally fabricated grating structures on AIST thin films at the writing speed of 8 m/s and $2 0 0 ~ { \mu \mathrm { m } } / { \mathrm { s } }$ , respectively [3, 15]. Figures 4.15 and 4.16 show the corresponding experimental results. One can see from Fig. 4.15 that the depth of the line patterns is about 37 nm, and the full width at the grating surface is about 100 nm.
--[p107]-- 4.16a–c that, under the writing speed of 200 lm/s and pulse width of 70 ns, the linewidth of grating structures are 430 nm, 570 nm, and 1.95 lm corresponding to the laser power of 2.6 mW, 3.0 mW, and 9.0 mW, respectively. The linewidth of the grating structures decreases with reducing laser power as shown in Fig. 4.16d. The minimum linewidth can be reduced to about
200 nm, which is much larger than that in high-speed writing.
Experimental and simulated results indicate that high-speed writing is beneficial for reducing feature size of patterns.
--[p106]-- However, the relationship between writing speed and heat-spot size has not yet investigated systematically. In this case, AIST is taken as an example; the dependence of writing speed on heat-spot size is simulated. Figure 4.14a shows the temperature profiles at different writing speeds. The thickness of AIST thin film is 50 nm and the laser power is 0.8 mW. One can see that the peak temperature decreases with the increment of writing speed.
--[p106]-- Although a small heat-spot size can be obtained after improving writing speed, the peak temperature will be reduced to even below the threshold temperature of AIST. In this case, one needs to increase laser power so that the peak temperature can be higher than the threshold temperature. The temperature profiles at different laser powers are shown in Fig. 4.14c, where the writing speed is fixed at 10 m/s and AIST thickness is 50 nm.

--[p78]-- 3.4.2 Theoretical Analysis of Focus-Seeking with a Pinhole Method
In the measurement process of sample movement fluctuation error, the sample must be first placed at the focal plane of the objective lens. Thus, to easily perform the measurement, a method for auto-seeking the focal plane of the objective lens is required. Here, a pinhole method is presented based on the principle of confocal microscopy, where stray light can be effectively filtered out.
--[p78]-- That is, when the sample is at the focal plane of the objective lens, the reflected light beam could totally pass through the pinhole, resulting in a strong signal on the photodetector, which is placed at the backside of the pinhole. When the sample is out of focus, parts of the reflected light beam can be filtered out by the pinhole, thus generating a weak signal on the photodetector.
--[p78]-- The distance between $F _ { 1 } ^ { \prime }$ and $F _ { 2 }$ is $\Delta .$ . It can be seen from Fig. 3.19a that a collimated light ray (marked with green solid line) passes through $L _ { 1 }$ , point $F _ { 1 } ^ { \prime }$ , and $L _ { 2 } .$ , and then intersects with the optical axis $o o ^ { \prime }$ at point $F ^ { \prime }$ . The distance between $F _ { 2 } ^ { \prime }$ and $F ^ { \prime }$ is $x _ { F } ^ { \prime }$ .
--[p78]-- Figure 3.19a presents the schematic principle of the duplet lens system, where $L _ { 1 }$ is an objective lens used to focus the light onto the sample surface [5]. $L _ { 2 }$ is a converging lens to collect the light into the pinhole. The object and image points of $L _ { 1 }$ are marked as $\mathrm { F } _ { 1 }$ and $F _ { 1 } ^ { \prime }$ , respectively. The object and image distances of $L _ { 1 }$ are $f _ { 1 }$ and $f _ { 1 } ^ { \prime } { \mathrm { . } }$ , respectively.

===== [p121-121] Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists > 5.3 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Nanolithography (6块) =====
--[p121]-- The AIST is taken as an example and analyzed for the high-speed laser heat-mode nanolithography, where the movement speed and spot size are fixed at 8 m/s and 550 nm, respectively.
--[p121]-- The threshold effect of structural change can greatly reduce the feature size theoretically, however, it is actually difficult to reduce the feature size to below 100 nm due to thermal diffusion effect. One needs to manipulate the thermal diffusion and take advantage of some other physical effects and processes, such as optical nonlinear absorption and short laser irradiation time, and realize the high-speed laser heat-mode nanolithography.
--[p121]-- 5.3 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Nanolithography
The feature size of laser heat-mode lithography is dominated by the laser spot, structural change threshold effect, and thermal diffusion channels. For a given laser heat-mode lithography system, the laser spot is fixed. For example, the laser spot size is about 550 nm for a laser heat-mode lithography system with a laser wavelength of 405 nm and numerical aperture of converging lens of 0.90.
--[p121]-- In most laser heat-mode resists, there are nonlinear absorptions, such as nonlinear saturation absorption and reverse saturation absorption [17, 18]. The optical nonlinear absorption can cause the photothermal localization. As was pointed out in Chap. 4, however, the thermal diffusion may increase the photothermal response region.
--[p121]-- To shorten l, on one hand, one needs to select the heat-mode lithography materials with small $D ;$ on the other hand, the laser irradiation time should be as short as possible. There are two ways to short laser irradiation time. One is the laser writing with short laser pulse, and the other is the sample movement with high speed.
--[p121]-- To reduce the feature size, on one hand, the laser heat-mode lithography materials are required to have nonlinear absorption response to laser spot; on the other hand, one needs to manipulate the thermal diffusion distance l at the horizontal direction. The l can be estimated as $l = \sqrt { D t }$ , where $D$ is thermal diffusion coefficient, and t is laser irradiation time.

===== [p134-134] Chapter 6 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography Using Organic Thin Films > 6.2.2 Thermal Properties (4块) =====
--[p134]-- 6.2.2 Thermal Properties
In the patterning through photothermal localization, the $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ is required to have thermal threshold effect. The thermal threshold effect can be characterized through thermal gravimetric (TG) and differential thermal analyses (DSC), as shown in Fig. 6.3. In Fig.
--[p134]-- The structural relaxation is an exothermal process and occurs at $2 1 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ The glass transition is an endothermic process and occurs at about $2 2 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ . The gasification follows after glass transition. Figure 6.3 shows that the patterning on the $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ thin films can be directly obtained through the photothermal structural changes of glass transition and gasification reaction.
--[p134]-- 6.3a, one can see from the TG curve that the weight abruptly decreases to $\sim 3 0 \%$ at about $2 4 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ , which indicates that the $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ is gasified at ${ \sim } 2 4 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ , and there is a clear threshold effect for gasification reaction. The DSC curve of Fig.
--[p134]-- 6.3b indicates that the gasification reaction is an endothermic process, and the initial gasification temperature is about $2 3 5 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ , and the peak temperature of gasification is at $2 4 0 . 8 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . More importantly, before the gasification, there are also structural relaxation and glass transition, as shown in inset of Fig. 6.3b.

--[p158]-- 7.5.1.1 Laser-Induced Structural Change of AgInSbTe Thin Films
In order to realize micro/nanolithography of transparent thin film, a proper light absorption material is required. Chalcogenide phase-change material, such as AgInSbTe (AIST), is a promising candidate as the light absorption layer since it possesses a high absorption coefficient of about $1 0 ^ { 7 } / \mathrm { m }$ in a wide visible spectral range and evident phase-transition thermal threshold effect.
--[p161]-- The $d _ { \mathrm { Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } } }$ are the diameter of structural change region of ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film, and $d _ { \mathrm { Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } } } = d _ { \mathrm { A I S T } }$ according to Fig. 7.10b, where the structural change regions is also considered to be cylinder due to the thickness of ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film is only 40 nm.
--[p161]-- To further verify the phase-transition process, the crystal structures of written samples are characterized by X-ray diffraction (XRD) data and optical imaging methods before the micro/nanopatterns are etched in hydrofluoric acid solution. XRD results of AIST thin films are revealed in Fig. 7.11a, where the laser power is about 1.15 mW. The XRD result of as-deposited AIST thin film is also shown for comparison and presents an obvious amorphous characteristics.
--[p160]-- Assuming that the phase-transition latent-heat release of AIST thin film is not considered, the temperature of the ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film is mainly from the thermal diffusion of AIST layer.
--[p158]-- The area, also as crystallization latent heat (DH), is 45.67 kJ/kg. Therefore, AIST thin film possesses evident thermal threshold effect and can release latent heat after heating to phase-transition temperature.
--[p161]-- 7.10b gives the results. One can see that the peak temperature of ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film can reach up to $2 6 2 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ , which exceeds to the maximum exothermal peak temperature of $T _ { \mathrm { e x } } = 2 2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . Thus, significant structural rearrangement can occur in ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film.
--[p158]-- In order to understand the effect of the phase-transition latent-heat of AIST thin film on the temperature of ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film, the temperature distribution of AIST thin film is first determined by heat transfer equation as shown in Fig. 7.9. The sample structure is designed as $\mathrm { \mathrm { \mathrm { \Sigma ^ { * } Z n S - S i O } _ { 2 } / A I S T / g l a s s } }$ substrate”.
--[p158]-- Moreover, chalcogenide phase-change material can generate latent heat after phase-transition from amorphous state to crystalline state and its phase-transition temperature is moderate. Here, the AIST material is selected as a light absorption layer. The absorption coefficient of AIST thin film is $\sim 6 . 3 2 \times 1 0 ^ { 7 } / \mathrm { m }$ at 405 nm wavelength and AIST thin film presents single-photon absorption characteristics since the bandgap of amorphous AIST is 1.42 eV [14].
--[p161]-- $d _ { \mathrm { A I S T } } ( = 1 0 0 \mathrm { n m } )$ can be determined by finely tuning the laser power and irradiation time according to Fig. 7.9b, where the laser power is 0.66 mW and irradiation time is 100 ns. It is noted that the phase-transition region is considered as a cylinder due to the thickness of AIST thin films is only 20 nm.
--[p161]-- However, the written sample indicates that a sharp diffraction peak occurs and assigns to crystalline phase structure, which indicates that the AIST thin film is crystallized due to laser-induced heating. The optical image of the written sample is given in Fig. 7.11b. The optical image also reveals obvious crystallization lines after writing. Therefore, the phase-transition process of AIST thin film happens after laser writing.
--[p158]-- The thermal analysis of AIST film is performed by differential scanning calorimetry (DSC) measurements. Figure 7.8 gives its DSC result to confirm the latent-heat release and thermal threshold effect of phase-transition process. One can see that a sharp exothermal phase-transition (crystallization) peak occurs at about $T _ { p } = 2 0 6 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ , indicating the phase transition from amorphous to crystalline state.
--[p161]-- where $\rho _ { \mathrm { A I S T } } ( = 6 6 3 2 \mathrm { k g } / \mathrm { m } ^ { 3 } )$ and $\rho _ { \mathrm { { Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } } } } ( = 3 6 5 0 \mathrm { { k g / m ^ { 3 } } ) }$ are mass density of AIST and ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ , respectively [15].
--[p158]-- Figure 7.9a shows the three-dimensional (3D) temperature profile. It is noted that the laser power is fixed at 0.66 mW and the irradiation time is 100 ns. Here, AIST layer will undergo thermal diffusion. The thermal diffusion coefficient (D) of AIST films can be determined by
$$
D = \frac { \kappa _ { \mathrm { A I S T } } } { \rho _ { \mathrm { A I S T } } C _ { P - \mathrm { A I S T } } }\tag{ð<sup>7:5</sup>Þ}
$$
where $\kappa _ { \mathrm { A I S T } } \ ( = 1 .
--[p161]-- The structural rearrangement region is also about 100 nm, which is determined by the phase-transition threshold effect of AIST thin film. That is, the release of phase-transition latent-heat of AIST thin film is sufficient to make ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ generate evident local structural change.
--[p160]-- \Delta T = \left( \Delta H _ { \mathrm { A I S T } } m _ { \mathrm { A I S T } } \right) / \left( C _ { p \mathrm { Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } } } m _ { \mathrm { Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } } } \right)\tag{ð<sup>7:7</sup>Þ}
$$
where the $\Delta H _ { \mathrm { A I S T } }$ is phase-transition latent-heat of AIST thin film, here is 45.67 kJ/ kg according to Fig. 7.8.
--[p161]-- The micro/nanopatterns on ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin films can be further etched into micro/nanostructures after wet etching.
According to above calculation, the phase-transition latent-heat of AIST is crucial for micro/nanolithography of ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film.
--[p160]-- $C _ { p \mathrm { Z n S - S i O } _ { 2 } } ( = 5 6 0 \mathrm { J } / ( \mathrm { k g K } ) )$ is the heat capacity of $\mathrm { Z n S - }$ $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ thin film [16]. m and $m _ { \mathrm { Z n S - S i O _ { \it 4 } ^ { \prime } } }$ are mass of heated region of AIST and $Z { \mathrm { n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 } .$ , respectively.
$$
--[p159]-- When the laser irradiation time (t) is 100 ns, the thermal diffusion length (L) can be determined by
$$
L = \sqrt { D t }\tag{ð<sup>7:6</sup>Þ}
$$
The obtained L is only about 350 nm if horizontal thermal diffusion is considered. However, the high-speed writing can take away the heat by promoting air flow and further reduce the effect of horizontal thermal diffusion. Thus, the influence of thermal diffusion becomes actually small.
--[p160]-- Figure 7.10a gives the temperature profile of ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ layer, where the highest temperature $( T _ { \mathrm { m a x } } )$ is only $2 0 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ , which is lower than the critical temperature $( T _ { \mathrm { e x } } = 2 2 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ .
--[p159]-- 7 \ \mathrm { W / ( m K ) } )$ is thermal conductivity of AIST thin film; q<sub>AIST</sub> $( = 6 6 3 2 ~ \mathrm { k g } / \mathrm { m } ^ { 3 } )$ is the mass density of AIST thin film and $\mathrm { C _ { p - A I S T } \ } ( = 2 2 8 . 3 3 \ \mathrm { J / ( k g }$ K)) is heat capacity of AIST thin film [15]. Thus, the D is estimated to only $1 . 2 4 \times 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \ \mathrm { { m ^ { 2 } s ^ { - 1 } } }$ .
--[p161]-- In order to demonstrate that the micro/nanolithography of the ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film results from the phase-transition latent-heat release of AIST thin film, the samples with the two-layer of $\mathrm { \mathrm { \tilde { \Delta } Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } / A I S T / g l a s s } }$ substrate” are prepared through magnetron-controlled sputtering method.
--[p161]-- The $h _ { \mathrm { A I S T } }$ and $h _ { \mathrm { { Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } } } }$ are the thickness of AIST and ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin films, respectively. Here, $h _ { \mathrm { A I S T } } = 2 0$ nm and $h _ { \mathrm { Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } } } = 4 0$ nm. The $d _ { \mathrm { A I S T } }$ is the diameter of phase-transition region of AIST thin film.

===== [p31-31] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (1) DUV Light-Mode Resists (5块) =====
--[p31]-- Among them, some small active species, such as the photon-generated acid or external contaminant will diffuse and have some impact on the window stability of the lithography process.
--[p31]-- Compared to the 248 nm light-mode resist, DUV light-mode resists are grafted on the polymer backbone to achieve all the lithographic specifications and have more functionalities. As an example, alicyclic units, such as norbornane and adamantane, have then been introduced in order to achieve a high C:H ratio and maintain a sufficient etching resistance (Fig. 1.14) [29].
--[p31]-- (1) DUV Light-Mode Resists
Thus far, 193 nm wavelength (DUV) immersion lithography has allowed the manufacturing of 10 nm node ICs in the semiconductor industry [28]. In this case, DUV light-mode resist is essentially a chemical amplifier resist. This concept has been retained for the development of DUV light-mode resists in order to keep a sufficient transparency at the DUV wavelength. However, the polymer backbone has been changed from poly-hydroxystyrene to acrylate.
--[p31]-- As the complexity of the polymer chemistry has increased with DUV light-mode resists, the control of the polymer composition and monomer arrangement can play an important role in the final lithographic performance, especially in the line edge roughness.
--[p31]-- For DUV light-mode resists, the glass transition temperature of the backbone polymers is approximately within the range of $1 5 0 { \mathrm { - } } 1 7 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ Thus, the majority of the chemically amplified DUV light-mode resists is in the non-annealing state. Some residual solvent and extra-free volume may remain inside the resist film after the postexposure baking treatment.

===== [p61-63] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.2 Basic Layout (11块) =====
--[p62]-- The modulated spot irradiates onto the sample surface, when the sample is heated to the temperature of structural change, and the heat-mode exposure happens, and the pattern structures are written on the sample accordingly.
--[p61]-- The sample is placed on a vacuum sorption stage, whose diameter is 120 mm. The vacuum sorption stage is driven by an air-bearing rotation motor with a maximum linear speed of tens of meters per second. The rotation movement angle (h) is instantaneously measured using a circular grating ruler and then sent back to the computer. The radial movement is provided by a linear motor, where the light module is installed on the linear movement motor.
--[p62]-- The laser beam is filtered and expanded, and then is reflected into the objective lens (numerical aperture $N A = 0 . 8 – 0 . 9 5 )$ and focused into a diffraction-limited spot onto the sample surface. The sample is placed onto the rotation-type movement stage. The blue-violet laser diode can be modulated into arbitrary pulsed light through connecting a signal generator to the high-frequency TTL analog/digital modulation port.
--[p63]-- However, compared with opto-mechanical module of traditional DVD or Blu-ray player system, the servo-tracking control accuracy, beam spot profile, and actuator design are greatly improved.
--[p62]-- The servo-tracking controller is dedicatedly designed to receive a focusing error signal (FES) from the photodetector and then to control the focusing actuator in closed loop. Moreover, a constant current driver with automatic power control (APC) is also adopted for the intensity of 405 nm blue laser diode. A computer is used to collect data and communicate with the servo-tracking control main board and the blue laser opto-mechanical unit (D).
--[p61]-- 3.2 Basic Layout
Figure 3.1 gives the schematics of $( r , \theta )$ polar coordinate laser heat-mode lithography system, where a GaN semiconductor diode laser device emits a light beam with a wavelength $\lambda = 4 0 5$ nm. The light beam propagates through a beam expander and is reflected into a high NA objective lens. The light beam is focused on the diffraction-limited spot on the sample surface. The spot size is about 0.50–0.55 lm at $1 / e ^ { 2 }$ maximum intensity.
--[p61]-- Additionally, the servo-tracking module needs to be designed and installed onto the system to retain the focal plane of writing spot onto the sample surface during the heat-mode exposure.
The basic optical layout is installed on a linear movement stage, and includes writing unit, illumination module, imaging, and servo-tracking units, as shown in Fig. 3.2a, where the sample is placed onto the rotation stage. The writing unit functions as an exposure equipment.
--[p62]-- In the process of direct laser writing, the servo tracking is used to keep the sample at the focal plane of the objective lens, where the objective lens is mounted onto an actuator (such as PZT or mechanical actuator) with z-direction movement. The illumination and imaging modules are used to observe the marks and pattern structures on the sample surface. Figure 3.2b shows the optical layout of writing unit, where a GaN diode emits a laser beam with a light wavelength of 405 nm.
--[p62]-- Figure 3.3a shows a real setup of $r { - } \theta$ type laser heat-mode lithography system, including servo-tracking control main board (marked with A); spin motor (h-axis, marked with B); high precision movement stage (r-axis, marked with C); small opto-mechanical module (marked with D) [3].
(1) The A has laser power control, servo-tracking control, and laser modulation functions.
--[p63]-- (2) The function for obtaining the substrate rotation (B) and r-axis linear movement (C) is supplied by the original servo-tracking control system and software of the laser heat-mode lithography. A magnification photography of optical head-like opto-mechanical module (marked as D in Fig. 3.3a) is shown in Fig. 3.3b, where the design rule of the opto-mechanical module is from traditional optical disk data storage system.
--[p61]-- The linear movement distance (marked as $r )$ is measured using a linear grating ruler. The rotation stage and linear motor are mounted into a polar coordinate $( r , \theta )$ system. The laser beam spot position on the sample can be rapidly obtained as a polar coordinate $( r , \theta )$ by reading the data from the linear and circular grating rulers.

===== [p120-120] Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists > 5.2.3 ${ \pmb T e O } _ { \bf x }$ Resists [16] (8块) =====
--[p120]-- That is, the formation of the $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ path by exposure and the dissolution of the path by the alkaline solution are the mechanisms that underlie the functionality of the ${ \mathrm { T e O } } _ { x } .$ -based heat-mode lithography material. In unexposed area, there is no $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ path.
--[p120]-- Figure 5.7c, d is the developed surface of pattern structures without and with thermal isolation films, respectively. One can see that the pattern structures with thermal isolation film are very clear and smooth, and the thermal isolation film can improve the patterning resolution. Besides, the exposure sensitivity is increased under the same condition as the case of with thermal isolation film due to the heat loss being greatly reduced.
--[p120]-- The laser spot irradiated onto the $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ thin films, and the Te was crystallized when the laser energy was suitable. Figure 5.7b shows the transmission electron microscope (TEM) image of a cross- section of an $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ thin film after the laser irradiation, in which phase change occurs at the upper region of the film (enclosed by a broken line). The dark regions consist of Te-based fine particles and the bright areas are $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$
--[p120]-- 5.2.3 ${ \pmb T e O } _ { \bf x }$ Resists [16]
TeO -based film, which mainly consists of Te and $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ and contains some dopants, can be obtained through reactive sputtering method. The sample is $ { ^ { 6 6 } } \mathrm { T e O } _ { x } /$ organic film/substrate”, where the organic film is used as thermal isolation film to increase the temperature at the bottom of $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ layer (as shown in Fig. 5.7a).
--[p120]-- In developing with alkaline solution, Te-based fine particles cover the surface of the film and stop dissolving, after the dissolution of $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ located on surface of the film.
It should be explained that the thermal isolation film is very useful for improving the exposure sensitivity and pattern resolution. Random pits are formed on the $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ layer.
--[p120]-- Before laser exposure, Te-based fine particles and $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ are homogeneously distributed in the non-exposed areas. After laser exposure, Te-based crystals have grown and $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ is continuously distributed among Te-based crystals in the phase-change areas. That is, the path of $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ was formed.
--[p120]-- Then, the exposed area was developed in alkaline solution to form pits, based on the characteristics that $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ is dissoluble in alkaline solution, and the Te-based material is insoluble.
In exposed areas, $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ , which is distributed continuously, keeps on dissolving and Te-based crystals flow into the alkaline solution, although the Te-based material itself is not dissolved.
--[p120]-- The thermal isolation film has two functional features. On one hand, it prevents the heat at the bottom from flowing to the substrate, which is achieved by thermal isolation layer with low thermal conductivity. On the other hand, it is optically designed to heat the bottom using the reflection from the substrate, which is achieved through transparency and optically designed thickness.
The laser heat-mode lithography was carried out by a laser writing setup with a wavelength of 351 nm.

===== [p151-151] Chapter 7 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Transparent Thin Films > 7.3 Thermo-Optical Properties of $\mathbf { Z n S - S i O } _ { 2 }$ Thin Films [1] (7块) =====
--[p151]-- However, the n and k at 405 nm wavelength are 2.1 and 0.014, respectively. The low absorption at 405 nm is not beneficial for direct patterning on ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin films while high refractive index in visible light range makes it be an interesting candidate for metasurface-based optical elements. Thus, it is an interesting issue that how to fabricate micro/ nanostructures on transparent thin films.
--[p151]-- In addition, the etching selectivity of ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film decreases as temperature decreases, which can be found in previous experiments. This may be because the structural change of $\mathrm { Z n S - }$ $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ thin film is a function of temperature, and does not show any threshold effect.
--[p151]-- In order to fabricate micro/nanostructures on a ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film, optical constants including refractive index (n) and extinction coefficient (k) are investigated by spectroscopic ellipsometry as shown in Fig. 7.3. One can see that the n and k values decrease with decreasing light wavelength. In general, the laser wavelength for direct laser writing is 405 nm.
--[p151]-- This indicates that the ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film has no obvious thermal threshold effect and significant structural rearrangement occurs at $T _ { \mathrm { e x } } = 2 2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ This also tells us that the ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film allows for maximum etching selectivity when the temperature exceeds to $2 2 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$
--[p151]-- The ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ composite has high transparency in visible and near-infrared light range and low thermal conductivity of 0.67 W/(m K). The low thermal conductivity can efficiently suppress the thermal diffusion in radial direction and is beneficial for the reduction of pattern linewidth.
--[p151]-- 7.3 Thermo-Optical Properties of $\mathbf { Z n S - S i O } _ { 2 }$ Thin Films [1]
In order to perform patterning on a transparent thin film, the thermo-optical properties are necessary to be understood. Here, a ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film is used as an example.
--[p151]-- The DSC analysis of the as-deposited ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film shown in Fig. 7.2 also indicates that there is an initial transformation point at $1 0 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ then the structural change goes on with temperature rise, and a maximum exothermal peak $( T _ { \mathrm { e x } } )$ occurs at $2 2 0 ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ .

--[p47]-- Thus, the heat diffusion can make the pattern feature size be larger than spot size. One can make use of the heat diffusion and obtain the trans-scale lithography from spot size to microscale.

--[p47]-- The heat diffusion makes the temperature rise occur in the region of outside spot. The lithography reaction (thermally induced structural change) will happen in the region outside spot when the temperature rise exceeds the thermal threshold, as is shown in Fig. 2.13. That is, the heat-mode exposure can take place even if there is no light irradiation. The lithography region size will be larger than the spot itself, accordingly.
--[p47]-- 2.3.3 Trans-Scale Lithography
In laser heat-mode lithography, trans-scale lithography from microscale to nanoscale can be achieved by tuning thermal threshold and heat diffusion. Figure 2.12 gives the schematics that the feature size of pattern structures is tuned from nanoscale to spot size through thermal threshold, where the spot with a Gaussian intensity profile is used as exposure light source. For example, in Fig.
--[p47]-- 6 \mu \mathrm { m }$ , which is easily obtained through the focusing optical system with a laser wavelength of 405 nm and numerical aperture of objective lens of 0.90, the lithography region size can be continuously reduced from 0.6 lm to 75 nm through the tuning threshold effect.
Laser heat-mode lithography is essentially a thermally induced structural change, and there is heat diffusion effect in the process of laser heat-mode exposure.
--[p48]-- Figure 2.14b is the experimental results, where a series of dots with different feature size are written through laser heat-mode lithography, the feature size is changed from 100 to 400 nm.
--[p48]-- One can see that the feature size of patterns decreases with both laser power and laser irradiation time (pulse duration), that is, the feature size of patterns decreases with laser energy (laser energy = laser pulse irradiation time). The feature size can change from 5 lm to 50 nm through tuning laser energy.

===== [p42-43] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.3 Heat-Mode Lithography (7块) =====
--[p42]-- Thus, the laser heat-mode lithography exposure is actually photophysical effect, not photochemical effect of light-mode lithography exposure.
The change of atomic arrangements in laser heat-mode lithography is schematically presented in Fig. 2.7, where the initial heat-mode resist is amorphous. A laser spot scans the sample to conduct the exposure, and the crystallization takes place after laser spot scanning if the laser energy exceeds certain threshold.
--[p42]-- In contrast to photochemical process of light-mode lithography, laser heat-mode lithography is a photothermal process. When a laser heat-mode resist is used, the resolution is not dependent on the integrated light exposure dose, but largely determined by the temperature profile at one moment. At the instant of exposure, there is a large temporary difference between the peak temperature at the center of the exposed area and the temperature of the surrounding area.
--[p43]-- The amorphous disorder arrangement becomes crystalline order arrangement. The etching selectivity occurs between disorder amorphous and order crystalline states in alkali solutions, and the disorder amorphous state is etched due to defects, accordingly. Finally, the order crystalline region (laser spot irradiation area) remains and the pattern structures are formed, as shown in Fig. 2.7e.
--[p42]-- 2.3 Heat-Mode Lithography
In conventional light-mode lithography, the photoresist layer is exposed throughout the entire laser spot. The exposed region undergoes a photochemical reaction to an extent that is proportional to the intensity of the light. The limit of the pattern resolution is approximately equal to the laser spot size because the photochemical reaction does not have a clear intensity threshold effect.
--[p42]-- Some inorganic nonmetallic materials can experience a thermally induced structural change when heated to certain temperature, as shown in Fig. 2.5. For example, the transition from amorphous to crystalline state occurs in Te-based chalcogenides when heated. The amorphous phase of inorganic nonmetallic thin-film materials has lots of defects such as dangling bonds, while the crystalline state has a few defects.
--[p42]-- In acid or alkali solutions, different wet-etching rates may take place among different phases, thus etching selectivity can occur in the developing process.
The schematic of laser heat-mode lithography exposure is displayed in Fig. 2.6, where the amorphous heat-mode resists are directly deposited on the substrates. The laser beam is used as a heating source and irradiates onto the heat-mode resists.
--[p42]-- The heat-mode resists absorb the laser energy and is heated to certain temperature such as crystallization point or phase-change temperature. The structural change from amorphous state to crystalline state happens, and the color of heat-mode resist changes from dark gray (before irradiation) to light gray (after irradiation). The laser heat-mode exposure is completed, accordingly.

===== [p115-115] Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists > 5.2 Te-Based Chalcogenides as Heat-Mode Resists (3块) =====
--[p115]-- The optical absorption coefficient at visible light wavelength is about $1 0 ^ { 7 } / \mathrm { m }$ , and the thermal diffusion coefficient is $\sim 1 0 ^ { - 6 } \mathrm { m } ^ { 2 } \mathrm { s } ^ { - 1 } \mathrm { \bar { [ 6 ] } }$ .
--[p115]-- According to the requirements, Te-based chalcogenide materials are good candidates due to strong visible light absorption characteristics, suitable structural change (crystallization) threshold temperature, and etching selectivity between amorphous and crystalline states. --[p115]-- 5.2 Te-Based Chalcogenides as Heat-Mode Resists
As is well known, the Te-based chalcogenide materials are usually used in rewritable optical recording and random access memory due to obvious threshold characteristics of structural change from amorphous state to crystalline state at about $1 0 0 { - } 2 0 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ .

===== [p22-22] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.2.3 Probe-Based Lithography (6块) =====
--[p22]-- (1) The lithography is a single-step process, and does not require subsequent developing (wet etching).
(2) The imaging and patterning in SPL are parallael, that is, the imaging process neither influences the written structures nor involves a partial writing operation.
The SPL possesses the fabrication capability with a nanoscale feature size. However, the lifetime of the probe tip is short, and the writing speed and the throughput are very low.
--[p22]-- (2) Different methods are proposed to control the position of the scanning probe relative to the surface underneath, such as quantum tunneling between the probe and a conductive surface (corresponding to the scanning tunneling microscope), and controlling the force between the probe and the surface (corresponding to the atomic force microscopy).
Figure 1.8a shows a general schematic of SPL, where the probe modifies a material at the interface between the probe and the surface [19].
--[p22]-- (1) Lithography processes, including mechanical, thermal, electrostatic, and chemical interactions, or different combinations of among them, which could be controlled using a sharp probe in contact or close to a nanoscale region of a sample surface.
--[p22]-- 1.2.3 Probe-Based Lithography
Probe-based lithography originated from scanning probe microscopy. The scanning probe microscopes can be used to image, modify, and manipulate surfaces at the nanometer and atomic scales.
--[p22]-- The scanning probe lithography (SPL) method is proposed based on the characteristics of atomic-scale manipulation and modification in the material surface, and usually applied to academic research owing to the nanoscale feature-size, relatively low technological requirements, and the ability to handle soft matter from small organic molecules to proteins and polymers [19].
In fact, the occurrence of SPL results from two main factors.
--[p22]-- The SPL can be classified in terms of the driving mechanisms used in the lithography process, such as thermal, electrical, mechanical, and diffusive, as shown in Fig. 1.8b. These approaches have a common performance, using a sharp scanning probe to produce local modification on the surfaces of the materials.
Compared to other techniques, such as electron-beam lithography, the advantages of SPL are as follows:

===== [p19-20] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.2.1.3 Chemical Self-Assembly (5块) =====
--[p19]-- 1.2.1.3 Chemical Self-Assembly
Self-assembly is a process of spontaneous formation of pattern structures without human intervention [11]. Self-assembly can occur at the molecular level through noncovalent or weak covalent interactions, such as van der Waals, electrostatic, hydrophobic interactions, or interfacial hydrogen bonding. Self-assembly can also occur at large-scale macroscopic levels based on gravitational, capillary, and external electromagnetic forces.
--[p20]-- Polyethylene terephthalate (PET) is chosen as the flexible substrate owing to its optical transparency at visible wavelength. A thin Si layer is deposited on PET substrate by electron-beam evaporation. Subsequently, a monolayer of polystyrene (PS) spheres is self-assembled on the surface of the Si layer, whereby the PS spheres form a compact hexagonal lattice array. A reactive-ion etching (RIE) is then utilized to transfer the close-packed lattice patterns to the Si layer.
--[p19]-- In the self-assembly process, the individual parts have to be mobile in a particular order. Therefore, the self-assembly generally occurs in the liquid phase or on a smooth surface, such that the individual parts can move around and interact with each other.
In real self-assembly process, numerous factors influence the formation of pattern structures, such as the environment, input of external energy, and confined geometrical boundaries.
--[p20]-- The final pattern structure is at the equilibrium state, where the individual parts are kept at equal distances with respect to each other, and not at random aggregation. The pattern structures are ordered in the long-range beyond a few molecules once the interaction reaches an equilibration state.
Figure 1.7 shows a schematic of the nanofabrication based on chemical self-assembly [12].
--[p20]-- The polystyrene (PS) spheres act as a mask in the process of pattern transfer. Finally, the sample is immersed in chloroform to remove all the remaining PS spheres with sonication, and Si nanostructures are fabricated on flexible substrates.
The self-assembly technique can only be utilized to fabricate periodic nanostructures such as nanoholes and nanodisks. Some complex and nonperiodic structures are difficult to be fabricated through self-assembly.
