===== [p186-187] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.3.1 Optical Reflectivity Change with Laser Irradiation Energy (9块) =====
--[p186]-- The crystallization extent can be finely controlled through tuning the laser energy, and the optical reflectivity changes with the crystallization extent. Thus, one can conduct the grayscale image lithography on the chalcogenide phase-change thin films through tuning crystallization extent.
$\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ is a kind of typical chalcogenide material and taken as an example of grayscale lithography through tuning crystallization extent.
--[p186]-- From the inset of Fig. 8.7b, the grayscale of $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ thin films becomes lighter with increasing laser energy from 0 to 3700 mJ/cm<sup>2</sup> and then darker with further increasing laser energy from 3700 to $2 8 , 0 0 0 \ \mathrm { m J / c m } ^ { 2 }$ . The reflection spectrum indicates that the reflectivity reaches the maximum when the laser energy is $3 7 0 0 \ \mathrm { m J / c m } ^ { 2 }$ .
--[p186]-- Further increasing laser energy leads to the gradual reduction of reflectivity. The difference of reflectivity is as high as 20%, in turn generating large optical contrast.
The structural evolution can also happen through the irradiation of laser pulse, such as nanosecond laser pulse and femtosecond laser pulse, and the crystallization extent is tuned through changing the pulse energy (including laser pulse power and pulse number).
--[p186]-- Further increasing laser energy to 28,000 mJ/ $\mathrm { c m } ^ { 2 }$ , the diffraction peaks become weaker gradually, which is due to that the films are excessively ablated after laser irradiation and then transformed back to an amorphous state.
The structural evolution causes the change of optical reflective spectra, as shown in Fig. 8.7b, where the $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ thin films are irradiated through different laser energy.
--[p187]-- The divergence of reflectivity causes the grayscale level, as shown in Fig. 8.8b, where the optical reflectivity contrast changes with laser pulse number.
--[p186]-- Figure 8.7a is XRD analysis on the structural evolution due to continuous wave (CW) laser irradiation, only a small mountain peak centered at $2 8 ^ { \circ }$ is observed in as-deposited sample, indicating an amorphous state. After laser irradiation, obvious diffraction peaks of the thin films are determined corresponding to typically metastable face-centered cubic crystals. The diffraction peaks become stronger with the increase of laser energy from 3700 to 12,000 mJ/cm<sup>2</sup>.
--[p186]-- 8.3.1 Optical Reflectivity Change with Laser Irradiation Energy
In the irradiation of laser pulse, besides the morphology change, the chalcogenide phase-change thin films also experience a structural change from amorphous state to crystalline state when the temperature exceeds the crystallization threshold, which is accompanied with an optical reflectivity change.
--[p187]-- Under the pulse energy of 0.28 nJ, the reflectivity of $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ thin film increases with the pulse number. Under the pulse energy of 0.36 nJ, the reflectivity increases quickly with the number of pulses increasing. Under the same laser pulse number, the reflectivity increases with increasing pulse energy. The divergence of reflectivity is mainly due to the difference of crystalline extent.
--[p187]-- Figure 8.8a shows the reflectivity contrast of $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ thin film versus pulse number of 85 fs duration with different pulse energy applied. When the pulse energy is 0.21 nJ, the reflectivity has no apparent changes when the pulse number is less than 45. The crystallization occurs and the reflectivity changes when the pulse number is more than 45.

===== [p182-185] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.2.3 Grayscale Lithography Through Laser-Induced Micro/Nanostructures (17块) =====
--[p184]-- If the pulse energy is further increased to the ablation threshold of the material, the ablation occurs and a hole forms at the center of bowl-shape.
Based on laser-induced micro/nanostructures and light scattering and trapping effects, the continuous-tone grayscale images are written on $\mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 3 }$ thin films by laser heat-mode lithography.
--[p183]-- The bottom of the “bowl” then becomes flat, and a round, smooth central dome starts to grow out. For even higher laser power, the height of the dome gradually increases due to the continuous increase of the chemicapillary effect and interior vaporization expansion effect, finally reaching a maximum value that is larger than the height of the rim, thus the pattern shape looks like a Sombrero.
--[p184]-- Figure 8.6c shows the SEM images of micro/ nanostructures for gray levels of 1–8. The size of structure increases with increasing gray level. Gray level 1 presents a very fine solid-bump structure with a size of 300 nm due to low laser energy. With the increase of laser energy, the cavity-bump structures begin to form and the grayscale becomes dark. The larger the bump size, the darker the grayscale and the gray level value becomes larger.
--[p184]-- 50 nm in height and the center hole is about 50 nm in depth, which is approximate to the thickness of the film. The profile of the central hole area also shows that the bottom of the hole is smooth, which indicates that the film is completely melted by the laser pulse and also makes it like a deep bowl.
More micro/nanostructures can be obtained through carefully tuning laser pulse width or power. As is shown in Fig. 8.5, where one refines the scale of the pulse width.
--[p184]-- The reflectivity difference between two adjacent levels is apparent. The reflectivity decreases with the increment of gray level due to the light scattering and trapping effects. This is because the height of micro/nanostructures decreases with the reduction of the laser energy. The light scattering and trapping effects also decreases with the reduction of laser energy, accordingly. The weak light scattering and trapping effects enhance the reflection.
--[p182]-- $\mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 3 }$ is one of the chalcogenide materials, and very sensitive to temperature, the Marangoni and interior vaporization effects can be observed more than other materials when induced by laser pulses, and different patterns can be formed accordingly. Thus, $\mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 3 }$ is taken as an example and different grayscale images are directly written on the $\mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 3 }$ thin films.
--[p183]-- Figure 8.4b shows AFM photo of the typical pattern unit with Sombrero-shape, and the inset in the photo is the corresponding cross-sectional profile. The laser power is about 6.5 mW and laser pulse width is about 30 ns. The deep ring surrounding the bump center is about 25 nm in depth, and the bump center is about 50 nm in height, which makes it similar to a Sombrero-shape. The chemicapillary and vaporization effects also reach the peak values and are completely dominant.
--[p185]-- 8.6d, e indicates that the appearance and expression of the bird have been fully written. Figure 8.6f, g shows the original and written images of a lifelike cat, respectively. By comparison, the expression and appearance of the lifelike cat, especially the fire-like eyes and some fine structures like furs and whiskers, are fully inscribed by precisely controlling laser energy.
--[p184]-- At $t _ { p } < 2 0$ ns, the material structure forms the patterns with a dimple bowl shape. $\mathrm { A t } 2 0 \mathrm { n s } < t _ { p } < 3 4 \mathrm { n s }$ , the rounded and smooth central dome grows out to the dome gradually increasing to its maximum value. At $t _ { p } > 3 4 \mathrm { n s }$ , the central dome is gradually broadened following the increase of pulse energy, and the bowl shape patterns become deeper and deeper.
--[p184]-- Further increasing laser energy leads to the coarse cavity-bump structures with a size of 1 lm. The grayscale is the darkest.

--[p183]-- If the laser power becomes higher, the center part of the molten zone attains a higher temperature and the thermocapillary effect becomes dominant again. This effect pushes the material outward, causing the central dome to broaden and develops into a deeper depression or dimple in its middle part. At the same time, the interior vaporization effect can cause the top of the micro/nanostructures to rupture.
--[p182]-- 8.2.3 Grayscale Lithography Through Laser-Induced Micro/Nanostructures
The micro/nanostructures can change the optical reflectivity and transmission due to light scattering and trapping effects, thus the grayscale image lithography can be obtained through the micro/nanostructures.
--[p182]-- The amorphous $\mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 3 }$ thin films with a thickness of 50–100 nm are directly deposited on the glass substrate through sputtering method at room temperature. When a focused Gaussian laser beam is used as the energy source, different surface patterns, such as bowl shape, dome shape, and Sombrero shape, can be formed due to the competition between the thermocapillary and chemicapillary effects, as are shown in Fig. 8.4.
--[p184]-- Figure 8.6 displays the grayscale levels and corresponding microscopic structures at different laser energy irradiations. In Fig. 8.6a, the laser energy intensities increases gradually when grayscale changes from level 1 to level 8. Level 1 is the lightest and level 8 is the darkest. These characteristics are beneficial for the formation of complex arbitrarily grayscale images. Figure 8.6b indicates the corresponding reflection spectra of the grayscale levels.
--[p183]-- Thus, the Sombrero shape gradually disappears, an irregular crater becomes observable, and a deep bowl shape pattern is produced. Figure 8.4c shows AFM photo of the typical pattern unit with deep bowl shape, and the inset in the photo is the corresponding cross-sectional profile. The laser power and laser pulse width are about 6.5 mW and 40 ns, respectively. The bump ring surrounding the hole is about
--[p183]-- If the laser energy is slightly higher than the melting threshold, the temperature gradient along the radial direction occurs, the material partially melts. Moreover, the thermocapillary effect is dominant and drives the material from the hot center to the cold periphery, then forms the patterns with a central depression and a surrounding rim, which looks like a dimple bowl. Figure 8.4a is the AFM photo of the typical dimple bowl shape and the inset is the corresponding cross-sectional profile.
--[p183]-- The laser power is 8 mW and the laser pulse width is 5 ns. As shown in the photo, the depth of the dimple bowl is only about 6 nm below the surface.
As the laser energy increases to another threshold, the chemicapillary effect is strengthened and some materials in molten pool are vaporized. A surfactant concentration gradient is established in the molten pool and carries the material toward the center.

===== [p27-28] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (4) Direct Laser Writing Through Focused Beam Spot (7块) =====
--[p27]-- The arbitrary patterns can be formed by pattern generator with a combined operation of the movement stage and the signal generator modulating the laser pulse.
Direct laser writing has the merits of operation in air and large-area pattern fabrication with high throughput. However, it is difficult to overcome the diffraction limit and to fabricate trans-scale structures ranging from the micrometer to the nanometer. Lithography procedure is complex since light-mode orgainc resist is used.
--[p27]-- The sample is placed onto a movement stage such as a two-dimensional piezoelectric transducer (PZT). The blue–violet laser diode can be modulated into arbitrary pulsed light by connecting a signal generator to the high-frequency TTL analog/digital modulation port.
--[p27]-- (4) Direct Laser Writing Through Focused Beam Spot
Direct laser writing through a focused beam spot is a common and typical lithography method [25, 26]. Figure 1.12 displays the schematic of the direct laser writing system. A blue–violet laser diode emits the laser beam with a light wavelength of 405 nm. The laser beam is filtered and expanded, and is then reflected into the objective lens and focused into a diffraction-limited spot onto the sample surface.
--[p27]-- The red light is reflected and passes through the 1/4 waveplate again, and becomes s-polarized light. The s-polarized light is refracted into a cylindrical lens and forms a focal spot onto the four-quadrant detector.
--[p28]-- The lithography needs the processes of soft baking, postexposure baking, and hard baking. Moreover, the lithography resolution is limited by organic molecular structures where the light-mode organic resists have large molecular groups. Thus, the resolution and line edge roughness of these patterns is poor.
--[p27]-- During the execution of the process of direct laser writing, the servo-tracking will be used to keep the sample at the focal plane of the objective lens, whereby the objective lens is mounted onto the PZT with movement along the z-direction.
For servo-tracking, an astigmatism method is used, where p-polarized red light laser beam passes through the polarized beam splitter and the 1/4 waveplate, enters the objective lens, and is then focused onto the sample surface.
--[p27]-- Based on the principle of astigmatism, the shape of the focal spot by the cylindrical lens can be tuned among circular spot, the first and third quadrant ellipse spot, and the second and fourth quadrant ellipse spot according to the sample position at the focal plane of the objective lens. The objective lens is moved in the z-direction by a PZT according to the signal from the four-quadrant detector.

===== [p60-60] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.1 Introduction (5块) =====
--[p60]-- To avoid the continuous deceleration/acceleration and stitching effects, the laser heat-mode lithography is usually conducted through high-speed rotation-type laser writing system, where the laser wavelength is 405 nm and the numerical aperture (NA) of objective lens (converging lens) is about 0.8–0.95. The sample is placed onto a rotation stage, the rotation stage can spin at a velocity of more than 1000–2000 r/min.
--[p60]-- 3.1 Introduction
Laser heat-mode lithography can write micro/nanostructures on the heat-sensitive resist thin-film materials. When one uses the traditional direct laser writing system to conduct heat-mode lithography, the writing speed is slow.
--[p60]-- For example, the real velocity of traditional x-y-type vector-mode writing is only several millimeters or centimeters per second due to continuous deceleration/acceleration effect; the velocity of beam-scan mode is faster than that of vector-mode, however, it still is not enough for high-speed writing nanoscale-resolved patterns due to pattern stitching and continuous deceleration/acceleration effects of galvanometers.
--[p60]-- The focused spot irradiates onto the sample surface, the sample is heated to the temperature of structural change, and the heat-mode exposure happens, accordingly. In the meantime, the focused spot can move along the radial direction, thus, the rotation and radial movements establish a $( r , \theta )$ polar coordinate system, and the high-speed large-area laser heat-mode exposure can be carried out through the rotation-type $( r , \theta )$ polar coordinate lithography system.
--[p60]-- For writing nanoscale-resolved pattern structures on large size samples, the exposure speed is required to be m/s or about $1 0 0 ~ \mathrm { m m } ^ { 2 } / \mathrm { m i n }$ for the patterns with a feature size of about 100 nm.

===== [p111-111] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.5.3 Optimization of Laser Writing Strategy (4块) =====
--[p111]-- For the fabrication of dot array structures, the optimal laser pulse width of 8 ns is required, and the writing speed is fixed at 6 m/s. Figure 4.20a, b shows the normalized temperature profiles of AIST thin films at continuous laser mode and pulse laser mode, respectively. For comparison, normalized laser spot intensity profiles are also indicated. It can be seen that the heat-spot size is very close to that of the laser spot.
--[p111]-- In addition, short laser pulse time can minimize the in-plane thermal diffusion distance thus restrains the heat-spot size.
--[p111]-- This indicates that both upper and lower Si layers can guide the heat flow along the out-of-plane thermal diffusion channel of the AIST layer. At the same time, high-speed writing/movement can further promote air flow above the upper Si layer and accelerate the cooling of the upper Si layer. Thus, the out-of-plane thermal diffusion channel is further improved, and the in-plane thermal diffusion channel is suppressed. Finally, the heat-spot size can become close to the laser spot size.
--[p111]-- 4.5.3 Optimization of Laser Writing Strategy
Based on the above analysis, one of optimized laser writing strategies can be given as follow. The sample structure is “upper Si layer/AIST layer/lower Si layer/ substrate.” The thickness of upper Si and lower Si layers are both 40 nm, meanwhile, the AIST thickness is 50 nm, and the laser power is 2 mW, as shown in inset of Fig. 4.20. For the line pattern structures, continuous laser writing mode is used, and writing speed is 4 m/s.

===== [p64-65] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.3.2 Theoretical Analysis of Focusing Error Signals (5块) =====
--[p64]-- 3.3.2 Theoretical Analysis of Focusing Error Signals
The FES can be analyzed as follows: $A _ { 1 } , A _ { 2 } , A _ { 3 } ,$ and $A _ { 4 }$ are the areas of the spot on each quadrant of the FQD, as shown in Fig. 3.5, with intensities of $I _ { 1 } , I _ { 2 } , I _ { 3 }$ , and $I _ { 4 }$
respectively. $r _ { x }$ and $r _ { y }$ are the radii of the x-axis and y-axis of the spot on the FQD, respectively. The FES is defined as
$$
--[p65]-- { \mathrm { F E S } } = { \frac { - I _ { 1 } + I _ { 2 } - I _ { 3 } + I _ { 4 } } { I _ { 1 } + I _ { 2 } + I _ { 3 } + I _ { 4 } } }\tag{ð<sup>3:1</sup>Þ}
$$
One assumes that the spot on the FQD has a uniform intensity distribution; then, one can obtain
$$
I _ { i } = C A _ { i } \left( i = 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 \right)\tag{ð <sup>3:2</sup>Þ}
$$
where C is a constant. Considering the symmetry of the spot,
$$
A _ { 1 } = A _ { 3 } , A _ { 2 } = A _ { 4 }\tag{ð<sup>3:3</sup>Þ}
$$
--[p65]-- Based on formulas (3.1)–(3.3) and geometrical relationship of the spot, one can calculate FES as follows:
$$
\mathrm { F E S } = \frac { 2 } { \pi } \left[ \arcsin \frac { \left| \boldsymbol { r } _ { x } \right| } { \sqrt { r _ { x } ^ { 2 } + r _ { y } ^ { 2 } } } - \arcsin \frac { \left| \boldsymbol { r } _ { y } \right| } { \sqrt { r _ { x } ^ { 2 } + r _ { y } ^ { 2 } } } \right]\tag{ð<sup>3:4</sup>Þ}
$$
--[p65]-- $a _ { y }$ and $b _ { y }$ are object and image distances of $\mathrm { C L } _ { y } ,$ respectively. $l _ { x y }$ is the distance between $\mathrm { C L } _ { x }$ and $\mathrm { C L } _ { y }$ . In order to calculate the $r _ { x }$ value, one can consider $\mathrm { C L } _ { y }$ as a glass plate, as is shown in Fig. 3.6a, where $r _ { o }$ and $r _ { \mathrm { c l x } }$ are the radii of light beam onto $L _ { o }$ and $C L _ { x }$ , respectively.
$m _ { x }$ is the distance between $\mathbf { C L } _ { x }$ and the FQD. Based on the Gaussian imaging formula, one can obtain
--[p65]-- The values of $r _ { x }$ and $r _ { y }$ can be obtained through the imaging principle, where the light is reflected by the sample, as shown in Fig. 3.6. The reflected light goes through $L _ { o }$ and DCLG, before arriving at the FQD. $a _ { o }$ and $b _ { o }$ are the object and image distances of $L _ { o }$ , respectively. $a _ { x }$ and $b _ { x }$ are the object and image distances of $\mathrm { C L } _ { x } ,$ respectively.

===== [p84-86] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.5 Arbitrary Pattern Generation in the $( r , \theta )$ Polar Coordinate System (8块) =====
--[p84]-- The data of the four quadrants is then stored in sequence by a compression algorithm. The compression algorithm is used to reduce the data volume. One traverses the data and uses a pixel value and the number of the same value to replace the data where the adjacent pixel values are the same. The images of Fig. 3.25b, c are in Cartesian coordinate and polar coordinate, respectively.
--[p84]-- The position coordinates of pixels of the bitmap in the Cartesian coordinate x; y system is then converted into polar coordinates $( r , \theta )$ by using a set of algorithm, as shown in Fig. 3.25 [6]. In the Cartesian coordinate, the image is arrayed into a matrix where the data of each row are stored in sequence. However, in the polar coordinate, the data of the same radius should be stored in an array. Therefore, the data are realigned with the formulas, as shown in Fig. 3.25a.
--[p85]-- 3.26 [6], which mainly contains three steps.
(1) The original data in the Cartesian coordinate $( x , y )$ are converted into data in the polar coordinate $( r , \theta )$ in the computer, and then the converted data are transferred to the center controller unit with the TCP/IP protocol. In the meantime, the parameters of radial (r) and angular (h) speeds are transferred to the motor controller unit from the computer with the TCP/IP protocol.
--[p86]-- (2) The motor controller unit sends data to the motor driver to set the specified speeds of the linear and rotary motors and translate the signals sent by linear and rotary encoders, which consist of the current positions of the radial (r) and angular (h) speeds, respectively. Then, the data of the current positions are transferred to the central controller every 10 ns.
--[p86]-- (3) The central controller receives the data of the radial (r) and angular (h) positions from the motor controller unit and the converted data from the computer. The central controller calculates and matches the current positions and the converted data precisely. When the motors move to specified positions, the central controller sends the corresponding data to the laser diode device.
--[p84]-- The arbitrary pattern is written in the format of spiral scanning, in which the writing spot moves continuously from the inner circles to the outer circles in the radial direction, while the sample stage rotates in the angular (h) direction. The data in the polar coordinate correspond to the specified position of the radial (r) and angular (h) directions precisely.
The flowchart of the laser writing arbitrary patterns in the polar coordinate system is shown in Fig.
--[p84]-- First, one sets the coordinate of the center of the circle, the range of the radius that is to be written, and the resolution in (r) direction and (h) direction. Next, one calculates the Cartesian coordinates of each pixel with the known polar coordinates. In order to improve the efficiency, one only needs to calculate the first quadrant because of the symmetry of the four quadrants, the data of the other quadrants can be obtained easily.
--[p84]-- 3.5 Arbitrary Pattern Generation in the $( r , \theta )$ Polar Coordinate System
Generally, the pattern data format is established with x; y -type Cartesian coordinate system. In order to write the arbitrary patterns through the $( r , \theta )$ -type polar coordinate system, the pattern data are first translated into bitmap.

===== [p143-144] Chapter 6 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography Using Organic Thin Films > 6.4.1 Focused-Spot-Induced Local Post-exposure Baking (6块) =====
--[p143]-- Compared with standard FEB, the FL-PEB can result in fine and uniform structures, which is because the structures fabricated by a PEB process using a hot plate are determined by the spot intensity profile, while in FL-FEB, only the material within the effective temperature region is properly photopolymerized.
The calculated results on local peak temperature at different focusing depth with different laser powers are given in Fig.
--[p143]-- Figure 6.13 gives the calculated results. Figure 6.13a is a standard PEB, where a hot plate is used to heat and bake the post-exposure samples, and the temperature is about $9 5 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . One can see that there are large-size structures and accumulation effect. Figure 6.13b is FL-FEB, where heating source is from 532 nm wavelength continuous laser.
--[p144]-- 6.14a, where the inset is the evolution of temperature as a function of laser spot heating time at $P = 5$ mW. The dependence of depth on temperature shows that FL-PEB can fabricate uniform deep sub-wavelength 3-dimension structures. Figure 6.14b presents the experimental results, where SU8 resist is first spin-coated on a substrate. The samples are then soft-baked on a hot plate to remove residual solvent.
--[p143]-- 6.4.1 Focused-Spot-Induced Local Post-exposure Baking
SU8 is a kind of a common light-mode negative and epoxy-based photoresist material, and contains a photoacid generator, which generates a strong acid during light exposure. After exposure, post-exposure bake (PEB) process is required to conduct thermal crosslinking reaction. The photoacid acts as a catalyst in the thermal crosslinking reaction. The PEB can be processed through a focused laser spot heating.
--[p143]-- That is, the thermal crosslinking process of the photopolymerization of SU8 can be locally completed through a heat assistant from tightly focused laser spot heating due to light absorption of SU resist materials, which can be called as focused-spot-induced local PEB (FL-PEB). The FL-PEB can be carried out during light-mode exposure. The photopolymerized structures can be only obtained when the laser energy of focused laser spot exceeds certain threshold value.
--[p144]-- After exposure, for standard PEB, the sample is post-baked using hot plate; for FL-FEB, a 532 nm wavelength laser beam is tightly focused and then scans the sample. Comparison of the sizes of pillars fabricated with FL-PEB and standard PEB indicates that the pillar size with FL-FEB becomes smaller than that with standard FEB and the FL-FEB can overcome the accumulation effect. Figure 6.14c is woodpile structures fabricated through FL-FEB.

===== [p137-138] Chapter 6 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography Using Organic Thin Films > 6.2.5 Physical Picture of Patterning Process (7块) =====
--[p137]-- 6.2.5 Physical Picture of Patterning Process
At the irradiation and heating of writing spot, the photothermoelastic expansion occurs when $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ is heated to above glass transition temperature $T _ { g }$ of $2 2 4 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ . If the heated portion of $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ doesn’t be constrained by surrounding medium, it would expand freely in all directions.
--[p137]-- However, the surrounding medium suppresses the expansion in the radial direction, producing radial compressive stresses to the heated portion, as shown in Fig. 6.6a. There are generally two contributions to the expansion. One is the linear thermal expansion along the z-axis (optical axis/ thin-film thickness direction), the other is the Poisson effect because of the radial compressive stresses [13].
--[p137]-- However, the $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ materials may be similar to chalcogenide glass, where the photothermal expansion is a plastic deformation, resulting from the photofluidity during laser irradiation [13–15]. The initial thermoelastic deformation would be converted into plastic deformation via viscous flow due to photofluidity.
--[p138]-- The nanobump is accordingly gasified, and then transformed into nanohole structure, as shown in Fig. 6.6b, where the nanohole structure are called as concave-type patterns. That is, either the convex or concave reliefs can be formed by one-step process for the $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ thin films when one finely tunes the laser irradiation energy, and the pattern feature size can be reduced to nanoscale.
--[p137]-- The free surface expansion can generate bump structure, and the bump size can be localized to nanoscale due to the nonlinear absorption and glass transition threshold effects. The bump size at $T = T _ { g }$ can be controlled at about $0 . 2 w _ { 0 }$ if one finely tunes the writing laser energy close to the glass transition threshold, as shown in Fig. 6.5b. The free surface expansion is generally thermal elastic deformation, which would disappear when the laser is off.
--[p137]-- The Poisson effect causes the materials to flow toward the free surface ${ ( \mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 } / \mathrm { a i r } }$ interface) because the flow toward the substrate is constrained due to the rigid interface between the $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ and substrate. The fluidity occurs only when the temperature rise exceeds the $T _ { g }$ , that is, there is threshold effect for free surface expansion.
--[p137]-- Therefore, one can directly achieve nanopatterns at visible light, and the nanobump relief patterns can be formed at the surface only at $T > T _ { \mathrm { g l a s s } }$ , where the bump (convex) reliefs are called as convex-type patterns.
However, one increases the irradiation energy, which causes $T > T _ { \mathrm { g a s } }$ . The thermal gasification effect occurs. The patterning mechanism can be also transferred from thermal deformation to thermal gasification.

===== [p193-193] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.5.1 Surface Oxidation (4块) =====
--[p193]-- 8.5.1 Surface Oxidation
Surface oxidation of metal thin films is another method to achieve grayscale image lithography. The detailed grayscale mechanism is ascribed to the coexistence of the opaque metal and the corresponding transparent metallic oxides. For example, the Sn thin films can be transformed into $\mathrm { S n O } _ { x }$ through laser-induced surface oxidization due to laser irradiation.
--[p193]-- The thickness of $\mathrm { S n O } _ { x }$ layer can be controlled through changing the laser energy, compared with Sn metal, the $\mathrm { S n O } _ { x }$ is transparent, and thus one can conduct the grayscale lithography through tuning the laser irradiation energy. Figure 8.15 displays the grayscale images written on Sn thin films with a thickness of 12 nm. Figure 8.15a is the two-level grayscale and Fig. 8.15b–d shows continuous-tone grayscale patterns.
--[p193]-- Figure 8.15d displays a set of optical images with discrete gray levels, where the brighter areas are exposed by higher laser power.
--[p193]-- The grayscale is realized by adjusting writing power where a high power yields a high transmittance and low reflectance. Each pixel of the image is assigned to a laser power to obtain certain transmittance or reflectance. Figure 8.15c is a lifelike wolf in the snowfall with fine structures like furs and whiskers. Besides, the In (chemical element) thin films can also be used as grayscale lithography materials through oxidation effect, grayscale images are fabricated on the In thin films [11].

===== [p32-35] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.3.2 S/Se-Based Chalcogenide Thin Films (19块) =====
--[p34]-- In other words, the selective etching between the as-deposited and exposed ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \bf S } _ { 3 }$ thin films (and other As-based chalcogenides) in aqueous alkaline solutions is based on the different dissolution rates of ${ \bf A } { \bf s } { \bf S } _ { 3 }$ pyramids, fragments with S–S homopolar bonds, and ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 4 } { \mathbf { S } } _ { 4 }$ structural units with As–As homopolar bonds.

--[p32]-- 1.3.2 S/Se-Based Chalcogenide Thin Films
In addition to organic polymer light-mode resists, S/Se-based chalcogenide thin films, such as $_ { \mathrm { A s } _ { 2 } ( \mathrm { S } , \mathrm { S e } ) _ { 3 } }$ and ${ \mathrm { G e } } ( \mathsf { S } , \mathsf { S e } ) _ { 2 }$ , can also be used as light-mode lithography [37]. Compared to the organic polymer light-mode resists, chalcogenide thin films have smaller structural units, i.e., at the atomic scale.
--[p33]-- ( \mathrm { A s } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S } _ { 3 } ) _ { \mathrm { l i q } } \stackrel { \mathrm { T h e r m a l d i s s o c i a t i o n } } { \longrightarrow } ( \mathrm { A s } _ { 4 } \mathrm { S } _ { 4 } ) _ { \mathrm { v a p } } + ( \mathrm { S } _ { n } ) _ { \mathrm { v a p } }
$$
The nonstoichiometric $\mathrm { \ A s _ { 4 } S _ { 4 } }$ unit contains an $\mathbf { A } \mathbf { s } { - } \mathbf { A } \mathbf { s }$ homopolar bond.
--[p33]-- Compared to the structures of bulk ${ \bf A s } _ { 2 } { \bf S } _ { 3 }$ , as-evaporated ${ \bf A } { \bf s } _ { 2 } { \bf S } _ { 3 }$ thin films, and exposed ${ \bf A } { \bf s } _ { 2 } { \bf S } _ { 3 }$ thin films, where each $^ { \mathfrak { s } } S ^ { \mathfrak { s } }$ element must be corner-shared between two neighboring ${ \bf A s S } _ { 3 }$ pyramids.
--[p34]-- The chemical etching reaction of as-deposited and light-exposed thin films have different kinetics, which results in selective positive etching. Additionally, the arsenic photo-oxidation on the surface of the thin film is another factor that influences selective etching.
--[p35]-- When one increases the etching time of the $\mathrm { A s } _ { 4 0 } \mathrm { S } _ { 3 0 } \mathrm { S e } _ { 3 0 }$ layer, the protrusion structures can be obtained, as shown in Fig. 1.18b, where the protrusion diameter is also determined by the etching time. In addition, the relief height and depth depend mainly on the etching time of the $\mathrm { A s } _ { 4 } \mathrm { G e } _ { 3 0 } \mathrm { S } _ { 6 6 }$ layer. The latter layer varies within its initial thickness.
--[p33]-- Owing to the fast condensation process of vapors on the substrate (room temperature), these structural units are frozen in the as-deposited thin films, and result in the photosensitivity of these films. The concentration of homopolar bonds can be estimated from the XPS core-level spectra. As an example, the XPS data for S 2p and As 3d core-level spectra for ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \bf S } _ { 3 }$ thin films are presented in Fig.
--[p35]-- Figure 1.18 shows the periodic structures formed in an $\mathrm { A s _ { 4 0 } S _ { 3 0 } S e _ { 3 0 } - A s _ { 4 } G e _ { 3 0 } S _ { 6 6 } }$ two-layer light-mode resist, using double interference exposure and two-step etching. Using appropriate exposure and etching time, one can obtain a pattern with periodic holes, as shown in Fig. 1.18a, where the diameter of the holes can be tuned by changing the etching time.
--[p32]-- For example, ${ \mathrm { A s } } _ { 2 } { \bf S } _ { 3 }$ thin films can be used as positive light-mode resists in the $\mathrm { N a } _ { 2 } \mathrm { C O } _ { 3 } + \mathrm { N a } _ { 3 } \mathrm { P O } _ { 4 }$ aqueous solution, and ${ \bf A s } _ { 3 5 } { \bf S } _ { 6 5 }$ thin films usually behave as negative resists in nonaqueous amine-based solvents [38].
--[p33]-- The concentration of the homopolar bonds is one of the key factors in the structural transformation.
Concentration of homopolar bonds, which is high in the as-deposited thin films, can be reduced by light exposure, as shown in Fig. 1.16c. The structure of thin film after light exposure becomes close to the structure of bulk materials. Compared to Fig.
--[p33]-- It is demonstrated that bulk stoichiometric chalcogenides of $_ { \mathrm { A s } _ { 2 } ( \mathsf { S } , \mathsf { S e } ) _ { 3 } }$ and Ge $_ { ( \mathrm { S } , \mathrm { S e } ) _ { 2 } }$ consist of pyramidal $\mathrm { A s } ( \mathrm { S } , \mathrm { S e } ) _ { 3 / 2 }$ and tetrahedral $\mathrm { G e } ( \mathrm { S } , \mathrm { S e } ) _ { 4 / 2 }$ structural units, respectively. However, in principle, some homopolar bonds are possible, only owing to deviation from stoichiometry.
--[p33]-- The homopolar bonds should not exist in bulk materials of stoichiometric composition. However, as-deposited chalcogenide thin films, even with stoichiometric compositions, contain a considerable fraction of “erroneous” homopolar bonds, such as As–As, S–S, Se–Se, and Ge–Ge. The concentration of such “erroneous” homopolar bonds in as-evaporated films can be more than 10%, depending on the conditions of thermal evaporation.
--[p33]-- 1.17a, b, respectively, where regions I and II, respectively, determine the concentration of atoms involved in heteropolar and homopolar bonds. The changes in the chemical bonds result from structural transformation, and are caused by light exposure irradiation. The changes in the chemical bonds are thus dependent on the type and intensity of light exposure, chemical composition, and surface state of the thin films.
--[p32]-- Thus, the patterns on chalcogenide thin films theoretically possess smaller line edge roughness and higher resolution. Moreover, chalcogenide thin films have higher durability under reactive-ion etching and higher resistance to acids. Chalcogenide thin films can be used as positive or negative light-mode resists in certain conditions such as changing temperature or etching time.

===== [p39-41] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.2 Light-Mode Lithography (8块) =====
--[p40]-- 2.2, where the patterns are finally transferred to the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ layer. It should be pointed out that the prebaking is used to remove the residual solvent. The hard baking functions as stiffening the photoresist layer and removes residual solvent. The patterns are written through exposure, and the wet etching (developing) can transfer the patterns into patterning micro/nanostructures on the photoresist layer.
--[p41]-- This will lead to poor resolution and large line edge roughness (LER), as shown in Fig. 2.4b. In addition, organic photoresist is made of large molecular groups, which also leads to poor LER.
--[p39]-- The solvent provides mobility, has no influence on the chemical properties of photoresists, and is volatile when being heated. The resin is an insoluble polymer and functions as adhesives, and provides the mechanical and chemical properties of photoresists. The PAG can be called as photosensitizer, and chemical reaction happens when light irradiation. The additives are used to tune the chemical performances and optical responses of photoresists.
--[p40]-- It is worth mentioning that the chemical composition of exposed regions is completely different than that of non-exposed regions, as shown in Fig. 2.3a, b, that is, the exposure process is a photochemical reaction, and some new substances generate. Thus, the etching selectivity of patterns is very high in developing.
In the post-exposure baking process, the $\mathrm { H } ^ { + }$ ions generated by PAGs can also diffuse into non-exposed regions, as shown in Fig. 2.4a.
--[p40]-- The photoresist solution is spin-coated on the substrate. The $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ thin layer is beforehand deposited on the substrate through physical vapor deposition. The procedure of light-mode lithography is complex and consists of substrate cleaning, photoresist spin-coating, prebaking, exposure, post-exposure baking, developing, hard baking, dry etching, and residual photoresist removal, as shown in Fig.
--[p40]-- A king of soluble polymer resins are accordingly formed (Fig. 2.3b). However, the soluble polymer resins in exposed region are only a little, and thus the solubility of exposed regions is still poor. Therefore, post-exposure baking (PEB) is required, which can promote $\mathrm { H } ^ { + }$ ions to react with more inert polymer resins and form more soluble polymer resins (Fig. 2.3c). Thus, wet-etching selectivity in the developing is improved.
--[p39]-- 2.2 Light-Mode Lithography
Before the introduction of laser heat-mode lithography, the conventional light-mode lithography is first detailed. One needs to prepare the photoresist solutions before lithography. As shown in Fig. 2.1, the photoresist materials are dissolved in organic solvent to form photoresist solution. The photoresist solution consists of solvent, resins, photo-acid generator (PAG), and additives.
--[p40]-- The micro/ nanostructures are further transferred to the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ layer through dry etching.
In the process flow of optical lithography, the exposure and post-exposure baking are key and can be elucidated by the photochemical reaction, as shown in Fig. 2.3. In general, the photoresist thin film is made of photo-acid generators and inert polymer resins. After exposure, the photo-acid generators (PAGs) releases $\mathrm { H } ^ { + }$ which is reacted to inert polymer resins.

First, the pattern structures are written on the heat-mode resist thin films. ===== [p21-21] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (2) Focused Ion Beam Lithography (4块) =====
--[p21]-- (2) Focused Ion Beam Lithography
Compared to electrons, ions possess a larger mass. For example, a hydrogen ion is 1840 times heavier than an electron. The heavy mass of ions makes them particularly suitable for nanofabrication based on the direct structuring of the material rather than through the exposure of an organic resist. Focused ion beam (FIB) really became a nanofabrication tool following the introduction of liquid metal ion sources (LMISs) [17].
--[p21]-- At this extremely high electric field, metal atoms at the liquid’s apex become ionized and escape from the liquid metal surface in the form of field evaporation, thus resulting in ion emission. Although the total emission current may be only a few micro-amperes, the current density can be as high as $\sim 1 0 ^ { 6 } \mathrm { A c m } ^ { - 2 }$ because of the extremely small emission area (liquid apex). The LMIS makes it possible to focus the ion beam to less than 5 nm in beam size.
--[p21]-- Gallium is a common and good liquid metal material because of its low melting temperature $( 2 9 . 8 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ . An electrostatic force from a high voltage is exerted on the liquid metal, thus pulling the liquid metal into an extremely small apex. The electrical field strength at the liquid apex can be as high as $1 0 ^ { 1 0 } \mathrm { V ~ m } ^ { - 1 }$ .
--[p21]-- With this fine beam and its heavy mass, FIB can directly remove materials from the surface as well as deposit materials with the assistance of some chemical compound gases with extremely high precision. Focused ion beam constitutes a truly flexible and versatile nanofabrication tool [18].

===== [p149-149] Chapter 7 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Transparent Thin Films > 7.1 Introduction (3块) =====
--[p149]-- In this chapter, the ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ transparent thin films are used as an example, various micro/nanostructures are fabricated on the surface of ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ through different light absorption layers.
--[p149]-- However, it is difficult to directly fabricate the micro/ nano-structures through traditional laser writing lithography because the laser energy cannot be absorbed due to the very low absorption coefficient of transparent thin films in writing laser wavelength. Laser heat-mode lithography provides an effective way for fabricating the micro/nanostructures on the transparent thin films through the assistance of light absorption layers.
--[p149]-- 7.1 Introduction
Micro/nanostructure-based optical devices, such as metalens and metasurface, are increasingly important in the optic/electronic fields. The micro/nanostructures are directly fabricated on the surface of transparent thin films or substrates through lithography methods.

===== [p147-147] Chapter 6 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography Using Organic Thin Films > 6.5 Summary (1块) =====
--[p147]-- 6.5 Summary
The organic thin films have some advantages in laser heat-mode lithography. The pattern structures can be directly written on the organic thin films without developing. The pattern structures can be formed through different physical or chemical effects, such as thermal gasification, thermal crosslinking, and thermal deformation. The positive resist can transfer to negative resist through tuning the laser power, and laser power is only about milliwatts.
Compared with inorganic heat-mode resists, the optical transparency is good, can be directly used as micro/nanostructured optical elements.

===== [p31-32] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (2) EUV Light-Mode Resist (5块) =====
--[p32]-- The absorption coefficient of the backbone polymers used for KrF and ArF resists is approximately $4 ~ { \mu \mathrm { m } } ^ { - 1 }$ in the case of EUV, and the transparency of resists with a thickness of 100 nm is 67% [33].
The quantum efficiency of light-mode resist is one of the important technical parameters. The quantum efficiency of EUV resists significantly depends on the acid generator concentration.
--[p32]-- The quantum efficiency of EUV resists can be improved by increasing the concentration of acid generators [34]. EUV resists also need a proton source for acid generation. Thus, increasing the quantum efficiency through the addition of proton source is also one of important strategies [35]. In addition, compared to blend-type resists, the chemically amplified resists with anion-bound acid generators yield both high resolution and high sensitivity [36].
The anion-bound resists can be applied to the lithography of the 16 nm node or less.
--[p32]-- In the early stage of development, the KrF resist platform was preferred because of the etching resistance of aromatic compounds. Currently, the mainstream of resist development is based on both KrF and ArF resist platforms. Poly(4-hydroxystyrene) (PHS) and styrene-derived copolymers have been used as backbone polymers for KrF resists. Acrylate copolymers have been used as ArF resists [32]. A schematic drawing of the molecular structures is shown in Fig. 1.15.
--[p31]-- (2) EUV Light-Mode Resist
Further reduction of the feature size of IC devices results in the appearance of EUV (\*13.6 nm wavelength) light-mode resist. EUV light-mode resist is a chemical amplifier resist and mainly contains polymer resin and acid generator [5]. The difference between EUV and DUV light-mode resists is the sensitization mechanisms of acid generators [30].
--[p31]-- In the DUV light-mode resists, the polymer absorption generally interferes with acid generation, although some acids are generated through the so-called polymer sensitization (the energy or electron transfer from excited polymers to acid generators) [31].
From the viewpoint of sidewall degradation, both KrF and ArF resist platforms have been considered to be applicable to EUV resists.

===== [p82-83] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.4.4 Sample Movement Fluctuation Error Measurement (7块) =====
--[p83]-- Figure 3.24 presents the experimental results of movement fluctuation error [5], where the sampling frequency is 100 Hz, the movement speed of the stepping motorized stage is 0.1 mm/s, and the total measured area is $1 0 \mathrm { m m } \ \times 9 . 4 \mathrm { m m } .$ Figure 3.24a shows the results of mapping profile of movement flatness error onto x–y plane.
--[p82]-- 3.4.4 Sample Movement Fluctuation Error Measurement
The sample movement flatness error was measured under different movement speeds. A smooth flat mirror was used as sample, and placed onto the stepping motorized stage, where the flat mirror could reflect the laser beam into the PD and FQD, the movement distance was 10 mm and the sampling frequency was 100 Hz.
The results are shown in Fig. 3.23 [5].
--[p83]-- Then the y-axis stepping motorized stage moved forward a small step $\Delta y$ of 100 lm, the x-axis stepping motorized stage moved along x-axis direction from 10 to 0 mm, again. After repeatable measurements along x-axis from 0 to 10 mm at different y-axis positions, the movement fluctuation error at any (x, y) position was measured. Finally, the profile of movement flatness error onto x–y plane was constructed and mapped.
--[p82]-- Figure 3.23a shows that the movement fluctuation errors of the x-direction, where the x-axis stepping motorized stage moves back and forth. One can see that the results are similar to different motorized stage movement speeds. Figure 3.23b shows the difference between maximum and minimum of movement fluctuation error under different movement speeds. One can see that the difference ranges from 0.45 to 0.75 lm, which is in the linear range of 12 lm marked in Fig. 3.21.
--[p83]-- Figure 3.24b shows the difference between maximum and minimum of movement flatness error of x-direction at different y-direction movement distance, one can see that the difference is within 1.3 lm, which is a reasonable value. It should be noted that the movement fluctuation error is unavoidable due to imperfect machining and peristaltic crawling effect in movement. However, the movement fluctuation error can be reduced as much as possible if advanced polishing and air-bearing motor are used.
--[p82]-- Here it needs to be pointed out that the sample movement fluctuation error includes two aspects, one is from sample surface itself, the other is from movement error of stepping motorized stage, which is related to the movement speed. The difference decreases as the movement speed increases, which coincides with the general mechanical motion theory, where the low-speed movement causes crawling effect, thus there is a large difference of movement flatness error.
--[p82]-- With the increase of movement speed, the crawling effect gradually disappears, thus, the difference decreases, accordingly.
The two-dimensional movement flatness error was also measured through x– y stepping motorized stages with a fixed movement speed. Similar to Fig. 3.23. In the measurement, the x-axis stepping motorized stage first moved along x-axis direction from 0 to 10 mm at fixed y-axis position.

When the laser spot irradiates onto the chalcogenide resists, the absorbed photons excite electrons and holes. When a part of the photo-excited electrons and holes recombine non-radiatively, the photon energy is transferred into heat, which enables the structural change. The AIST resists are found to have large nonlinear absorption coefficient of $\beta \sim - 4 .

===== [p131-132] Chapter 6 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography Using Organic Thin Films > 6.1 Introduction (8块) =====
--[p132]-- (1) Appropriate absorption coefficient at the writing laser wavelength, which can cause the patterning at several milliwatt laser power.
(2) Photothermal localization response to writing spot, which can cause the pattern feature size to be reduced to nanoscale.
(3) The obvious threshold characteristics of photothermal effect such as thermal gasification, thermal decomposition, or thermal deformation.
--[p131]-- The spot intensity profile can be written as $I ( r ) = I _ { 0 } \mathrm { e x p } ( - 2 r ^ { 2 } / w _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } )$ , where r is radial coordinate, and $w _ { 0 }$ is spot radius at $1 / e ^ { 2 }$ intensity. For laser writing lithography, the lithographic reaction originates from the light energy absorption. The light energy absorption can be estimated as $Q ( r ) = - \alpha I ( r )$ , where a is absorption coefficient.
--[p131]-- The organic thin films are quickly heated due to the irradiation of writing spot. The gasification reaction takes place when the central temperature of spot exceeds to the gasification threshold point of organic thin films. The pattern structures form and the feature size is far smaller than the writing spot itself, accordingly.
In order to directly produce nanoscale pattern structures on the organic thin films, the organic thin films are required to have the basic features as follows:
--[p131]-- 6.1 Introduction
Generally, the laser heat-mode lithography materials are from inorganic chalcogenide phase-change thin films due to selective wet etching between crystalline and amorphous phases [1, 2].
--[p131]-- Actually, some organic thin films can be also used as heat-mode lithography materials [3–8], and the lithography is completed through a single-step process, where the pattern structures are directly produced through laser-induced thermal gasification, thermal decomposition, or thermal deformation, and no developing process is required.
--[p132]-- (4) The temperature of photothermal threshold effect is about $1 0 0 { - } 2 0 0 \ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } .$ , which can be obtained through several milliwatt laser power.
The patterning usually includes four types, including thermal decomposition, thermal gasification, thermal deformation, and thermal crosslinking effect.
--[p131]-- Compared with the inorganic chalcogenide phase-change thin films, the organic thin films have the following advantages: (1) low thermal diffusion coefficient, which can prevent the pattern feature size from becoming large due to thermal diffusion; (2) laser-induced gasification, deformation, or decomposition response to writing spot, which can directly cause patterning without wet etching and developing processes.
--[p131]-- Figure 6.1 gives the schematics of laser heat-mode lithography on the organic thin films through the photothermal localization effect, where the lithographic reaction is localized at a very small region. A focused spot with Gaussian intensity profile irradiates onto the organic thin films.

===== [p13-13] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.1 Introduction (2块) =====
--[p13]-- 1.1 Introduction
Lithography is one of the critical processes used for the fabrication of microelectronic chips and micro/nanostructure-based electro-optical devices. The pattern structures are usually fabricated on the resist thin films and then transferred to the silicon or fused quartz substrates through the exposure and etching techniques.
--[p13]-- In the current lithographic methods, the exposure is generally based on a photochemical reaction after the resist thin film absorbs the light energy, which is referred to as light-mode lithography. The resists are known as light-mode resist materials. There is a variety of lithography methods for complying with different requirements, including template-based, vacuum-based, and probe-based methods.

===== [p176-176] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography (1块) =====
--[p176]-- Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography

===== [p45-46] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.3.2 Non-diffraction-Limited Lithography (7块) =====
--[p46]-- After exposure, the developing is carried out by a conventional tetramethylammonium hydroxide (TMAH) solution, which dissolves part of the resist where it is exposed, that is, the transition metal oxide thin film acts as a positive-type resist. The pit size is about 130 nm, which is only about 1/4 the spot size.
--[p45]-- All light levels including side lobes contribute to the exposure, which may cause the line edge roughness of the photoresist pattern to become worse. The lithography area is approximately to spot size. That is, the feature size of lithography patterns is restricted by the spot size, which is determined by the optical diffraction limit.
--[p46]-- However, in the laser heat-mode lithography, only some light above threshold contributes to the exposure, the lithography area can be far smaller than the spot itself due to the thermally induced phase-change threshold characteristics of heat-mode resist. Only the central area is thermally exposed and forms the pattern structure, whose feature size is obviously smaller than the spot, and is not restricted by the optical diffraction limit. As shown in Fig.
--[p45]-- 2.3.2 Non-diffraction-Limited Lithography
In laser heat-mode lithography, the initial state of heat-mode resists is usually amorphous. Increasing the temperature above the phase-change threshold point, the heat-mode resist experiences a structural change from amorphous state to crystalline state, as shown in Fig. 2.10a, where a typical differential scanning calorimetric analysis is schematically presented, an exothermic process is accompanied with the structural change.
--[p46]-- Figure 2.11b is a microscopic image of read-only optical memory with a capacity of 25 GB obtained using the laser heat-mode lithography, where the transition metal oxides are used as the heat-mode resists.
--[p46]-- 2.10b, the heat-mode lithography area is smaller than the light-mode lithography area.
Figure 2.11 gives typical experimental results. In Fig. 2.11a, the pit patterns are obtained on a laser heat-mode resist of transition metal oxide thin film. The spot size is about 0.5 lm, the exposure speed is 6 m/s, and laser wavelength is 405 nm.
--[p45]-- In laser heat-mode lithography, the heat-mode resist can be heated through a focused laser spot. Generally speaking, the intensity of focused spot presents Gaussian profile, the central intensity of spot is the highest, and gradually reduces along radial direction, as shown in Fig. 2.10b. The light-mode lithography is performed through photonic energy to dissociate the organic photoresist layer.

--[p102]-- The marks are molten-ablation hole structures. The mark size is about 100 nm, and the mark depth is also about 100 nm. The size of molten-ablation marks is reduced using the upper thermal conduction layer.
--[p101]-- The reduction of heat-spot size may be because the Si layer, possessing higher thermal conductivity, can efficiently promote out-of-plane thermal diffusion and minimize the in-plane thermal diffusion. Moreover, the larger the Si layer thickness, the more the heat quantity is taken away from AIST layer.
--[p100]-- 4.4.2 Upper Layer
The Si layer can be also deposited onto the AIST thin film and used as an upper thermal conduction layer. Figure 4.8a shows the temperature profiles with an upper Si layer at different laser powers. The inset is a schematic of the sample structure. The thicknesses of the AIST and upper Si layers are 50 nm and 40 nm, respectively. The writing speed is 4 m/s. One can see that the peak temperature can reach the threshold temperature of AIST when no Si layer is added.
--[p100]-- However, the addition of an upper Si layer causes the peak temperature to become lower than the threshold temperature of AIST. This cannot result in the formation of micro/nanopatterns. Fortunately, one can increase the peak temperature by increasing laser power. When the laser power is more than 1.8 mW, the peak temperature can reach the threshold temperature and the micro/nanopatterns can be formed, accordingly.
The normalized temperature profiles are presented in Fig. 4.8b.
--[p101]-- A magnified image of Fig. 4.9a is presented in Fig. 4.9b. The heat-mode lithography marks are hole structures, and the mark size is about

--[p100]-- Compared with that without a Si layer, the heat-spot size of AIST thin film can be reduced from 456 to 400 nm with the addition of an upper Si layer. The heat-spot size can be further reduced to 360 nm as the laser power increases to 2.2 mW. That is, the addition of upper Si layer is an efficient method to reduce heat-spot size.
--[p101]-- 4.8c, the upper Si layer can take away the heat, the in-plane thermal diffusion can be minimized, and heat-spot size can be reduced.

However, as shown in
Fig.
--[p100]-- In addition, it can be considered that with the increase of laser power, the temperature difference with an upper Si layer becomes steep, as the air flow exists above the upper Si layer. Thereby, the cooling rate of the upper Si layer increases with the increment of laser power. This promotes out-of-plane thermal diffusion of AIST thin film and prevents the increase of heat-spot size. Therefore, increasing laser power can further reduce heat-spot size when the upper Si layer is added.

===== [p50-52] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.3.5 Convertible Characteristics Between Positive and Negative Resists (13块) =====
--[p51]-- The electrons react with the electrophilic c-GSBT much more easily than the nucleophilic a-GSBT. In the alkaline solution, the c-GBST, whose electrons are paired, has more valance orbits than the a-GBST. The unoccupied orbits are attractive for the $\mathrm { O H } ^ { - }$ group since OH<sup>−</sup> has the lone-pair elections, which results in the larger etching rate of c-GBST in alkaline environment [23]. The reaction rate of c-GSBT is faster than that of the a-GSBT.
--[p50]-- All of the elements have an octet of valence electrons, and the configurations are called as Lewis structures. The a-GSBT is nucleophilic because the lone-pair electrons can provide electrons easily in a chemical reaction. In c-GSBT, there are the resonance bonds between Ge (or Sb, Bi) and Te, and the number of bonding electrons is less than twice the number of the bonds [22], as shown in Fig. 2.17b, where the configuration is called the resonance structure.
--[p50]-- The resonance structure is a body of electron deficiency, which results that c-GSBT is electrophilic. Thus, c-GSBT has electro-affinity and receives electrons easily in a chemical reaction.
The c-GSBT is electrophilic and can react with Lewis base. It is well known that the reaction happens when KOH is mixed with $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ [22],
$$
--[p51]-- The reasons may be that the a-GBST has more unpaired electrons or dangling bonds than the c-GBST. In the acid solution, the unpaired electrons tend to bond to $\mathrm { H } ^ { + }$ to lower its energy, hence $\mathbf { a - G B S T }$ with more dangling bonds is etched off easily. That is, in the Lewis acid $\mathrm { ( H N O _ { 3 } + H _ { 2 } O _ { 2 } ) }$ solution, the GSBT materials function as the negative photoresist.
--[p50]-- 2.3.5 Convertible Characteristics Between Positive and Negative Resists
The heat-mode resists can be used as both positive and negative resists. That is, there are convertible characteristics between positive and negative resists for heat-mode resist materials. Here, the GeSbBiTe (GSBT) thin film is used as an example, the convertible characteristics between positive and negative resists are analyzed.
--[p50]-- The GSBT thin film, deposited by magnetron-controlled sputtering method, is usually amorphous state. The amorphous GeSbBiTe (a-GSBT) thin film can be thermally induced into crystalline state (c-GSBT) by laser heating.
In a-GSBT, there are two type of configurations, as is shown in Fig. 2.17a. One is polar covalent bond, including Te–Bi bonds and Te–Sb bonds and Te–Ge bonds; the other is lone-pair electrons, each Te atom has two non-bonding 5p lone-pair electrons.
--[p51]-- \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }  \mathrm { H O } _ { 2 } ^ { - } + \mathrm { H } ^ { + } , \mathrm { O H } ^ { - } + \mathrm { H O } _ { 2 } ^ { - }  \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } + \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } + 2 e _ { \mathrm { l } }\tag{ð<sup>2:1</sup>Þ}
$$
Reaction (2.1) indicates that $\mathrm { K O H } { + } \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ solution is an electron donor, can be considered as a Lewis base. There are lots of electrons in the solution.
--[p52]-- Figure 2.18 gives the experimental results of laser heat-mode lithography. The a-GSBT thin films are deposited on the substrates. The laser beam irradiates onto the a-GSBT thin films, the a-GSBT thin films absorb the laser energy and are heated to crystallization temperature. The phase-change happens from amorphous state to crystalline state, as is shown in Fig.
--[p51]-- \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 } + 2 \mathrm { H } ^ { + } + 2 e  2 \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O }\tag{ð<sup>2:2</sup>Þ}
$$
Reaction (2.2) indicates that $\mathrm { H N O } _ { 3 } { + } \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ solution is an electron acceptor, can be considered as a Lewis acid. The $\mathrm { H N O } _ { 3 } { + } \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ solution reacts with the nucleophilic a-GSBT much more easily than the electrophilic c-GSBT.
--[p51]-- Thus, c-GSBT is easily etched off, while a-GSBT can be remained. That is, in the Lewis base $\mathrm { ( K O H + H _ { 2 } O _ { 2 } ) }$ solution, the GSBT materials function as the positive photoresist.
The a-GSBT is nucleophilic and can react with Lewis acid (an electron acceptor). It is well known that the reaction happens when ${ \mathrm { H N O } } _ { 3 }$ is mixed with $\mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ [22],
$$
--[p52]-- 2.18b, where the GSBT thin film acts as positive photoresist. When the GSBT sample is developed in the $\mathrm { H N O } _ { 3 } { + } \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ solution, the amorphous regions are completely etched off, and the laser-induced crystallization regions are retained. As shown in Fig. 2.18c, the uniform lands are formed, and the GSBT thin film acts as negative photoresist.
--[p52]-- 2.18a, where the laser-irradiated regions (crystallization regions) are 2.5 nm lower than these regions without laser irradiation (amorphous regions) because the density of crystalline state is higher than that of the amorphous state. When the GSBT sample is developed in the $\mathrm { K O H } { + } \mathrm { H } _ { 2 } \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ solution, the laser-induced crystallization regions are completely etched off, and the uniform grooves are generated, as is shown in Fig.
--[p52]-- Thus, GSBT materials can act as both positive and negative resists depending on the different developing solutions, and the difference of chemical bonds between the a-GSBT and c-GSBT is responsible for the positive and negative resists. These are advantages over the traditional organic resists.

===== [p103-104] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.4.3 Both Lower and Upper Layers (12块) =====
--[p103]-- Figure 4.12 shows the optimized experimental results, where Si of 100 nm thickness and Si of 20 nm thickness are used as lower and upper thermal conduction layers, respectively. The reduction amplitude is as high as 7%. Thus, the addition of both upper and lower Si thin films can further decrease the heat-spot size.
--[p103]-- Uniform marks are formed on the surface of “glass/Si (100 nm)/heat-mode resist layer (100 nm)/Si (20 nm)” sample. --[p103]-- This means that the in-plane thermal diffusion can be completely restrained when both lower Si layer with 40 nm thickness and upper Si layer with 40 nm are adopted.
In order to experimentally confirm that both lower and upper Si layers can reduce the feature size of patterns, one further prepared “glass substrate/Si (100 nm)/heat-mode resist layer (100 nm)/Si (20 nm)” sample. Nanoscale marks were fabricated by direct laser writing.

Further increasing the thickness from 3 nm to 40 nm, decreases the heat-spot size gradually, and the minimum heat-spot size can be as low as 352 nm, which is very close to the laser-spot size (350 nm).
--[p104]-- One can see that uniform line patterns on both samples are observed, and the linewidth on the “AIST/Al/glass substrate” sample is 288 nm, which is smaller than that on the “AIST/glass substrate” sample, where the linewidth is about 363 nm. That is, the resolution of the patterns on the sample with an Al thermal conduction layer is enhanced to as high as \*21% compared with that on the sample without an Al thermal conduction layer.

The inset of Fig. 6.5b gives an illustration of photothermal localization responses to writing spot.
--[p135]-- 6.2.4 Theoretical Analysis of Photothermal Localization Response
In laser heat-mode lithography, the pattern formation originates from the light energy absorbed by the materials.
--[p136]-- For example, when the threshold temperature is finely tuned to about 95% the maximum temperature rise, the written dot size is reduced to about 0:20w .
--[p136]-- When the temperature rise of heating area exceeds certain transition threshold, the photothermal deformation may occur, and the photothermal deformation region can become further smaller than the heating area due to the threshold effect, as illustrated in red area of inset in Fig. 6.5b, where the written dot size can be reduced by changing the laser power or pulse width according to the threshold effect.
--[p136]-- The absorbed energy is transferred into temperature rise. The temperature rise profile can be roughly estimated as $\begin{array} { r } { \Delta T ( r ) = \frac { Q ( r ) } { 2 \rho C } t _ { p } } \end{array}$ [10], where the $\rho$ and $C$ are density and heat capacity, respectively. $t _ { p }$ is laser irradiation time. The density and heat capacity can be assumed to be $\rho \approx 1 . 2 0 \mathrm { g } / \mathrm { c m } ^ { 3 }$ and $C \approx 1 .
--[p136]-- When the writing spot irradiates onto the $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ thin films, a yellow heating area (energy absorption area) is formed, and the heating area size is smaller than the writing spot due to nonlinear absorption. The normalized temperature rise profile of heating (yellow) area corresponds to the black curve of Fig. 6.5b.
--[p135]-- For nonlinear absorption materials, the absorption coefficient is expressed as $\alpha ( r ) = \alpha _ { 0 } + \beta I ( r )$ . The profile of absorbed light energy intensity can be thus calculated as $Q ( r ) = [ \alpha _ { 0 } + \beta I ( r ) ] I ( r )$ . When the laser power $P = 2 . 2 5$ mW and other parameters are from $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ thin films, the normalized light energy intensity profile along the radial direction is shown in Fig.
--[p135]-- The absorbed light energy intensity can be estimated as $Q ( r ) = \alpha I ( r )$ , where $\mathscr { X }$ is absorption coefficient and $I ( r ) =$ $I _ { 0 } \mathrm { e x p } ( - 2 r ^ { 2 } / w _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } )$ is the intensity profile of writing spot with a radius of $w _ { 0 }$ at $1 / e ^ { 2 }$ normalized intensity. $I _ { 0 } = 2 P / \pi w _ { 0 } ^ { 2 }$ is laser intensity at $r = 0 ,$ , and $P$ is laser power.
--[p135]-- 6.5a, where the normalized light energy intensity profile of writing spot is also presented in order to exhibit the reduction of the energy absorption region compared with the writing spot. The full-width at half maximum (FWHM) of the light energy absorption region is reduced to about one-third of the writing spot. Thus, using the nonlinear absorption, the effective energy absorption region with sub-diffraction-limited feature size can be obtained.

--[p140]-- The organic heat-mode resist thin film absorbs the laser energy and is heated, and the central temperature on the laser spot is higher than other region due to the characteristics of Gaussian intensity profile of laser spot. The central gasification happens when the temperature at the central region of laser spot exceeds the gasification threshold point. The hole or groove structures are directly generated on the organic heat-mode resist thin films.

The pattern structures are finally formed on the substrate.

--[p140]-- 6.3 Patterning Through Direct Thermal Gasification
At the instant of exposure in laser heat-mode lithography, there is a large temporary temperature difference between the center and the surrounding area of the exposed area. Beyond a thermal gasification threshold temperature, the heat-mode resist material is removed through vaporization process, and the pattern structures are directly formed without developing, accordingly.
--[p140]-- (1) A clear gasification threshold and without melting below that threshold temperature. If the resist melts, the pattern structures on the resist will widen, and the pattern resolution will worsen. Thus, one needs to design the materials that undergo endothermic gasification so that the material surrounding the gasified portion solidifies as the pattern is created.
--[p141]-- 6.11b, one can see that there is a gasification threshold of about 530 K.
The organic heat-mode resists of anionic conjugated compounds can be dissolved into fluoro alcohol solution. The resist thin films can be coated on the substrate through spin-coating method, correspondingly. The small pattern structures are formed through the process described below.
--[p141]-- (2) A higher gasification temperature, which can raise the required central region temperature of laser spot at the time of structure formation and achieve a sharp peak temperature profile.
(3) The resist layer itself can be used as an optical component, which can increase the potential applications of the resist through substrate etching.
(4) The resist material needs to have a low etching rate to etching gas.
--[p142]-- The dot structures on the heat-mode resist layer can be transferred to Si substrate through dry etching, and create moth-eye structures, as shown in Fig. 6.12b. The dry etching gas is SF6. The etching ratio of the Si to the resist is 2.5.

===== [p39-39] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.1 Introduction (3块) =====
--[p39]-- 2.1 Introduction
Photolithography is actually the interaction of the light with photoresist. It is well known that light is not only information carrier, but also energy carrier. The lithography transfers both information and energy.
--[p39]-- The feature size of optical lithography may be not restricted by optical diffraction limit, and the trans-scale lithography from micrometer to nanometer can be achieved through making use of the photolithographic characteristics of information and energy carriers.
--[p39]-- On one hand, the light can transfer information into photoresists to form pattern structures based on the function of information carrier of light; on the other hand, the feature size of pattern structures can be reduced based on the function of energy carrier of light.

===== [p194-195] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.5.2 Grain Refinement (7块) =====
--[p194]-- 8.5.2 Grain Refinement
Laser heating can induce the grain refinement, which can reduce the light scattering and change the optical reflectivity and transmittance. Tellurium (Te) thin films are usually crystalline state through magnetron-controlled sputtering deposition method, and the grains can be further refined through laser irradiation. Figure 8.16 is the analysis of the grain refinement.
--[p194]-- Figure 8.16c is the micro-Raman spectra, two obvious Raman peaks at $1 2 7 ~ \mathrm { c m } ^ { - 1 }$ and $1 4 5 ~ \mathrm { { c m } ^ { - 1 } }$ occurs in unirradiated region, which corresponds to two vibration mode $( \mathsf { A } _ { 1 }$ and $\mathrm { { E } _ { T O } }$ mode) of Te–Te bond, respectively.
--[p194]-- Figure 8.16b is the comparison of micro-area reflection spectra, the reflection of laser-irradiated region is obvious larger than that of unirradiated region. The laser irradiation makes grains become smaller, and small grains rearrange closely and make the surface of Te thin-film smoother, resulting in lower light scattering and higher reflectivity.
--[p194]-- Figure 8.16d indicates the X-ray diffraction peaks, the peak position and shape of unirradiation region are the same as the irradiation region, corresponding to hexagonal Te nanocrystals. However, the diffraction peak intensity at the irradiation region is lower than that at the unirradiation region, which also indicates the reduction of grain size and low crystallinity of irradiation region.
--[p194]-- Figure 8.16a gives the AFM image comparison between unirradiated and laser-irradiated regions, which are marked as $^ { \mathfrak { s } } 1 ^ { \mathfrak { s } }$ and $" 2 "$ , respectively. The inset is the cross-sectional profile. Obviously, the surface of region $^ { \mathfrak { s } } 1 ^ { \mathfrak { s } }$ is smoother than that of region “2”.
--[p194]-- After laser irradiation, the intensity of two peaks has no significant changes while the peak positions both shift to low wavenumber region, at $1 2 1 ~ \mathrm { { c m } ^ { - 1 } }$ and $1 4 0 ~ \mathrm { { c m } ^ { - 1 } }$ , respectively. The Raman shift is closely related to grain size of the material. Small grain size leads to Raman shift to lower wavenumber.
--[p195]-- According to laser-induced grain refinement effect, one fabricates two-level grayscale images on Te thin films. Figure 8.17 displays the optical images of patterned micro-hexagonal stars, micro-snowflakes, micro-gears and the SEM patterns of micro-hexagonal stars. The insets give corresponding original pictures. The obtained images are clear and similar to original pictures.

===== [p81-82] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.4.3.2 Performance Analysis of the Measurement System (3块) =====
--[p81]-- In the experiment, the maximum response frequency of the actuator is 185 Hz.
Before measuring the sample movement fluctuation error, the accuracy of the system must be tested. A metal aluminum film sample with a step was used as a standard sample. The step was first measured by a high precision step profiler, which has a resolution of approximately 10 nm. The result is given in Fig. 3.22a, which shows a step height of $2 . 8 8 ~ \mu \mathrm { m }$ .
--[p82]-- The step was then measured by the system of Fig. 3.20, and Fig. 3.22b presents the experimental results [5]. The step height is 2.92 lm, which is close to the value measured by the step profiler. The discrepancy between the two results is within $0 . 1 ~ { \mu \mathrm { m } }$ , which indicates that the measurement system has an accuracy of less than ±100 nm.
--[p81]-- 3.4.3.2 Performance Analysis of the Measurement System
In order to check the correctness of the sample movement fluctuation error measurement system, the linear range of FES needs to be obtained. One can drive the actuator to move up and down at a frequency of 0.1 Hz, and simultaneously record the value of FES. The relation of FES with actuator value can be obtained, as shown in Fig. 3.21 [5]. The linear range of the FES curve corresponds to 12 lm of the actuator movement distance.

===== [p91-91] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.1 Introduction (3块) =====
--[p91]-- Therefore, one needs to manipulate the thermal diffusion channels and reduce the feature size of lithography, and realize super-resolution or even nanoscale lithographic linewidth.
--[p91]-- 4.1 Introduction
In laser heat-mode lithography, the resist thin films absorb the laser spot energy and are heated, and then experience a structural change when the temperature exceeds to certain threshold point, such as crystallization, melting, and gasification.
--[p91]-- Generally speaking, the heat will diffuse from high temperature to low temperature due to the temperature gradient, thus the heated region will be enlarged and extended from spot center to outer region due to the thermal diffusion effect of the resist thin films. The size of the structural change region becomes larger than spot itself, accordingly.
