===== [p190-190] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.4 Grayscale Lithography on $\mathbf { T e O } _ { x }$ Thin Films Through Structural Evolution (2块) =====
--[p190]-- 8.4 Grayscale Lithography on $\mathbf { T e O } _ { x }$ Thin Films Through Structural Evolution
$\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ thin films are actually mixtures of Te and $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ . Te and $\mathrm { T e O } _ { 2 }$ can experience structural evolution under the irradiation of laser beam spot.
--[p190]-- The structural evolution can cause the change of optical reflectivity; thus, the grayscale lithography can be achieved on the $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ thin films through the laser-induced structural evolution.

===== [p54-55] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.4.2 Multi-Ring Model (9块) =====
--[p55]-- According to the reflective spectra characterization of amorphous $\mathrm { A g I n T e } _ { 2 }$ and crystalline $\mathrm { G e } _ { 1 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 4 }$ , it is found that the reflective spectra of $\mathrm { A g I n T e } _ { 2 }$ are the same before and after phase transformation while the interference fringes, reflectance maxima and the difference in spacing of the reflectance minima of crystalline $\mathrm { G e } _ { 1 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 4 }$ is smaller than that of amorphous $\mathrm { G e } _ { 1 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 4 }$ . According to the measurement of dielectric constants, it is found that the dielectric constant of crystalline sample reduces 50–70% compared with amorphous phase.
--[p54]-- [26] proposed multi-ring model on large scale to explain fast phase-change mechanism, which is based on reverse Monte Carlo analysis with synchrotron irradiation X-ray diffraction data.
--[p55]-- The construction of odd-numbered rings induced by the formation of Ge–Ge homopolar bonds in amorphous GeTe disturbs the fast crystallization of the amorphous phase. It should be noticed that the addition of Sb atom to GeTe can effectively prevent the formation of Ge–Ge bonds (such as Ge–Sb–Te material). Multi-ring theory can better explain the vacancy role of fast phase-change of Ge–Sb–Te and the reason that Ge–Sb–Te has faster phase-change speed than GeTe.
--[p54]-- Figure 2.21 shows that in the amorphous-crystal phase-change process, amorphous $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ can transform to the crystal phase with only the transformation of the large-size even-numbered (8-, 10-, 12-fold) rings to NaCl-type structure (4-and 6-fold rings) with forming Ge (Sb)–Te bonds and without broken bonds.
--[p54]-- However, amorphous GeTe exhibits both odd- and even-numbered rings with different size due to the formation of Ge–Ge homopolar bonds. Therefore, the recombination of the different size rings plays an important role in breaking of Ge–
Ge homopolar bonds, and the formation of Ge–Te bonds is required accordingly. Thus, the odd-numbered rings can be transformed into even-numbered rings when the breaking of Ge–Ge bonds happens.
--[p55]-- It is suggested that the atom polarizability has evident difference before and after the crystallization. This divergence is due to the bonding difference of atoms in amorphous and crystalline states. In amorphous phase, the atoms are covalently bonded. The degree of electron localization is high, and the binding force is strong. In contrast, the atoms of crystalline phase are bonded by resonant bonding.
--[p55]-- A single, half-filled p-band simultaneously forms two unsaturated resonance bonds on the left and right sides as shown in Fig. 2.22. Therefore, the difference in bonds leads to large difference of dielectric constants.
--[p54]-- 2.4.2 Multi-Ring Model
Umbrella-flip model and the modified theory are all obtained by X-ray fine-structure spectra. This indicates that large-scale atom migration does not occur, but only local atom migration happans in the phase transformation of $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ material. In this case, Kohara et al.
--[p55]-- It is worth mentioning that the proposed umbrella-flip model and multi-ring theory can also be applied to other material systems such as AgInSbTe and so on due to the similarity of structures.
In laser heat-mode lithography, etching selectivity is an important factor to achieve micro/nanostructure fabrication. The etching selectivity between amorphous and crystalline phases can be ascribed to large structural difference.
So far, resonant bonding theory has been proposed to elucidate the difference between amorphous and crystalline states [19].

===== [p90-90] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.7 Summary (3块) =====
--[p90]-- In order to meet the performance specifications, the system is required to possess high-speed servo-tracking function, high-speed data processing and transferring, and operation reliability. High-speed rotation-type laser heat-mode lithography system is very suitable for fabrication of the micro/nanostructure-based circular-symmetric optical elements.
--[p90]-- In the meantime, the arbitrary pattern structures can be also rapidly fabricated through the data transferring software from Cartesian coordinate to polar coordinate system. The high-speed rotation-type laser heat-mode lithography system may enable the low cost and high-efficiency supply of functional micro/nanostructures and have excellent potential for different applications, such as pattern fabrication of nanophotonics devices.
--[p90]-- 3.7 Summary
High-speed rotation-type laser heat-mode lithography system generally uses GaN diode as an exposure source and a high NA objective lens as converging lens. The exposure spot size is about 0.5–1.0 lm. The exposure speed is at m/s or about 100 mm<sup>2</sup>/min @100 nm feature size. The sample size is more than 5 inch diameter.

===== [p195-195] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.6 Summary (1块) =====
--[p195]-- 8.6 Summary
The laser-induced thermally structural evolution and morphology change lead to the change of optical reflectivity in visible light wavelength, which is used as grayscale image lithography. Different laser heat-mode grayscale lithography can be well obtained and the corresponding photonic elements are being developed. The laser heat-mode grayscale lithography may be further applied to the fabrication of grayscale mask.

===== [p23-24] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (1) Laser Interference Lithography (4块) =====
--[p23]-- The light waves are coherent with each other, because they either come from the same light source, or have the same wavelength. The interference patterns, with an intensity profile containing periodical maxima and minima, irradiate and record on the two-dimensional (thin) or three-dimensional (thick) photosensitive layer and form periodic micro/nanostructures.
Figure 1.9 shows the schematic of interference lithography, where two beams are used.
--[p23]-- (1) Laser Interference Lithography
Laser interference lithography (LIL) is a type of optical lithography based on which one can quickly fabricate large-area periodic micro/nanostructures with a small feature size below 1.0 lm [20, 21]. Laser interference refers to the interaction of light waves, and generates a distribution of light intensities with periodic maxima and minima.
--[p24]-- The laser beam is focused and passes through a pinhole and collimated through a collimator lens (beam expander), then split into two beams, which are reflected by two mirrors onto a light-mode resist layer and form interference patterns. The interference patterns are further recorded on the light-mode resist layer due to a light-sensitive optical exposure effect.
Laser interference lithography possesses a simple optical framework.
--[p24]-- One can directly fabricate large-area pattern structures on the light-mode resist without a mask. Unfortunately, one can only fabricate periodic dots or line structures, and it is difficult to fabricate arbitrary nonperiodic structures.

===== [p114-114] Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists > 5.1 Introduction (3块) =====
--[p114]-- 5.1 Introduction
As is well known, the photolithography patterns are generally written on organic resists. The organic resists are large molecular-weight long-chain compounds (polymers) [1, 2]. The photolithography exposure is due to photo-induced glass transition effect. The exposure dose is required to be low, and thus the organic resist can be used in the projection (mask) lithography.
--[p114]-- However, the feature size of photo-induced exposure lithography is determined by the spot size, and difficult to reduce down to nanoscale for visible light lithography due to diffraction limit [3, 4]. Fortunately, the laser heat-mode lithography through threshold effect of structural change (such as crystallization) can overcome the diffraction limit, and achieve nanolithography [5].
In laser heat-mode lithography, the lithography material is required to possess the following properties:
--[p114]-- (1) Appropriate linear and nonlinear absorption coefficients at the laser wavelength, which can cause the lithography at several milliwatt laser power.
(2) Suitable structural change temperature, which can cause the pattern feature size to be reduced to nanoscale.
(3) Excellent etching selectivity between the laser irradiation and non-irradiation areas.
(4) Low in-plane thermal diffusion capability, which can prevent the pattern feature size from becoming large due to thermal diffusion.
The inorganic heat-mode lithography resists are generally from Te-based chalcogenide materials.

===== [p64-64] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.3.1 Schematic of Optical Astigmatic Method with a Double Cylindrical Lens Group (5块) =====
--[p64]-- The FQD, which is placed behind the DCLG, is used as the signal detection element and acquires the focusing error signal (FES). $L _ { o }$ is the objective lens with a focal length of $f _ { o }$ , and used to focus the laser beam into a spot. $P _ { f }$ is the focal plane of $L _ { o }$ , while $P _ { a }$ and $P _ { p }$ are the apofocal plane and perifocal plane of $L _ { o }$ , respectively.
In Fig. 3.4a, the DCLG actually functions as an astigmatic element and generates the FES.
--[p64]-- 3.3.1 Schematic of Optical Astigmatic Method with a Double Cylindrical Lens Group
The schematic of the optical path is presented in Fig. 3.4a, where two cylindrical lenses (marked as $\mathrm { C L } _ { x }$ and $\mathbf { C L } _ { y } )$ are used as astigmatic elements. The focal lengths of $\mathrm { C L } _ { x }$ and $\mathrm { C L } _ { x }$ are marked as $f _ { x }$ and $f _ { y }$ , respectively.
--[p64]-- The cylindrical lenses $\mathrm { C L } _ { x }$ and $\mathbf { C L } _ { y }$ are placed into a mutually orthogonal system. $\mathrm { C L } _ { x }$ tunes the x-directional optical axis of spot on the four-quadrant detector (FQD) and $\mathrm { C L } _ { \mathrm { y } }$ changes the ydirectional optical axis of spot on the FQD. Thus, $\mathbf { C L } _ { x }$ and $\mathrm { C L } _ { y }$ together form a double cylindrical lens group (DCLG).
--[p64]-- The generation principle of FES is as follows. When the sample is placed on the focal plane $P _ { f }$ of $L _ { o }$ , the reflected light passes through $L _ { o }$ and DCLG, then arrives at the FQD. The spot on the FQD is circular (Fig. $3 . 4 \mathsf { b } \cdot \mathsf { b } _ { 2 } )$ . The spot on the FQD becomes an upright ellipse when the sample is placed on the plane $P _ { a }$ (apofocus) of $L _ { o } ~ ( \mathrm { F i g . } ~ 3 .
--[p64]-- 4 \mathrm { b } { \cdot } \mathrm { b } _ { 1 } )$ . A horizontal elliptical spot can be formed if the sample is placed on the plane $P _ { p }$ (perifocus) of $L _ { o } ~ ( \mathrm { F i g . } ~ 3 . 4 \mathbf { b } \mathbf { - } \mathbf { b } _ { 3 } )$

===== [p176-176] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.1 Introduction (4块) =====
--[p176]-- 8.1 Introduction
In the process of interaction of laser with thin-film materials, a laser pulse irradiates onto the surface of thin-film materials, and the laser pulse energy is absorbed by the thin-film materials. The thin-film materials are heated and the temperature is raised, accordingly. A thermally induced structural change occurs when the temperature exceeds certain threshold.
--[p176]-- In this process, the interaction between laser and materials usually involves structural changes, such as crystallization, oxidization, melting, and solidification. The vaporization and even ablation occur when the laser pulse energy is high enough to exceed the certain threshold of the materials. Accompanied with the structural change, the surface morphology experiences a large change, the micro/ nanostructures are formed, accordingly.
--[p176]-- The micro/nanostructure features are sensitively dependent on laser pulse energy, properties of thin-film materials, and the material preparation procedures. This formation of micro/nanostructures takes only tens and hundreds of nanoseconds and the size of the patterns often ranges from tens of nanometers to micrometers.
--[p176]-- Accompanied with the structural change and micro/ nanostructure formation during the laser irradiation, the reflection and transmission optical spectra are changed, and the grayscale image lithography can be achieved on the thin-film materials, accordingly. Different grayscale images can be directly written on the surface of thin-film materials by the laser heat-mode lithography.
© Springer Nature Singapore Pte Ltd. 2019 J.
Wei, Laser Heat-Mode Lithography, Springer Series in Materials Science 291, https://doi.org/10.1007/978-981-15-0943-8\_8

===== [p149-150] Chapter 7 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Transparent Thin Films > 7.2 Principle of Laser Heat-Mode Patterning of Transparent Thin Films (8块) =====
--[p149]-- 7.2 Principle of Laser Heat-Mode Patterning of Transparent Thin Films
The patterning of transparent thin films is based on the release of phase-transition latent. Laser-induced thermal phase-transition can obtain submicrometer (or even nanoscale) phase-transition region due to the thermal threshold effect of phase-transition process, which has been used as laser heat-mode lithography and applied to the fabrication of Blu-ray disk of optical data storage.
--[p149]-- Thus submicrometer (or even nanoscale) phase-transition region is obtained at the center of laser spot. Accompanied by nanoscale phase-transition, the phase-transition latent-heat is released. For example, in chalcogenide phase-change materials, the phase-transition from amorphous state to crystalline state can release structural heat, since the energy of stable crystalline phase is lower than that of metastable amorphous phase.
--[p149]-- Thus, the remained energy is released in phase-transition process, as shown in right inset of Fig. 7.1a.
Inspired from this case, one proposes micro/nanolithography of transparent thin films through laser-induced release of phase-transition latent-heat of light absorption layer. The basic physical picture is as follows. First, the transparent thin film is deposited onto the light absorption layer, as shown in Fig. 7.1b.
--[p149]-- The light absorption thin film absorbs the laser spot energy and is heated to phase-transition threshold temperature. Based on the phase-transition threshold effect, the phase-transition region can be finely tuned through changing laser power and irradiation time. The submicrometer (or even nanoscale) phase-transition region can be realized when one utilizes proper peak intensity to heat the light absorption thin film to phase-transition threshold temperature.
--[p150]-- Thus, the region above the threshold temperature generates evident structural changes. The phase-transition latent-heat is released and subsequently released latent-heat heats upper transparent thin film, making its structure changed. Thus, micro/ nanopatterns can be written on the transparent thin film as shown in Fig. 7.1c, where the feature size of micro/nanopatterns can be tuned to be smaller than the writing spot due to the phase-transition threshold effect of light absorption thin film.
--[p150]-- Micro/nanopatterns are further etched due to the etching selectivity between written and unwritten regions at acid/alkali solution. Finally, the large-area micro/ nanostructures can be inscribed on the transparent thin films, as shown in Fig. 7.1d, where the feature size of micro/nanostructures is smaller than the writing spot due to laser-induced below diffraction-limited phase-transition region.
--[p150]-- Then the laser beam is focused into a diffraction-limited spot (the writing spot) on the light absorption thin film. The writing spot energy is absorbed and heats the light absorption thin film. Since the laser intensity presents a Gaussian profile shown in the left inset of Fig. 7.1a, the intensity at spot center is the largest, and the spot heats the light absorption thin film to phase-transition threshold temperature.
--[p149]-- Figure 7.1a gives the physical picture of laser-induced thermal phase-transition. It is worth mentioning that the laser wavelength is 405 nm in laser writing procedure. A collimated laser beam is focused onto the light absorption thin films, where the light absorption thin film is deposited on the glass substrate. The intensity of focused spot is typical Gaussian profile, as shown in left inset of Fig. 7.1a.

$$

===== [p109-110] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.5.2 Short Laser Pulse (8块) =====
--[p110]-- Figure 4.17c shows temperature profiles at different laser power, where the laser pulse width and the writing speed are fixed at 8 ns and $6 ~ \mathrm { m / s } ,$ , respectively. It is revealed that the peak temperature increases with increasing laser power. The peak temperature is higher than the threshold temperature of AIST when the laser power reaches 1.5 mW. Figure 4.17d gives the normalized temperature profiles.
--[p109]-- One can see the peak temperature increases with increasing laser pulse time. When laser pulse time is longer than 18 ns, the peak temperature is higher than the threshold temperature, which can cause the formation of micro/ nanopatterns. To understand the effect of laser pulse time on heat-spot size, the normalized temperature profiles are shown in Fig. 4.17b, where the inset is the relationship between heat-spot size and laser pulse time.
--[p110]-- [16] fabricated crystalline $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ thin film, and then the amorphous marks were written by a phase-change disk tester with a laser wavelength of 405 nm and an objective lens with numerical aperture of 0.65. After wet etching in NaOH solution, convex amorphous mark patterns are displayed as shown in Fig. 4.18. It is noted that the pulse laser mode is used.
--[p110]-- One can see that the pulse width decreases from Fig. 4.19a to Fig. 4.19d. The feature size of patterns gradually reduces with the reduction of pulse width. It is worthwhile mentioning that the irregular dot shape in Fig. 4.19a–c is due to the effect of combination of high writing speed and long laser pulse width. The line patterns with a pitch of 600 nm, linewidth of 140 nm, and height of 70 nm are shown in Fig. 4.19e, f.
--[p109]-- 4.5.2 Short Laser Pulse
In real applications, the dot array nanostructures are also very useful in data storage devices, metasurface-based optical elements and so forth. Dot array structures can be written by laser pulse. The laser pulse time is important to obtain patterns with small feature size. The temperature profiles of AIST thin film at different laser pulse times are presented in Fig. 4.17a, where the laser power is 1 mW and writing speed is 6 m/s.
--[p110]-- The minimum linewidth of dot patterns is as small as 40 nm, which is close to 1/7 of diffraction-limit spot.
Li et al. fabricated dot array structures on AIST thin films by direct laser writing and developed in 17 wt% $\mathrm { ( N H _ { 4 } ) _ { 2 } S }$ solutions [17]. The laser power was 1.35 mW; writing speed was 2 m/s and the pulse period was 2 ls. Figure 4.19 shows the SEM images of dot-shaped structures on AIST thin films with different laser pulse widths.
--[p109]-- The heat-spot size decreases from 440 to 390 nm with the reduction of laser pulse time from 58 to 8 ns. The reduction amplitude can be as high as 11.4%. The reduction of heat-spot size is caused by the weakening thermal diffusion owing to short laser pulse time.
One can see from Fig. 4.17a that when laser pulse time is 8 ns, the peak temperature is lower than the threshold temperature of AIST. To perform patterning, one needs to increase laser power.
--[p110]-- One can see the heat-spot size reduces from 390 to 377 nm when the laser power increases from 1 to 2 mW. This may be because the high laser power leads to a steep temperature gradient and high cooling rate. This, in turn, restrains the in-plane thermal diffusion thus reduces the heat-spot size. Therefore, to obtain smaller dot array pattern feature size, one can both increase laser power and reduce the laser pulse time.

===== [p125-126] Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists > 5.3.2 Short Irradiation Time Through High-Speed Rotation Movement (5块) =====
--[p125]-- The laser beam is modulated into arbitrary wave-shape light through connecting a signal generator to the TTL input port of diode laser. Then the pulsed laser is focused into a diffraction-limited spot on the surface of a circular disk of 120 mm in diameter mounted on a rotating stage. The chalcogenide heat-mode resists are deposited onto the circular disks. The maximum linear speed can reach up to 10 m/s.
--[p125]-- 5.3.2 Short Irradiation Time Through High-Speed Rotation Movement
The high-speed sample movement can be obtained through rotation-type laser heat-mode lithography system. Figure 5.10 is the schematics of rotation-type laser heat-mode lithography system, where a collimated beam from a GaN-based diode laser $( \lambda = 4 0 5$ nm) is focused by an optical lens $( N A = 0 . 9 0 )$ . The focused spot size is theoretically $D = 1 . 2 2 \lambda / N A \sim 5 5 0 \mathrm { n m }$ .
--[p125]-- One can manipulate the dwell time of focused spot onto the sample below 100 ns or even several nanoseconds through changing the sample movement speed, the dwell time can be calculated as $t _ { p } \sim 2 w _ { 0 } / \nu$ , where v is sample movement speed.
In addition, the $\mathrm { \Delta D _ { \perp } }$ channel, including $\mathrm { D } _ { \perp \mathrm { u p } }$ and $\mathrm { D \_ d o w n }$ , can improve the lithography pattern depth.
--[p126]-- Unlike S/Se-based chalcogenides and organic photoresists, AIST bears a relatively large thermal diffusion coefficient, which enables the photothermal response depth to be significantly larger than the light penetration depth, and consequently extends the lithography depth.
--[p126]-- For AIST resists, the light penetration depth is only about 10 nm, which is a very low value for lithography. Without the thermal diffusion, the lithography depth would be restricted to only about 10 nm, since there is no light at a deeper place.

===== [p69-69] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.3.4.1 Optical Module Design (3块) =====
--[p69]-- 3.3.4.1 Optical Module Design
In order to verify the optimized results obtained through theoretical analysis, a servo-tracking module with a double cylindrical lens group (shown in Fig. 3.10) was established [4]. In this setup, a laser with a wavelength of 658 nm passes through 1/2 waveplate, polarized beam splitter (PBS), 1/4 waveplate, and an objective lens $L _ { o }$ in sequence, then focuses onto the sample surface.
--[p69]-- Based on the theoretical calculation, the parameters are chosen as follows: $f _ { o } = 2$ mm and $r _ { o } = 5 \mathrm { m m }$ for $L _ { o } ; f _ { x } = 8 0$ mm for $\mathrm { C L } _ { x }$ $f _ { y } = 1 5 0 \mathrm { m m }$ for $\mathrm { C L } _ { y } ; l _ { x } = 4 0 0$ mm; $m _ { x } = 1 5 0$ mm; $l _ { x y } = 3 0$ mm.
--[p69]-- The beam reflected from the sample surface passes through the objective lens $L _ { o }$ and PBS, goes through the DCLG, and focuses on the FQD. The objective lens is installed on an actuator and can be moved up and down along the z-direction by feeding the FES into the actuator controller.

===== [p70-70] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.3.4.2 Design of Elastic Damping Arm Actuator (4块) =====
--[p70]-- 3.3.4.2 Design of Elastic Damping Arm Actuator
The actuator provides stable focusing in z-axis direction and is one of the critical elements in the servo-tracking module. The actuator is actually an important energy transfer device in the precision motion control of the module that needs to integrate the mechanics and electrics. There are different actuators such as PZT and voice coil motor.
--[p70]-- The magnetic circuit comprises yoke, magnet, and copper coil, which are designed to produce the optimal magnetic flux density within the limit volume. To adjust the static tilt of objective lens, the bottom of yoke needs to be fabricated into bowl shape, and the center of yoke should be the same as the objective lens.
--[p70]-- The assembly device needs to be tested to perform high linearity during its operation working distance, and the dynamic tilt measurement should be lower than 1 arcminute (1/60 of one degree). Combination of high precision actuator and automatic servo control system, the servo-tracking error may be controlled within about tens of nanometers.
--[p70]-- Based on the current through the magnetic field, one can produce Lorentz force to drive the objective lens, an elastic damping arm actuator was designed [3]. Figure 3.11a, b gives the exploded view diagram and assembly diagram, respectively. The movement part is made of elastic material like copper foil, which is called as arm. In the design, the arm shape needs to be optimized to reduce the influence of higher order resonance oscillation.

===== [p179-179] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.2.2.1 Bump Patterns (7块) =====
--[p179]-- 8.2.2.1 Bump Patterns
As mentioned in Marangoni effect, the molten material in the closed system may be further vaporized when the temperature exceeds to the vaporization point. The micro/nanostructures will continue to expand and become large due to the vaporization effect of the molten materials in the closed system. The large bump structure is formed, accordingly.
In order to analyze the physical mechanism of bump formation, oner need to make some assumptions.
--[p179]-- Generally speaking, when laser beam irradiates the film, strong interaction with the material occurs. A small volume of thin film is locally and rapidly heated to above the melting point and as much as the vaporization point. Because of the high heating rate, the material is rapidly vaporized, and then the material undergoes a large volume change due to the strong gas expansion.
--[p179]-- However, if the laser energy is too high, and exceeds certain threshold of the material, the bump structures grow so big that a hole is formed at the apex of the bump, as shown in Fig. 8.2d, where the small holes with a size of about 100 nm are formed at the top of the bumps.
--[p179]-- (1) The affected area is defined as the locus zone where the laser energy could reach the largest depth. Any structural change occurs only in the area, including phase change, melt, vaporization, and ablation.
(2) Because of the rapid heating rate, the chalcogenide phase-change material will turn into gaseous state directly when the temperature exceeds the vaporization point, and the volume change caused by phase change is neglected.
--[p179]-- Actually, the microbump is not a complete enclosed space, interdiffusion between the gas inside the bump and the air outside occurs, which causes the pressure inside and outside the microbump to reach a balance, just as shown in Fig. 8.2b. During this process, a part of the gas inside the microbump escapes, and the other resolidifies as solid residue. Figure 8.2c is the experimental images of bump array.
--[p179]-- If the laser energy is not high enough, the expanded volume would remain as microbump after the material is cooled down to the room temperature because of the fast cooling rate. Figure 8.2 shows the process of laser direct writing chalcogenide phase-change thin film deposited on glass substrate. Figure 8.2a is the schematic of the microbump array when the AIST film is heated beyond the vaporization threshold temperature.
--[p179]-- (3) The incident pulse laser with Gaussian intensity profile is used. The thermal diffusion and heat loss during the whole process is neglected.
(4) Because the AIST phase-change material is protected by the carbon film, it is assumed that the material is not ablated during laser processing. The gas converted by the material vaporization is trapped in the bump without escaping before the laser being removed.

===== [p193-193] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.5 Other Grayscale Lithography Methods (1块) =====
--[p193]-- 8.5 Other Grayscale Lithography Methods

===== [p29-30] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.3.1 Organic Resist Thin Films (5块) =====
--[p29]-- 1.3.1 Organic Resist Thin Films
To this date, light-mode resist has been commonly used in the manufacture of ICs and other micro/nanostructures. The lithographic process is schematically shown in Fig. 1.13a. It consists of steps for substrate cleaning, light-mode resist coating, prebaking, exposure, postexposure baking, developing, and hard baking [27].
(1) Preparing light-mode resist solution, the light-mode resist material dissolves into a solvent.
--[p30]-- However, if the unexposed region is completely removed and the exposed region is maintained in organic solvent, the light-mode resist is viewed as a negative tone resist.
In addition, the entire lithographic process is executed in a room with yellow lighting.
--[p30]-- (5) Postexposure baking, postexposure baking is required so that the $\mathrm { H } ^ { + }$ can interact with insoluble groups, thus improving the solubility in the developer. Meanwhile, the postexposure baking process will release additional H<sup>+</sup> until the insoluble groups of the exposed region are completely converted into soluble groups, as shown in the right part of Fig. 1.13b.
--[p30]-- (6) Developing, after the development in the solvent, the light-mode resist thin films need to be hard baked so that the mechanical property and the etching resistance are improved. Generally, the developer is either an organic solvent (acetone) or an organic alkali solution (TMAH). The light-mode resist is regarded as positive tone resist in organic alkali solution, that is, the exposed region is completely removed, while the unexposed region is maintained.
--[p29]-- (2) Preparing light-mode resist thin film, the light-mode resist solution is coated onto a wafer and a light-mode resist thin film is formed.
(3) Prebaking, in order to remove the remaining solvent, the coated light-mode resist thin film requires prebaking.
(4) Exposure, when the light-mode resist is exposed, photo-acid generators (PAG) of light-mode resist thin films will absorb a photon, and an H<sup>+</sup> will then be generated.

===== [p53-53] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.4.1 Umbrella-Flip Model (7块) =====
--[p53]-- An alternative description of the structural transformation is an umbrella-flip distortion resulting in disordering of the Ge sublattice. Notice that the three covalent bonds remain intact. This conservation of the system of stronger covalent bonds is crucial: The Ge atoms occupy the octahedral and tetrahedral symmetry positions in the crystalline and amorphous states, respectively. The stronger covalent bonds are shown with thicker lines.
--[p53]-- 2.4.1 Umbrella-Flip Model
For the umbrella-flip model, the structural transformation (such as $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 } )$ is illustrated in Fig. 2.19, where a Ge atom is shown within the face-centered cubic structure formed by Te atoms (Fig. 2.19, left). An intense laser (or electric) pulse induces rupture of the weaker bonds and the Ge atom flips into the tetrahedral position (Fig. 2.19, right).
--[p53]-- According to umbrella-flip model, it is proposed that the structural transformation is just a flip between the octahedral and the tetrahedral structures through the Ge atoms.
Based on the analysis of extended X-ray absorption fine structure (EXAFS), Krbal et al.
--[p53]-- There is a strong conflict among the results of DFT simulations, experimental analysis of EXAFS, and the umbrella-flip model derived from the EXAFS. The umbrella-flip model based on EXAFS analysis depends only on interatomic distances but not on the bonding angles.
--[p53]-- In phase transformation, there appears not only the umbrella-flip of Ge atoms but also the rupture of “resonant” bonds with the subsequent formation of three-fold coordinated Ge sites. This modified model can better elucidate the fast phase-change mechanism.
--[p53]-- [25] performed Ab initio computer simulations and found that melt-quenched amorphous $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ thin films comprised only about 30% of the total number of Ge atoms, which were located at central position of tetrahedral structures. The majority of Ge atoms were found to have a defective “octahedral” local structure, with bond angles close to $9 0 ^ { \circ }$ although a significant fraction of these were four-fold coordinated.
--[p53]-- According to the comparison between X-ray absorption near edge structure (XANES) and first-principles simulations, one finds that the local structure around Ge atoms can be divided into three characteristic groups: tetrahedral $\mathrm { ( T _ { d } ) }$ configurations (seven sites) with bond lengths typically around 2.58 $\dot { \mathrm { A } } ;$ pyramidal $( \mathrm { P _ { y } } )$ with a Ge atom at the apex and with Te–Ge–Te angles very close to $9 0 ^ { \circ }$ and highly distorted octahedral $\mathrm { ( O _ { h } ) }$ structures, as are shown in Fig. 2.20. Further study indicates that the four-coordinated Ge atoms in amorphous $\mathrm { G e } _ { 2 } \mathrm { S b } _ { 2 } \mathrm { T e } _ { 5 }$ thin films possess tetrahedral and defective octahedral configurations.

===== [p13-13] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.2.1.1 Projection Lithography (DUV/EUV) (1块) =====
--[p13]-- 1.2.1.1 Projection Lithography (DUV/EUV)
Projection light lithography has been used in the mass production of very large-scale integrated circuits. According to the development of the technique and
requirements, the projection lithography can be classified into two categories; one is refraction-mode projection lithography and the other is reflection-mode projection lithography.

===== [p14-15] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (1) Refraction-Mode Projection Lithography (6块) =====
--[p14]-- The collimated beam passes through the mask and is then focused onto the light-mode resist layer. The resolution limit (R) of projection lithography is expressed as
$$
R = k _ { 1 } \frac { \lambda } { N A }\tag{ð<sup>1:1</sup>Þ}
$$
where $\gimel$ is the wavelength of the light source, NA is the numerical aperture of the optical system, and $k _ { 1 }$ is a processing factor related to a specific imaging process.
--[p15]-- One can observe that a large NA value and a short wavelength will result in increased lithography resolution.
In the early days, mercury lamps were used as the light sources. The spectral line at the wavelength of 436 nm was used initially to illuminate the mask. With the reduction of the feature size of the masks, the short light source wavelength of 365 nm became the common choice.
--[p14]-- (1) Refraction-Mode Projection Lithography
Contact optical lithography has been used since the early days of integrated circuit manufacturing. In this method, an optical mask is in direct contact, or has a proximity gap, with a photoresist-coated substrate. The features in the mask are transferred to the light-mode resist at 1:1 ratio without reduction [3].
--[p15]-- In IC manufacturing, the mainstream wavelength of the excimer laser is 193 nm, which is in the deep ultraviolet (DUV) region. To this date, the minimum feature size has reduced down to 10 nm. Further reduction of the feature size faces great challenges owing to the sophisticated processes and increasing cost.
--[p14]-- In order to achieve high resolution and eliminate mask damages and contamination problems, projection optical lithography is developed, which can project a demagnified image onto light-mode resist at the reduction ratio of 5:1 or 10:1 [4]. Thus far, projection optical lithography prevails for submicron patterning. Figure 1.1 shows the basic schematic of projection optical lithography, where the light is first adjusted into a collimated beam.
--[p15]-- However, additional demands imposed in the imaging of small mask features made the mercury lamp unsuitable as the illumination source, owing to the insufficient photon energy at shorter wavelengths, which did not meet the mass production requirement of ICs.
Excimer laser has the advantages of possessing both high-photon energy and a shorter wavelength. Excimer laser results from a pulsed gas discharge, and can produce the ultraviolet light [4].

===== [p192-192] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.4.2 Structural Evolution Induced Grayscale Patterns (3块) =====
--[p192]-- 8.4.2 Structural Evolution Induced Grayscale Patterns
In the process of crystallization, oxidization, melting, and ablation, the optical reflectivity of $\mathrm { T e O } _ { x }$ thin films changes with the structural evolution due to different laser energy irradiation. Figure 8.14a shows the optical reflectivity spectra under the irradiation of different laser energy.
--[p192]-- The reflectivity in visible light range enhances with the increment of laser energy from 0 to $2 8 0 0 \ \mathrm { m J / c m } ^ { 2 }$ . However, further increasing laser energy from 2800 to $5 6 0 0 ~ \mathrm { m J / c m } ^ { 2 }$ leads to the gradual reduction of reflectivity. The grayscale bars are fabricated, accordingly, and the grayscale becomes lighter first and then is deeper with increasing laser energy.
--[p192]-- Figure 8.14b–e presents the arbitrary patterns recorded on the $\mathrm { T e O } _ { \mathit { s } }$ thin film, where the laser energy is changed from 0 to $\mathbf { \bar { 5 6 0 0 } } \mathbf { m J / c m ^ { 2 } } .$ . The recorded patterns are clear, and the appearance is lifelike.

For the purpose of minimizing the in-plane thermal diffusion and improving the pattern resolution, a thermal conduction layer, such as Al or Si thin film, can be employed. ===== [p25-26] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (3) Zone-Plate-Array Lithography (6块) =====
--[p26]-- Control software synchronizes the sample stage with the multiplexers in order to produce the desired pattern on the substrate. The address grid on the pattern is smaller than the minimum feature size. This ensures smooth lines and gives better linewidth and edge placement control. However, since the diffractive lenses can be designed to focus any wavelength light, ZPAL offers a solution scalable to shorter wavelength with relatively small changes in architecture.
--[p26]-- Zone-plate arrays consist of numerous zone plates. The zone plate can be fabricated using laser writing and electron-beam lithography. The zone plate is generally fabricated in fused silica. The numerical aperture of the zone plate ranges from 0.7 to 0.95.
ZPAL combines the advantages of maskless lithography with the high throughput of a parallel-beam writing system. However, some issues need to be considered as follows:
(1) The pattern resolution is still limited by the light wavelength.
--[p25]-- (3) Zone-Plate-Array Lithography
Zone-plate-array lithography (ZPAL) is a maskless lithographic technique that provides an inherent flexibility and a quick turnaround time for innovative chip designs [24]. A schematic of ZPAL is shown in Fig. 1.11, where the zone-plate array is actually an array of diffractive lenses, and the light source is GaN diode laser with a wavelength of 405 nm. The array of the diffractive lenses focuses the incident light into an array of spots on a substrate.
--[p26]-- (2) The lithographic throughout of the system is influenced by the speed of the sample stage and the power of the laser.
(3) The switching speed of these multiplexers must be high enough to meet the throughput requirements of the system.
--[p25]-- The size of the diffraction-limited spot is determined by the numerical aperture of the lens. Correspondingly, arbitrary patterns can be generated by using micromechanics to independently multiplex the light incident on each lens, and to simultaneously scan the substrate and write arbitrary patterns.
In lithography, the stage scans the substrate under a zone plate in a serpentine manner with high precision.
--[p26]-- Since diffractive elements exhibit large chromatic aberrations, it is important that the light source has sufficiently small bandwidth.
In ZPAL, the zone plate consists of a set of concentric rings in which alternating rings have a p phase shift with respect to each other. The rings are arranged such that the first diffraction orders from all the rings interfere constructively at the focal plane. The focusing efficiency of such a zone plate is approximately 40%.

===== [p171-173] Chapter 7 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Transparent Thin Films > 7.6 Direct Patterning Through $\mathbf { A g 0 } _ { x }$ as Light Absorption Layer [19] (10块) =====
--[p173]-- In general, the higher aspect ratio results in a better performance. In Fig. 7.25c, one can see that the aspect ratios rapidly increase from the minimum of 0.012 at laser power of 3.0 mW to the maximum of 0.201 at laser power of 5.0 mW, which indicates that the better aspect ratio can be obtained at higher laser powers.
--[p171]-- 7.6 Direct Patterning Through $\mathbf { A g 0 } _ { x }$ as Light Absorption Layer [19]
One can directly fabricate the pattern structures on the ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin films without wet etching. It is well known that, in an open system, the $\mathrm { A g O _ { \it 3 } }$ material is chemically decomposed into $\mathbf { A } \mathbf { g }$ particles and $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ at about $1 6 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C }$ [20].
--[p171]-- Figure 7.23b shows the interior situation when $\operatorname { A g O } _ { x }$ decomposes into silver and oxygen. The $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ and $\mathbf { A } \mathbf { g }$ particles are rough and tumble, and filled in the whole room. After the $\mathrm { A g O } _ { x }$ is cooling down to the room temperature, the expanded volume will be left as a bump. If one precisely controls the laser parameters, the regular and uniform bump array pattern structure can be obtained.
--[p172]-- At the laser power of 3.0 mW, the pattern is almost undistinguishable.
Figure 7.25 shows the dependences of pattern height, diameter, and aspect ratio (aspect ratio = height/diameter) on laser power. One can find that both height and diameter increase with the laser power, as shown in Fig. 7.25a, b. The range of the height is from 6 to 183 nm, and the diameter is varied from 482 to 912 nm, accordingly. The aspect ratio is an important factor to estimate the pattern quality.
--[p172]-- In order to direct patterning on ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin film, Dun et al. designed three-layered $^ { \mathrm { { < Z } n S - S i O _ { 2 } ( 1 0 \ n m ) / A g O _ { \scriptscriptstyle { x } } ( 1 0 0 \ n m ) / Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } ( 1 0 \ n m ) ^ { \mathrm { { > } } } } }$ sample structure [19].
--[p172]-- Figure 7.24 shows pattern structures written on multilayered $\mathrm { Z n S - }$ $\mathrm { S i O _ { 2 } / A g O _ { \scriptscriptstyle x } / Z n S - S i O _ { 2 } }$ films by green laser with the laser wavelength of 488 nm and the laser power from 3 to 5 mW. One can see that the pattern structure appears to be taper shape and very regular and uniform. The boundary between the areas with and without laser irradiation is well defined, and the patterns are very steep with smooth wall.
--[p172]-- Figure 7.24a, c shows the three-dimensional (3D) photos written by higher and lower laser powers, respectively. Figure 7.24b, d shows the lateral photos of Fig. 7.24a, c, respectively. The different pattern height can be realized by tuning the laser power. The larger the laser power, the higher the pattern. When the laser power is 5.0 mW, the height reaches the largest value. As the laser power decreases, the height gradually decreases.
--[p171]-- The oxygen released by the decomposition stays in the enclosed system, so it will apply pressure to surface. When the thickness is increased to more than 100 nm, it may produce a big oxygen bubble by the pressure and heat, following the $\operatorname { A g O } _ { x }$ decomposition induced by the focused laser beam, and a huge volume expansion is formed at last, as shown in Fig. 7.23a.
--[p172]-- In fact, the layer structure is not a completely enclosed system; inter-diffusion between the gas in the bump and the air outside occurs, which causes the pressure inside and outside the bump to reach up to balance. However, if the laser energy is very high and exceeds the ablation threshold of $\operatorname { A g O } _ { x }$ , the bumps may grow so big that a hole will form at the apex.
--[p171]-- When the film structure is heated beyond this temperature, $\mathrm { A g O _ { \it 3 } }$ layers will decompose to $\mathbf { A } \mathbf { g }$ and $\mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ according to $\mathrm { A g O } _ { x } { \longrightarrow } \mathrm { A g } + x / 2 \mathrm { O } _ { 2 }$ . When the laser beam irradiates on the $\operatorname { A g O } _ { x }$ film, a small volume of thin film is locally and rapidly heated to above the decomposing temperature, and then the reaction happens.

===== [p96-96] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.3.2 Thin-Film Thickness (7块) =====
--[p96]-- This case is explained by the absorption of laser energy, which increases when the AIST thickness increases. However, the thermal diffusion of AIST thin film increases with further increasing AIST thickness, which causes the peak temperature to be reduced. Moreover, the peak temperatures at various thicknesses are all higher than threshold temperature $( T _ { c } = 2 0 0 ~ ^ { \circ } \mathrm { C } )$ of AIST thin film [7].
--[p96]-- 4.4c, one can see the heat flow diffuses along thickness direction, marked as black arrows. At the same time, the temperature decreases as AIST thickness increases. However, the heat flow also goes in the radial direction, as shown in Fig. 4.4d, which will cause the temperature profile to become flat and the pattern linewidth to increase. Thus, it is essential to restrain the influence of in-plane (radial) thermal diffusion to obtain the minimum pattern linewidth.
--[p96]-- The micro/nanopatterns can be obtained at threshold temperature region.
Figure 4.4b gives normalized spot intensity and temperature profiles of AIST thin film. One can see that the laser spot size is only 350 nm; however, the heat-spot size of AIST thin film can be as large as 460 nm due to the thermal diffusion effect. The thickness of AIST thin film (h) is first tuned and the results are displayed in the inset of Fig. 4.4b.
--[p96]-- One can see that the heat-spot size increases with the increment of AIST thickness. It is reported that the competition between $\mathrm { D _ { \parallel } }$ and $\mathrm { { D _ { \perp } } }$ can be expressed as $\eta _ { D _ { \parallel } / D _ { \perp } } \sim h$ [10]. That is, the in-plane thermal diffusion is more seriously than out-of-plane thermal diffusion with increasing AIST thickness, which is because the large thickness will increase the thermal diffusion time.
--[p96]-- The long thermal diffusion time increases the out-of-plane thermal diffusion length; however, the in-plane thermal diffusion length also increases, accordingly. Thereby, the reduction of AIST thickness is beneficial for the reduction of in-plane thermal diffusion distance and heat-spot size.
To understand the thermal diffusion, Fig. 4.4c, d shows the thermal field profiles of AIST thin film in the thickness and radial directions, respectively. From Fig.
--[p96]-- 4.3.2 Thin-Film Thickness
In laser heat-mode lithography, thermal diffusion is important for achieving a small pattern feature size. However, in-plane heat diffusion and out-of-plane heat diffusion are both greatly dependent on the heat-mode resist thickness. In order to understand the influence of heat-mode resist thickness on heat-spot size, the temperature profiles of heat-mode resist thin films are analyzed. AIST thin film, as an example, is used for a heat-mode resist layer.
--[p96]-- The laser wavelength is 405 nm and the NA of the converging lens is 0.85. The laser power is 0.8 mW and the writing speed is 4 m/s. Figure 4.4a displays the temperature profiles with different thicknesses. One can see that the peak temperature increases first with the increment of AIST thickness from 10 to 50 nm. However, the peak temperature decreases with further increases in AIST thickness from 50 to 110 nm.

===== [p20-20] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.2.2 Maskless Lithography (1块) =====
--[p20]-- 1.2.2 Maskless Lithography
Maskless lithography is suitable for individuals and small-volume manufacturers of micro/nanostructure devices [13]. In order to meet the requirements, different maskless lithography methods have been proposed and developed in recent years. Based on the operation tools, the maskless lithography can be classified in three categories, including high-energy beam writing, probe-based lithography, and laser beam writing.

$$

--[p157]-- 3$ the ZnS grains are dispersed in a matrix that is composed of $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ grains. The $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ grains isolate the ZnS grains, because the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ grain content is higher than that of ZnS grain. On the other hand, the ZnS grains are in contact with each other at $x = 0 . 8$ . The closed ZnS grains are grown by the annealing, and the grown ZnS grains surround the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ grains.
--[p157]-- In the wet etching process, the HF solution dissolves the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ grains, and a lift-off of ZnS grains occurs in the matrix. In the case of $x = 0 . 8$ , the ZnS grains grow enough to surround the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ grains during the annealing process, and the grown ZnS protects the $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ grains from the HF solution. Therefore, the etching tolerance of $( \mathrm { Z n S } ) _ { 0 . 8 } ( \mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 } ) _ { 0 .

===== [p21-21] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.2.2.1 High-Energy Beam Writing (1块) =====
--[p21]-- 1.2.2.1 High-Energy Beam Writing
The high-energy beam generally includes the electron beam, ion beam, and X-rays, etc. High-energy beam lithography has the merits of increased pattern resolution, and can be used for individual manufacturing as well as arbitrary nanofabrication. It operates in high-vacuum environments, thus the fabrication speed and cost are slow and high, respectively.

===== [p97-99] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.4.1 Lower Layer (9块) =====
--[p99]-- 4.7b, where the heat flow goes along both radial and thickness directions, as marked with black arrows. By comparing Fig. 4.7a with Fig. 4.7b, one can see that the addition of a lower Si layer can efficiently enhance the heat flow of the AIST thin film along the thickness direction and minimize the heat flow in radial direction, which can cause the heat-spot size to be reduced.
The relationship between the thickness of lower Si layer and the size of heat-spot is given in Fig. 4.7c.
--[p99]-- In order to confirm that the lower Si thin film can reduce the feature size of patterns, “AgInSbTe/glass substrate” and “AgInSbTe/Si/glass substrate” samples were prepared, respectively [11]. Direct laser writing experiment is further performed and the ablation holes are obtained as shown in Fig. 4.6. Figure 4.6a gives the ablation holes in the AgInSbTe/glass sample and the hole size is about 500 nm.
--[p98]-- Figure 4.5b shows the normalized temperature profiles of AIST thin film. The size of the heat-spot decreases from 447 to 386 nm with increasing the lower Si layer thickness. The addition of the lower Si layer can reduce the 13.6% heat-spot size, and in turn causes the pattern linewidth to decrease.
In laser heat-mode lithography, appropriate laser power is required to obtain the thermal threshold region. Figure 4.5c gives the temperature profiles of AIST thin film at different laser powers.
--[p99]-- It can be seen that the heat-spot size decreases as the lower Si layer thickness increases. Since the lower Si layer has high thermal conductivity, which can efficiently reduce the heat from the out-of-plane channel and prevent the thermal diffusion from in-plane channel. Moreover, the larger the Si thickness, the stronger the thermal diffusion capability of out-of-plane channel. Additionally, the reduction of heat-spot size is the largest when the lower Si layer thickness is 20 nm.
--[p99]-- Figure 4.6b shows the ablation hole morphology of the AgInSbTe/Si/glass sample, and the hole size is about 70 nm, which is evidently smaller than that without Si thin film. Thus, adding a lower Si layer is an efficient pathway to reduce the heat-spot size and feature linewidth of patterns.
In order to elucidate the effect of lower Si layer on heat-spot size, Fig. 4.7a shows thermal field distribution of AIST thin film. For comparison, a thermal field profile without Si layer is given in Fig.
--[p97]-- It is noted that the laser power is fixed at 0.8 mW and writing speed is 4 m/s. It is found that the peak temperature is higher than the threshold temperature of AIST thin film when no Si film is added. However, the peak temperature decreases and is lower than the threshold temperature as the Si thickness increases. This is due to the fact that the lower Si layer can take away the heat of AIST layer; thus, the temperature of AIST layer decreases.
--[p97]-- 4.4.1 Lower Layer
As a thermal conduction layer, the Si thin film can be inserted between AIST and glass substrate. The inset of Fig. 4.5 is the schematic of a sample structure, where the Si thin film is added to the bottom of the AIST layer and marked as lower Si layer. Figure 4.5a shows the temperature profiles of AIST thin film with different lower Si layer thickness. For comparison, the temperature profile of AIST thin film without Si layer is also given.
--[p98]-- The thickness of the AIST and Si layer is 50 nm and 20 nm, respectively, and the writing speed is 4 m/s. One can see that the peak temperature increases with increasing laser power. When the laser power is higher than 1.5 mW, the peak temperature reaches the threshold temperature of AIST thin film, thus micro/nanopatterns can be obtained. Figure 4.5d is the normalized temperature profiles. One can see that the laser power has no obvious influence on heat-spot size.
--[p99]-- Thus, the addition of a lower thermal conduction layer is an efficient method to obtain small heat-spot size.

===== [p56-57] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.5 Comparison Between Laser Heat-Mode and Light-Mode Lithography (6块) =====
--[p56 [表]]-- That is, the laser heat-mode lithography can bypass the optical diffraction limit and realize trans-scale lithography, where one can arbitrarily tune the lithographic feature size from nanoscale to micrometer without changing the laser spot size. The development process is a broken process of atom bonds, thus, the margin of lithographic patterns is more smooth and clear than the small molecular photoresist materials. That is, the line edge roughness can be controlled at a very low value.
--[p56]-- The lithography production procedures are greatly simplified because there are no prebaking and post-exposure baking steps required. The heat-mode resist thin films are not sensitive to laser wavelength, and the writing process does not need any darkroom or yellow light environment. The laser beam is focused on the far-field range, and the distance between sample and writing head is far larger than light wavelength, thus the writing operation is easy and writing speed is very fast, accordingly.
--[p56]-- The lithographic feature size is determined by four factors including laser spot, thermal phase-change threshold, thermal diffusion, and nonlinear responses. The pattern feature size can be either larger or far smaller than laser spot by tuning the writing strategy.
--[p57]-- Table 2.1 Comparison of laser heat-mode and light-mode lithography
Light-mode lithography | Heat-mode lithography
Lithography process | Complex | Simple
Environmental influence | Large | Small
Basic component unit | Molecules | Atoms
Feature size | Restricted by diffraction limit | Without diffraction limit
Exposure sensitivity | High | Low
Exposure light spectrum | Single wavelength light | Broadband light
Trans-scale lithography | No | Yes
Exposure response time | μs/ms | ns
Lithography environment | Yellow or vacuum chamber | In white light and air
--[p56]-- 2.5 Comparison Between Laser Heat-Mode and Light-Mode Lithography
Compared with light-mode lithography consisting of preparing the photoresist solution, photoresist spin-coating, prebaking, exposure, post-exposure baking, development, and hard baking, the laser heat-mode lithography has simple processes only including thin-film deposition, exposure, and development.
--[p56]-- Besides, the whole lithographic process is performed in white light and atmospheric environments, and the air has little effect on lithographic process. Table 2.1 gives the advantages and drawbacks of laser heat-mode and light-mode lithography, respectively.
In laser heat-mode lithography, the resist thin films are essentially opto-thermal response to the laser spot.

===== [p115-116] Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists > (1) Laser-Induced Crystallization (5块) =====
--[p116]-- From the pictures, one can clearly see the obvious color difference between the as-deposition region and the crystallization areas. The contour curves of the two kinds of crystallization areas in the insets show that the depth is about 10 nm, approximately 5% of the film thickness. This volume shrinkage effect is mainly due to the density difference between amorphous and crystalline states [8, 9].
--[p116]-- One can write different patterns on the Te-based chalcogenide materials through a focused laser beam spot. Figure 5.2 presents the results of laser-induced crystallization patterns on the AIST thin films. Figure 5.2a is the crystallization dots induced by laser pulses, and the size of the crystallization dots is about 1 lm. Figure 5.2b is the lines crystallized by continuous laser beam, where the linewidth is about 3 lm.
--[p116]-- During the laser irradiation, AIST film would transform from amorphous state to crystalline state [10, 11]. The density of crystallization state is 5% larger than the amorphous state, thus the volume of the material would decrease after this transformation.
--[p115]-- (1) Laser-Induced Crystallization
AgInSbTe (AIST) is one of the Te-based chalcogenide materials. The AIST thin film can be prepared through magnetron-controlled sputtering or thermal evaporation methods. The deposited AIST thin films are generally amorphous state, and can be crystallized after laser irradiation, as is shown in Fig. 5.1a, b [7]. One can see that there is a reflectance difference between laser-irradiated region and non-irradiated region. In Fig.
--[p115]-- 5.1c, X-ray diffraction analysis indicates that there are some crystallization peaks after laser irradiation, and without any crystallization peak occurs before laser irradiation (as-deposited state). This indicates that the laser irradiation causes the crystallization, and the laser intensity has little influence on crystallization peak position and intensity.
The laser-induced crystallization is a thermal process, and the laser is used as a heating source.

===== [p116-117] Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists > (2) Etching selectivity in alkaline solutions (7块) =====
--[p117]-- By comparison one can see that the $\mathrm { ( N H _ { 4 } ) } _ { 2 } S$ solution is a good etchant, in which the difference of etching rate between amorphous and crystalline states reaches 20 times.
The etching selectivity between crystalline and amorphous states can be obtained in NaOH solution. Dot-shaped and line-shaped pattern structures in Fig. 5.2 were obtained by the etching process.
--[p117]-- Figure 5.4 shows the AFM images of the dots and lines obtained after developing in NaOH solution, which appears to be smooth and uniform [12]. From the contour curves in insets, one can also see that both the dots and lines after developing are about 100 nm higher than the as-deposition state, just like relief structures with steep wall and smooth top.
--[p117]-- The difference of etching rate between laser irradiation region and non-laser irradiation region produces etching selectivity, and thus the pattern structures are formed through the laser heat-mode lithography of the AIST thin films.
The $\mathrm { ( N H _ { 4 } ) _ { 2 } S }$ and NaOH solutions are usually used as developing solutions. The etching selectivity of the AIST thin film in $\left( \mathrm { N H } _ { 4 } \right) _ { 2 } S$ solution is shown in Fig.
--[p117]-- For AIST thin films, the number and type of defects of laser irradiation region are different from non-laser irradiation region, which causes that the A of laser irradiation region is different from that of non-laser irradiation region. Thus, the etching rate of laser irradiation region is also different from that of non-laser irradiation region.
--[p117]-- $C _ { A } ^ { m }$ is initial concentration of alkaline solutions. $C _ { B } ^ { n }$ is concentration of AIST and can be considered to be a constant. A is effective molecular collision coefficient, and determined by concentration of alkaline solutions and defect density of AIST thin films. Formula (5.1) is simplified as
$$
\nu = A C _ { A } \mathrm { e x p } ( - E _ { a } )\tag{ð <sup>5:2</sup>Þ}
$$
--[p116]-- (2) Etching selectivity in alkaline solutions
AIST thin films are metal-type semiconductor materials, and can be developed in alkaline solutions. The developing rate may be expressed as
$$
\nu = A \mathrm { e x p } \left( - { \frac { E _ { a } } { k _ { B } T } } \right) C _ { A } ^ { m } C _ { B } ^ { n }\tag{ð<sup>5:1</sup>Þ}
$$
where $E _ { a }$ is surface activation energy of AIST thin film, $k _ { B }$ and T are Boltzmann constant and temperature, respectively.
--[p117]-- 5.3a, where one can see that an obvious step is produced due to the different etching rate between laser irradiation region and non-laser irradiation region. The step is clear and the height is about 175 nm (as shown in Fig. 5.3b). The AIST thin film displays excellent etching selectivity and laser heat-mode lithography characteristics.

===== [p18-18] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (3) UV-Cured Nanoimprint Lithography (2块) =====
--[p18]-- (3) UV-Cured Nanoimprint Lithography
Nanoimprint can be performed at room temperature, however, it is still not a low-pressure process. A room temperature and low-pressure process corresponds to the UV-cured NIL (UV-NIL) [3]. Figure 1.5 schematically depicts the process flow, which is almost the same as the thermal NIL. The key differences include the use of transparent stamp (such as quartz glass) and UV-curable polymer.
--[p18]-- The UV-curable polymers have viscosities ranging from 50 to 200 mPa.s in liquid form at room temperature. A very small pressure (<1 bar) is needed to press the stamp into the liquid polymer. The solidification of liquid polymer is conducted by UV illumination through the transparent stamp.

===== [p203-203] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 9.2.1.4 Transferring the Patterns on $\mathbf { G e S b S n O } _ { x }$ to Si Substrates (4块) =====
--[p203]-- Figure 9.9c indicates that the etching depth increases gradually with the platen power of less than 12 W, and increases significantly when the platen power is over 12 W. Figure 9.9d gives the hole structures through optimizing etching parameters, the diameter and depth of hole are 650 nm and 3.35 lm, respectively, the aspect ratio is as high as 5. It is noted that the worm shape in the top may be from the etching gas change.
--[p203]-- The $\mathrm { G e S b S n O } _ { x }$ thin film deposited on a Si substrate is first exposed by laser heat-mode lithography system, and then developed in alkaline solution. The pattern structures (hole structures) with the feature size of 350 nm are formed on $\mathrm { G e S b S n O } _ { x }$ thin film as pattern mask. Finally, the patterns are transferred to the Si substrates through the RIE method.
--[p203]-- 9.2.1.4 Transferring the Patterns on $\mathbf { G e S b S n O } _ { x }$ to Si Substrates
To obtain the pattern structures with large aspect ratio in transferring using reactive-ion etching, the laser heat-mode resist is required to have higher resistance to the plasma bombard and large etching selective ratio to Si substrates. The $\mathrm { G e S b S n O } _ { x }$ thin film is a good laser heat-mode resist [5]. The flow of pattern transferring is presented in Fig. 9.8.

In Fig. 9.9a, both etching depth and reamed width decrease with the increment of the ratio of passivation time to etching time. Figure 9.9b indicates that the etching depth of hole structures increase with increasing total pressure.

===== [p114-114] Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists (1块) =====
--[p114]-- Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists

===== [p52-52] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.4 Mechanism of Fast Phase Change of Te-Based Chalcogenides (1块) =====
--[p52]-- 2.4 Mechanism of Fast Phase Change of Te-Based Chalcogenides
The laser heat-mode resists are mainly from Te-based chalcogenide materials. In the laser heat-mode lithography, the thermal threshold effect of Te-based phase-change material is mainly due to the nature of fast phase change. In order to elucidate the fast phase-change characteristics, some phase-change mechanisms and models have been proposed including umbrella-flip model [24, 25] and multi-ring model [26].

===== [p170-170] Chapter 7 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Transparent Thin Films > 7.5.3 Amorphous Si as Light Absorption Layer (2块) =====
--[p170]-- 7.5.3 Amorphous Si as Light Absorption Layer
The amorphous Si (a-Si) thin film can be acted as optical absorption layer and pit patterns are written on the ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ layer [7, 8]. Figure 7.21 shows the pit shapes obtained through different cooling rate, where the cooling rate is manipulated through changing the thickness of a-Si thin film. The thinner amorphous Si film increases the cooling rate.
--[p170]-- An improvement of the pit shape can be obtained with 50 nm thickness of a-Si thin film. When the thickness of a-Si thin film is 25 nm, the cooling rate is too high to form suitable pit shapes. In the developing process, the basic part of a pit becomes slim and it collapses at random as shown in Fig. 7.21c.
Therefore, the thickness of the a-Si thin film of 50 nm is beneficial for uniform pit patterns on ${ \mathrm { Z n S } } { \mathrm { - } } { \mathrm { S i O } } _ { 2 }$ thin films.

===== [p29-29] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.3 Light-Mode Resist Materials (1块) =====
--[p29]-- 1.3 Light-Mode Resist Materials
The exposure in lithography is generally based on a photochemical reaction after the photoresist thin film absorbs the light energy. The resists are known as light-mode resist materials. Until today, the light-mode resist materials have included organic resist thin films and S/Se-based chalcogenide thin films.

===== [p129-129] Chapter 5 High-Speed Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Chalcogenide Resists > 5.4 Summary (2块) =====
--[p129]-- 5.4 Summary
The Te-based chalcogenides experience a structural change from amorphous state to crystalline state after a laser beam irradiation with an appropriate intensity. The structural change, such as crystallization, is a thermal process and takes place at a certain threshold temperature. The heat-mode phase-change lithography can be achieved through using a laser beam as a heating source.
--[p129]-- The nanolithography is obtained due to the threshold effect of structural change, and the feature size can be reduced down to about 46 nm, which is only about 1/12 of the laser spot size. The Te-based chalcogenide resist is one of the candidates for high-speed laser heat-mode lithography.

===== [p197-197] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 9.1 Introduction (1块) =====
--[p197]-- 9.1 Introduction
Laser heat-mode lithography is useful for obtaining arbitrary micro/nanostructures on the heat-mode resist thin films. In real applications, the micro/nanostructures need to be further transferred to the substrates, such as silicon, quartz, or sapphires. The optical/electronic elements and devices can be also obtained through the pattern transfer techniques.

===== [p15-15] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (2) Reflection-Mode Projection Lithography (4块) =====
--[p15]-- According to EUV lithography, the feature sizes of the patterns can be further reduced below the 10 nm boundary to 7 nm nodes, or lower [8, 9]. Moreover, the complexity of the process can be significantly reduced compared to DUV lithography. In parallel, EUV lithography can increase the design flexibility, shorten the time to yield, and lower the production cost.
--[p15]-- Reflective optics has to be used, and the basic system is schematically shown in Fig. 1.2, where the EUV light from the EUV source is collected by a set of reflective mirrors (illumination optics unit) and projected onto a EUV mask. The mask image is then focused by another set of reflective mirrors (projection optics unit) and projected onto a wafer to expose the EUV photoresist. EUV lithography must be performed in a near-vacuum condition to reduce atmospheric absorption of EUV light [6].
--[p15]-- Although the projection optical lithography has the advantages of mass production and high efficiency, its high-cost lithography system and masks impose constraints on individual and small-volume manufacturing.
--[p15]-- (2) Reflection-Mode Projection Lithography
Fortunately, extreme ultraviolet (EUV) lithography has been proposed to further reduce the feature size for node patterns from 7 to 3 nm and production costs [5]. EUV is in a strict sense no longer optical irradiation. The wavelength of the EUV lithography is approximately 13.6 nm, and is often referred to as soft X-ray. Conventional refractive optics ceases to function at EUV because of the strong absorption in almost all materials.

===== [p197-197] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > Chapter 9 Pattern Transfer for Laser Heat-Mode Lithography (1块) =====
--[p197]-- Chapter 9 Pattern Transfer for Laser Heat-Mode Lithography

===== [p75-75] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 3.4 Measurement Module of Sample Movement Fluctuation Error (2块) =====
--[p75]-- 3.4 Measurement Module of Sample Movement Fluctuation Error
As is well known, FES can be also detected through optical astigmatic method with a single cylindrical lens, where the cylindrical lens and four-quadrant photodetector are used as astigmatic and signal acquiring elements, respectively. Actually, the optical astigmatic method based on four-quadrant photodetector can be used to measure the sample movement flatness error. The measurement has a fast response and high accuracy.
--[p75]-- In the measurement, the sample is required to place at the focal plane of the objective lens, both focus error signal detection and auto-seeking focal plane of the objective lens are required.

===== [p174-174] Chapter 7 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography on Transparent Thin Films > 7.7 Summary (2块) =====
--[p174]-- 7.7 Summary
Micro/nanostructure-based optical devices, such as metalens and metasurface, are generally fabricated on the surface of transparent thin films or substrates through lithography methods. However, it is difficult to directly fabricate the micro/ nanostructures through traditional laser writing lithography, because the laser energy cannot be absorbed due to the very low absorption coefficient of transparent thin films.
--[p174]-- Laser heat-mode lithography provides an effective way for fabricating the micro/nanostructures on the transparent thin films, where the light energy can be absorbed through the light absorption layers, and then the light absorption layers are heated, the heat is transferred to the transparent thin films. When the temperature of transparent thin films exceeds certain threshold, the heat-mode lithography takes place, and thus the micro/nanostructures are produced on the transparent materials.
Therefore, the laser heat-mode lithography is one of good fabrication methods for metasurface optical elements.

===== [p17-18] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (2) Room-Temperature Nanoimprint Lithography (4块) =====
--[p17]-- The sample structure can be designed as an “HSQ (40 nm)/PMMA (150 nm)/silicon substrate,” whereby the HSQ has similar properties to $\mathrm { S i O } _ { 2 }$ when heated to high temperature.
The process flow is as follows:
(1) Coating PMMA on the silicon substrate.
(2) Baking the PMMA layer in the oven at 180 °C for 1 h.
(3) Coating the HSQ resist on the top of the PMMA layer.
(4) Baking the sample on a hotplate at 150 °C for 2 min followed by oven baking for 20 min at 180 °C.
--[p18]-- (2) The HSQ layer of approximately 40 nm is needed owing to the high etching resistance of HSQ to oxygen plasma.
(3) PMMA can function as a buffer layer to provide a soft landing for the silicon stamp and avoid damaging the stamp.
(4) The stamp can be repeatedly used without additional cleaning.
--[p17]-- (2) Room-Temperature Nanoimprint Lithography
Room-temperature nanoimprint lithography (RT-NIL) includes single RT-NIL and bilayer RT-NIL [3]. Figure 1.4 shows the schematics of RT-NIL where the HSQ/ PMMA bilayer resists are used, and the imprinted patterns are further transferred to the bottom layer by RIE.
--[p18]-- (5) Imprinting the pattern structures on the sample by a silicon stamp at 440 bars.
(6) Transferring the HSQ pattern to the PMMA layer through oxygen RIE.
(7) Removing the thin residual layer using RIE.
Compared with thermal press nanoimprint and single-layer RT-NIL, the advantages of the bilayer RT-NIL are as follows:
(1) A high aspect ratio can be achieved through the RIE of the PMMA layer.

--[p210]-- (2) The optical information patterns are written onto $\mathrm { W O } _ { x }$ thin films through laser heat-mode lithography system (Fig. 9.18b), whose laser wavelength is 351 nm, and numerical aperture of a converging lens is 0.9.
(3) The patterns are developed in an alkaline solution, which dissolves the unexposed part of the $\mathrm { W O } _ { x }$ heat-mode resist. Thus, the stamper with convex $\mathrm { W O } _ { x }$ patterns is obtained (Fig. 9.18c).
--[p210]-- (4) The multilayer optical disc can be fabricated through photo-polymerization (2P) process. For example, UV curable resin on a glass substrate (Fig. 9.18d) replicates the patterns from the PTM stamper by photo-polymerization (2P)
