===== [p21-21] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > (1) Electron-Beam Lithography (3块) =====
--[p21]-- Thus far, the EBL system combined with special e-beam resists materials and process allows the formation of nanostructures with feature sizes of 5 nm [16]. The state-of-the-art electron-beam lithography has been implemented extensively for patterning mesoscopic structures with the unique advantages of increased resolution in feature size, high reliability in processing, increased accuracy in positioning/alignment, and increased flexibility in pattern replication.
--[p21]-- (1) Electron-Beam Lithography
Electron-beam lithography (EBL) emerged in the early 1960s at approximately the same time as optical lithography [14]. EBL uses a Gaussian electron beam or beams with variable shapes for high-resolution or high-throughput demands. The trade-off between resolution and throughput is determined by the sustainable beam current and resist sensitivity.
--[p21]-- Generally speaking, both higher current and enhanced sensitivity lead to a reduction in resolution, thus limiting the throughput at high resolution.
Actually, EBL evolved from scanning electron microscopy (SEM) owing to the discovery of an electron-sensitive polymer material known as polymethylmethacrylate (PMMA) [15]. Because of the fine beam enabled by electron optics, EBL using PMMA resist can achieve a much higher resolution capability than optical lithography.

===== [p35-35] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.4 Remarks and Questions (4块) =====
--[p35]-- 1.4 Remarks and Questions
Among these lithography methods, vacuum-based high-energy beam (such as X-ray and ion/electron beam) lithography technique has been extensively utilized to fabricate small area micro/nanostructures because of their high resolution. However, a high-vacuum environment is required in the process of micro/ nanofabrication. Additionally, this technique also suffers from low speed, high cost, and low throughput.
--[p35]-- Direct laser writing lithography based on light-mode exposure is a good method to fabricate arbitrary structures for applications in microelectronics, integrated optics, diffractive optics, and other fields. Compared to other lithography methods, its operation is facile, and conducted in air. Moreover, its cost is relatively low. Unfortunately, the feature size is dominated by the laser spot size, and it is difficult to surpass the optical diffraction limit determined by 1.22 k/NA.
--[p35]-- Probe-based lithography is able to fabricate arbitrary patterns in atmospheric environment at the nanoscale, but the lithography still operates at low speed and fits only a very small area. Template-based lithography (such as nanoimprint, projection lithography, and self-assembly) is a good method for mass production, but it is not a good choice for complying with individual demand owing to encountered difficulties in fabricating large-area stamper with nanoscale feature size.
--[p35]-- In order to obtain a small feature size, one needs to reduce the laser wavelength of the lithography system. Accordingly, this requires the development of new organic photoresists corresponding to new exposure wavelength, because each photoresist is only sensitive to specific wavelength. This takes a very long research/ development period and a considerable investment. Thus, one needs to develop novel methods based on new physical and chemical models and mechanisms.

===== [p0-0] Laser Heat-Mode Lithography Principle and Methods (Jingsong Wei) (1块) =====
--[p0]-- Jingsong Wei

===== [p177-177] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 8.2 Grayscale Lithography Through Laser-Induced Micro/Nano-Structures (1块) =====
--[p177]-- 8.2 Grayscale Lithography Through Laser-Induced Micro/Nano-Structures

===== [p43-44] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.3.1 Atomic-Scale Line Edge Roughness (5块) =====
--[p43]-- In laser heat-mode lithography, the heat-mode resist is a kind of inorganic nonmetallic thin-film materials, and the basic component unit is atom, and the lithography reaction is conducted among atoms.
The transition region between exposed and unexposed areas is only several atoms, as shown in Fig. 2.8.
--[p44]-- 2.9, where the roughness of lithographic patterns and line edge are reduced to atomic scale. The minimum feature size of pattern structures is only about several nanometers, accordingly.
--[p44]-- Figure 2.8b is the experimental results of high-resolution TEM image, where the interface between exposed and unexposed regions is only in atomic scale.
The lithography is actually dissociation reaction among atoms because the basic component unit is atoms in the inorganic resist materials, the atom network is broken after lithography, and the line edge roughness of the lithography structures is in atomic scale, as shown in Fig.
--[p44]-- That is, the inorganic heat-mode resist shows a high resolution compared to organic resists due to its finer grain size and larger c value (c is a parameter indicating the slope of the remaining resist pattern boundary).
Figure 2.8a is a schematic diagram of transition region, the exposed region is order (crystalline state), and the unexposed region is disorder (amorphous state), and the interface (transition region) is in atomic scale.
--[p43]-- 2.3.1 Atomic-Scale Line Edge Roughness
In light-mode lithography, the resist is organic polymer, and the basic component unit is molecule. The molecule consists of lots of atoms, and the lithography reaction is conducted among molecules. The laser heat-mode lithography is different from the light-mode lithography.

===== [p13-13] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.2.1 Template-Based Lithography (1块) =====
--[p13]-- 1.2.1 Template-Based Lithography
In template-based lithography, the micro/nanostructures are first fabricated on the templates using maskless lithography methods, such as laser and electronic beam writing, and then further transferred to the silicon wafers through projection or nanoimprint lithography [1, 2]. Template-based lithography is suitable for mass production, such as for chips of very large-scale integrated circuits (ICs).

===== [p197-197] Chapter 8 Laser Heat-Mode Grayscale Lithography > 9.2 Pattern Transfer Through ICP/RIE Etching (1块) =====
--[p197]-- 9.2 Pattern Transfer Through ICP/RIE Etching
ICP/RIE etching is one of the popular methods for pattern transfer in traditional lithography due to low cost and high resolution. The patterns are usually transferred to quartz or silicon substrates. The patterns of laser heat-mode lithography can be also transferred the quartz or silicon substrates through ICP/RIE etching methods.

===== [p57-57] Chapter 2 Principles of Laser Heat-Mode Lithography > 2.6 Summary and Remarks (3块) =====
--[p57]-- The phase-change region is further etched off in acid/alkali solutions due to the etching selectivity between laser-irradiated and non-irradiated regions. Laser heat-mode lithography is a photo-physical reaction, and can bypass the optical diffraction limit, and realize high-speed and large-area trans-scale broadband optical lithography. The lithography system is based on far-field focusing optics, and lithography operation is easy and conducted in natural light and atmospheric environments.
--[p57]-- 2.6 Summary and Remarks
Laser heat-mode lithography uses a laser spot to irradiate onto the resist thin films. The light energy is absorbed and transferred into thermal energy. The thermally induced structural transformation takes place when the resist thin film is heated to certain phase-change threshold temperature, such as crystallization point.
--[p57]-- The feature size of patterns can be arbitrarily tuned from nanoscale to microscale, and the resolution and line edge roughness can be reduced to atomic scale. The laser heat-mode lithography is an overthrow to the traditional light-mode lithography, and useful in lots of fields, such as the fabrication of chips and micro/nano-optical elements.

===== [p28-29] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.2.4 Comparison Among Lithography Techniques (5块) =====
--[p28]-- DLW has the merits of operation in air and large-area fabrication with high throughput. However, it is difficult to overcome the diffraction limit and to fabricate trans-scale structures spanning the micrometer to nanometer.
Lithography is generally conducted on the light-mode organic resist thin film, and the procedure is complex owing to the inclusion of soft baking, postexposure baking, and hard baking.

--[p28 [表]]-- Moreover, the lithography resolution is limited by organic molecular structures where light-mode organic resist contains large molecular groups. Thus, in some cases, the resolution and line edge roughness of patterns is poor for large-molecule light-mode resists.

--[p28]-- 1.2.4 Comparison Among Lithography Techniques
Table 1.1 lists the comparison of various lithography techniques, indicating that mask lithography has the merits of high efficiency, mass production, and high throughput. However, it is not suitable for individual fabrication and low-volume manufacturing. LIL can conduct maskless fabrication, and its throughput is high.
--[p28]-- Unfortunately, one can only fabricate periodic dots or line structures by LIL, and it is difficult to pattern arbitrary, nonperiodic structures. In addition, SPL generally has scanning speed ranging from submicrometer to micrometer per second, which is several orders of magnitude lower than the conventional laser beam lithography. The working area of SPL is also small, making it suitable only for patterning small area of the target surface.

===== [p138-139] Chapter 6 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography Using Organic Thin Films > 6.2.6.1 Convex-Type Pattern Formation Through Thermal Deformation (4块) =====
--[p138]-- 6.2.6.1 Convex-Type Pattern Formation Through Thermal Deformation
Figure 6.7 gives the typical convex-type patterns. The dots with a size (at FWHM) of $4 7 \pm 5$ nm are presented in Fig. 6.7a, where the writing laser power and pulse width are about $P = 2 . 1 0 \ : \mathrm { m W }$ and $t _ { p } = 1 1$ ns, respectively. The writing laser energy $\left( E = P \times t _ { p } \right)$ is $\sim 2 3 . 0 \ \mathrm { p J }$ , accordingly. The inset is the three-dimensional (3D) images.
--[p139]-- The feature size decreases with the laser energy decreasing, which indicates that the pattern feature size can be reduced through decreasing the laser energy. However, the feature size cannot be infinitely reduced due to low pattern contrast. In addition, Fig. 6.7 also indicates that the writing process is stable and the patterns are homogeneous, and the smallest feature size is 31 nm, which is far smaller than the writing spot, and only 1/20 of the writing spot size.
--[p139]-- The line-shaped patterns are also directly written on the $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ thin films, as shown in Fig. 6.7c, where the laser energy is set at $\sim 2 4 ~ \mathrm { p J }$ . The inset is the 3D image, accordingly. The linewidth (feature size) is $7 1 \pm 7 ~ \mathrm { n m }$ , which is only about 1/8 the writing spot size. Figure 6.7d gives the dependence of the feature size on writing laser energy.
--[p138]-- One can see that the dot-shaped patterns are uniform and the size of $4 7 \pm 5$ nm is only 1/12 that of the writing spot. Further optimizing the experimental parameters, such as writing power and pulse width, the dot-shaped patterns with a feature size of $3 1 \pm 3$ nm are obtained, as shown in Fig. 6.7b, where the dots are obtained using $P \sim 3 . 2 5$ mW and $t _ { p } = 7$ ns. The writing laser energy is $\sim 2 2 . 5$ $\mathbf { p J } .$ The inset is the 3D image, accordingly.

===== [p23-23] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.2.3.1 Direct Laser Writing Lithography (1块) =====
--[p23]-- 1.2.3.1 Direct Laser Writing Lithography
Direct laser writing (DLW) is popular in the fabrication of micro/nanostructures owing to the high writing speed, large patterning area, and easy operation. The DLW technique can be classified as laser interference lithography, digital micromirror device (DMD)-based laser writing, zone-plate-array lithography, and laser writing through a focused beam spot.

===== [p1-1] Springer Series in Materials Science (1块) =====
--[p1]-- Springer Series in Materials Science
Volume 291

===== [p112-112] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.6 Summary (1块) =====
--[p112]-- 4.6 Summary
To reduce the lithographic feature size, one can prevent in-plane thermal diffusion and promote out-of-plane thermal diffusion through decreasing the thickness of the resist thin films, inserting the thermal conduction layers, and shortening the laser irradiation time. These cause the size of the structural change threshold region to become much smaller than the focused laser spot itself; thus, nanoscale feature size can be obtained.

===== [p16-16] Chapter 1 Current Status of Lithography > 1.2.1.2 Nanoimprint Lithography (1块) =====
--[p16]-- 1.2.1.2 Nanoimprint Lithography
There are different nanoimprint lithography methods. Four types of mainstream operation modes are introduced, including the thermal press mode, room temperature mode, UV-cured mode, and the reverse mode.

===== [p132-132] Chapter 6 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography Using Organic Thin Films > 6.2 Patterning Through Combination of Thermal Deformation with Thermal Gasification (2块) =====
--[p132]-- 6.2 Patterning Through Combination of Thermal Deformation with Thermal Gasification
Among metal–hydrazone complexes, the metal(II)–hydrazone complexes have good solubility, film-forming performance, sharp thermal decomposition threshold effect, and strong nonlinear response to visible wavelength laser. The thermal diffusion coefficient is also low compared with the chalcogenide heat-mode lithography materials.
--[p132]-- Copper–hydrazone complex (abbreviated as $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 } )$ materials are low-molecular-weight compounds (not polymers). Such low-molecular-weight compounds have sharp gasification point instead of glass transition temperature, thus $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ is a kind of good laser heat-mode resist materials.

===== [p106-106] Chapter 3 High-Speed Rotation-Type Laser Heat-Mode Lithography System > 4.5 Manipulation Through Writing Strategy (1块) =====
--[p106]-- 4.5 Manipulation Through Writing Strategy
Laser irradiation time is one of the important factors influencing thermal diffusion. Generally, the longer irradiation time can lead to a larger thermal diffusion distance, owing to thermal diffusion distance of ${ \sqrt { D t } } .$ . Thus, the pattern feature size will become small if the laser irradiation time is short [13]. Here, the high-speed writing and short laser pulse are adopted to obtain short laser irradiation time.

===== [p138-138] Chapter 6 Laser Heat-Mode Lithography Using Organic Thin Films > 6.2.6 Patterning Structures (1块) =====
--[p138]-- 6.2.6 Patterning Structures
The patterning can be conducted through a writing spot size with a size of about 600 nm. The sample structure is designed as “glass substrate/metal $\mathrm { l a y e r / C u L } _ { 2 }$ layer”, where the $\mathbf { A } \mathbf { g }$ is used as metal layer and functions as thermal sink. The $\mathbf { A } \mathbf { g }$ thin film is deposited on the glass substrate through magnetron-controlled sputtering method.
The $\mathrm { C u L } _ { 2 }$ thin films are prepared on the Ag layer by spin-coating method.
